**Beta.141 adds a bounded recovery reserve instead of reversing the retention policy.** At most the newest 16 otherwise-removable transient process results completed within thirty minutes receive temporary high eviction priority. An incoming transient consumes one of those slots before capacity is reclaimed, so sustained helper churn cannot grow the protected set beyond 16. Older/excess transient results remain first eviction candidates; when fewer than 16 recent transient results exist, the oldest ordinary non-protected durable terminal may be reclaimed to preserve immediate helper recovery; recent transient results are still removable as a final capacity fallback. Active, staged, unreadable, retired-integrity, and dependency-protected state keep their existing fail-closed semantics, and total retained-state capacity stays fixed at 512. No result contents or identities are newly projected.

**Merged-main Windows CI independently exposed a test deadline that contradicted the production retry schedule.** The failing log recorded runner-exit reconciliation `error_class=permission_denied`, `recovery_attempt=1`, and `retry_scheduled=true`, then the integration fixture failed its autonomous-relaunch assertion. Production waits 10.1 seconds before attempt one and another 10.1 seconds before each bounded retry; the old 20-second fixture deadline expired before the earliest second attempt at 20.2 seconds. The fixture now uses forty seconds, enough for all three scheduled attempts plus ordinary runner/filesystem settlement margin. The deterministic unit test with 1ms delays still proves that transient Windows reconciliation is retried while non-transient failure is not.

**Residual host limitation remains unchanged.** Durable managed jobs can continue across MCP disconnects and service replacement, and beta.141 makes recent one-step terminal evidence substantially harder to evict before recovery. Machine Bridge still reports that host-turn deadline/final-receipt state is not observable locally; no retention change can proactively re-enter a ChatGPT conversation after the external host has ended that turn. The operational mitigation remains lower host-visible call density plus larger coherent durable pipelines, not fabricated host acknowledgements.

## 2026-08-26 beta.138 Windows hard-timeout tree-settlement follow-up

**Beta.137 was rejected because exact-head Windows CI proved that the publication hard-timeout contract still returned before whole-tree settlement.** The beta.137 provider run failed `publish-npm:test` at the unchanged resistant-descendant assertion: after the publication helper had reported its hard timeout, a descendant was still able to execute its delayed marker write. This is stronger evidence than beta.136's earlier passing run because the invariant is safety-critical and one observed post-settlement descendant effect is sufficient to falsify the implementation. The test timing is therefore unchanged; extending the marker delay would only hide the contract violation.

**The defect was in the shared `runExecutable(hardTimeout)` settlement boundary.** Beta.136 replaced synchronous publication execution with the repository process-tree helper, but its hard-timeout callback called `terminateProcessTree(child, "SIGKILL")` and then allowed the normal direct-child `close` handler to settle the executable. On Windows, `terminateProcessTree` intentionally returns true as soon as `taskkill.exe /T /F` is successfully spawned; historical repository contracts explicitly treat that as an asynchronous termination request, not proof that the target tree has exited. The npm leader could therefore close before `taskkill` had finished walking and terminating its descendants, allowing `runExecutable` and `publish-npm` to return while a descendant still had CPU time.

**Beta.138 adds an explicit OS tree-settlement barrier instead of changing the existing request-oriented API.** The widely used synchronous `terminateProcessTree()` keeps its historical semantics. A new `terminateProcessTreeAndWait()` is used only by callers that require hard settlement proof. POSIX preserves the isolated-process-group `SIGKILL` request, whose successful group signal dispatch synchronously prevents surviving members from continuing ordinary execution. Windows launches `taskkill.exe /PID <pid> /T /F` and waits for the helper's own terminal `close(0)` inside a bounded five-second settlement window. Spawn failure, helper error/nonzero exit, or settlement timeout remains unconfirmed termination; the fallback direct kill is best effort only and does not manufacture whole-tree proof.

**`runExecutable` now has a two-part hard-timeout terminal condition.** A timeout result cannot settle until the direct child has closed **and** the hard tree barrier has resolved. The result carries explicit `termination_settled` evidence; `publish-npm` maps an ordinary confirmed deadline to `ETIMEDOUT` but maps an unconfirmed tree barrier to `EUNSETTLED`. Deterministic runtime tests hold the tree barrier pending after synthetic direct-child close and require the executable promise to remain unsettled, then separately hold a Windows taskkill helper open and require the barrier to remain unsettled until `close(0)`. The real publication descendant regression remains the end-to-end proof on each hosted platform.

## 2026-08-26 beta.137 exact-head Windows Wrangler settlement follow-up

**Beta.136 was rejected after provider verification exposed an independent packaged-script defect.** The exact accepted beta.136 bytes had already passed two local 131-task full plans, final supply-chain checks, isolated install, guarded owner-machine activation, activated-package OAuth canary, live runtime diagnostics, and acceptance. PR #97 then ran the exact pushed head on Windows. `publish-npm:test` completed successfully there, directly validating the new asynchronous publication/process-tree timeout boundary. The same platform job failed later in `worker-types-generator:test` at `wrangler-command-lifecycle.mjs` with `wrangler types could not force cleanup`. Because the failing lifecycle wrapper ships under `scripts/`, repairing it changes npm package bytes; beta.136 acceptance is therefore removed instead of being grandfathered into a different artifact.

**The causal defect was request/settlement conflation, not demonstrated failure to terminate Wrangler.** After Wrangler emitted its completion marker, the wrapper allowed a bounded natural-exit grace, requested `SIGTERM`, and after another grace attempted `SIGKILL`. It then treated `child.kill("SIGKILL") !== true` as immediate proof that force cleanup had failed. That inference is invalid across the repository's own process model: a kill API return value describes a request boundary, while process ownership is released only after terminal settlement. On Windows in particular, process shutdown and libuv `close` delivery can race a later kill request; a false return can occur while the child is already exiting. The wrapper already had historical evidence that a natural zero exit must win over a raced cleanup request, but the newer force-kill branch had reintroduced the same category error one stage later.

**Beta.137 makes the Wrangler lifecycle close-settled.** Graceful cleanup and force cleanup are requests only. A force request no longer fails merely because the injected/default kill call returns false; after escalation, a distinct bounded settlement timer waits for the child `close` event. Real `code=0` still wins over cleanup-request flags, a completion-marked child closed by `SIGTERM` remains the accepted bounded-hang path, a child that closes only after the force request remains a verification failure, and a child that never closes reaches the separate `could not force cleanup` deadline. The hard command deadline also forces escalation when cleanup was already in progress instead of becoming a no-op behind the `cleanupRequested` guard.

**The regression models the provider race directly without requiring a Windows host.** A synthetic Wrangler command emits its completion marker and schedules a later zero exit. The test shortens the completion and termination graces, injects a successful `SIGTERM` request followed by a `SIGKILL` request that returns false without mutating the child, and requires the wrapper to wait for the eventual zero `close`. The old beta.136 logic necessarily failed at the false return; the repaired logic records both cleanup requests and succeeds only from the later process settlement. The existing completed-but-resident, pre-completion timeout, nonzero-exit, and natural cleanup-race cases remain intact.

## 2026-08-26 beta.136 npm publication timeout and relay interruption review

**The beta.135 npm publication attempt failed before upload even though its descendants kept printing.** The canonical publisher launched `prepublishOnly` through synchronous `spawnSync` with a ten-minute timeout shared by all npm stages. The full verification plan completed in 681.4 seconds, so the direct npm child crossed that 600-second deadline and `publish-npm.mjs` emitted `npm.publish.failed` with `ETIMEDOUT`. npm lifecycle descendants were outside the direct-child kill boundary and continued the tail of `check`, then `version:check` and `release:check`, which made the terminal look as if publication continued after failure. A direct registry lookup after settlement returned `E404` for `machine-bridge-mcp@3.0.0-beta.135`; the dry-run and immutable upload stages were never reached. The failure is therefore a local process-tree/deadline bug, not an ambiguous registry write.

**The repair separates verification budget from upload budget and makes timeout settlement tree-complete.** Publication stages now use the repository's isolated asynchronous process execution boundary. `prepublishOnly` receives a thirty-minute deadline consistent with the documented full-plan envelope, while npm dry-run and upload remain bounded to ten minutes. Publication opts into hard timeout termination of the complete isolated process tree instead of waiting for a direct child only. A behavior regression launches a lifecycle descendant that ignores `SIGTERM` and attempts a delayed marker write; the test requires explicit `ETIMEDOUT`/hard-tree settlement and proves the descendant cannot execute after the publication helper returns. Registry reconciliation and the explicit owner-confirmation boundary remain unchanged.

**The reported beta.135 relay interruption is a separate pre-existing network-path event, and the fallback substantially preserved service.** Runtime evidence records WebSocket close `1006`/`connection_interrupted` at `2026-08-26T02:21:34Z` on an operating-system VPN/TUN route, no correlated system sleep, no event-loop stall, and Green local resource pressure. HTTPS fallback became verified-ready at about `02:21:35.399Z`; WSS did not regain verified readiness until about `02:21:47.319Z`. Historical logs contain the same `1006`/connection-reset class before beta.135, and beta.135 changed Worker relay source only by its version string. The evidence therefore does not support changing retry/replay semantics or assigning fault to a particular VPN, network provider, or Cloudflare edge. The actual defect was observability: WSS-local outage warnings said the whole "remote relay" was unavailable even while HTTPS was ready. Beta.136 names WebSocket degradation explicitly and retains `https_fallback_last_takeover_ms` after primary recovery so later diagnosis can distinguish WSS outage duration from the much smaller whole-bridge continuity gap.

## 2026-08-25 beta.133 durable interruption evidence review

**Beta.132 fixed planned-restart settlement but its first live canary exposed a second-order diagnostic defect.** A ninety-second detached managed job remained healthy while an independent delayed `service restart` terminated the beta.132 daemon. The in-flight hosted `read_job` returned exactly the intended structured result: retryable `unavailable`, `reason=daemon_planned_drain`, `side_effects_started=false`, `recovery.mode=read_same_job`, and the original job ID. Reading that same ID after reconnect reached the unique terminal result without replaying the business step. A subsequent full `server_info`, however, landed on a fresh Worker isolate whose isolate-local `observability.continuity` counters had reset to zero. The causal event was therefore visible at settlement time but not reliably available for later diagnosis.

**The final repair persists only bounded causal metadata, not a tool transcript.** Durable Object storage now keeps one normalized continuity record containing planned-drain count/affected-call count and timestamp, socket-disconnect count plus the latest planned/unplanned close/error classification with bounded close code/clean flag, and latest request-abort / stream-cancel-control timestamps. It contains no account/client identity, daemon/call ID, tool name, arguments, results, host conversation identifier, URL/endpoint, network address, close reason, or error text. Malformed stored state normalizes to bounded defaults, and diagnostic persistence failure never blocks planned-drain acknowledgement or socket cleanup.

**Residual boundary remains explicit.** This evidence can distinguish an observed planned restart, Worker-visible socket loss, or Worker-visible client cancellation from a local managed-job execution failure even after isolate replacement. It still cannot observe a ChatGPT host turn that dies outside the Worker, cannot prove that the final assistant message rendered, and cannot identify the failing VPN/TUN provider from a socket interruption alone. Long-task policy is unchanged: durable work and six-hour managed-job steps remain available, and no task-duration reduction or user-driven polling is introduced.

## 2026-08-25 beta.132 end-to-end continuity re-review

**The previous diagnosis was directionally correct but too broad.** The relay already has a two-minute same-instance reconnect grace, verified WebSocket replacement, signed HTTPS fallback, `resume_calls` ownership reconciliation, bounded same-ID redelivery after proven non-delivery, and daemon-result retention until Worker acknowledgement. Rebuilding those mechanisms, lengthening their timers, or persisting one daemon instance identifier across process generations would not be a justified continuity repair. In particular, sharing one instance identity between concurrently old/new daemon processes without stronger execution fencing could make the replacement misclassify a still-executing old-process call as missing and duplicate a side effect. HTTPS also shares the operating-system network path with WSS, so it is transport diversity rather than a cure for VPN/TUN failure.

**Two interruption classes are now causally separated.** A recent live episode recorded relay WebSocket close 1006/`connection_interrupted` at `2026-08-25T11:02:27.296Z`; an independent GitHub HTTPS watcher failed with `unexpected EOF` at the same point, and diagnostics reported a tunnel/VPN system route with no event-loop stall and Green resource pressure. That is strong evidence of a shared system-network interruption, not proof of a daemon CPU/resource failure. Separately, release activation intentionally replaces the daemon process. Each process deliberately has a fresh random instance identity, while current Worker call rebinding is same-instance only; before beta.132 the old runtime closed its relay before cancelling active calls, so an intentional upgrade was communicated upward through the same transport-loss symptom as an accidental outage.

**Causal repair.** A ready runtime now performs a bounded planned-drain handshake before closing its relay. `daemon_draining` asks the Worker to settle calls still owned by that exact channel using their existing recovery descriptors, and `daemon_draining_ack` confirms that settlement before local shutdown proceeds or the short control-plane acknowledgement window expires. `read_job` now has an explicit read-only recovery descriptor: a planned restart returns retryable `unavailable`, `reason=daemon_planned_drain`, `side_effects_started=false`, and the original `job_id`; the caller recovers by reading that same job after reconnect. Mutating calls retain fail-closed ambiguous-settlement semantics unless they already have an idempotency recovery credential. This improves upgrade continuity without making a new daemon inherit the old process's execution identity and without changing any managed-job execution/admission ceiling.

**Recovery visibility and residual host boundary.** The 512-state managed-job retention policy already evicted transient one-step process history before ordinary durable terminal history, so increasing an eviction weight would have been a no-op and was rejected during review. The actual visibility defect was the 50-record listing order: recent transient terminals could still hide older durable terminal results. `list_jobs` now orders durable terminal managed jobs before transient helper terminals and owner/local capacity diagnostics distinguish `durable_terminal` from `transient_terminal`. These are recovery aids, not terminal-consumption claims. Machine Bridge still reports `host_turn_deadline_observable=false` and `host_terminal_receipt_observable=false`; no MCP-side change can prove or revive an external ChatGPT assistant turn after that host has terminated it. Long-task correctness therefore remains anchored in durable jobs and recoverable identifiers rather than shorter user tasks or invented host acknowledgements.

## 2026-08-24 beta.120 repeated host-interruption review

**Beta.119 did not eliminate the user-visible interruption class.** The strongest available local evidence does not show a daemon crash or Worker execution timeout. Around the reported interruption, the live runtime remained callable and its content-free hash-chained audit retained the host-facing call sequence. Freezing that audit at 2026-08-24T13:58:58Z shows 152 tool calls in the preceding fifteen minutes, with a peak of 17 calls in one minute: 62 were `read_job`, 51 were `exec_command`, and another three were one-step `run_process` helpers. Only three calls in that window had non-success outcomes. Machine Bridge still cannot observe the private ChatGPT condition that ended/faulted final message delivery, so this evidence does not prove a specific host timeout; it does materially strengthen high host-visible tool-event density as the repeated amplification mechanism.

Those audit counts cover only relay tool calls that reached the daemon. ChatGPT-side schema discovery, workspace/control-plane operations, connector routing before daemon dispatch, and final-message delivery are outside the audit boundary, so 152 is not a claim about the total number of host events. A later severe interruption recurred before beta.120 was activated: live `server_info` still reported beta.119/schema generation 8. The immediate recovery inventory was 512/512 retained managed-job states with the visible 50-record window dominated by recent one-step helpers, while one older job had remained in pre-spawn `resource_admission` for more than nine minutes. The daemon remained callable, no relay outage was active, sampled resource pressure was Green, and the event-loop stall counter remained zero. Neither capacity saturation nor that waiter is therefore promoted to the hidden host termination cause; both are concrete amplification/diagnostic signals. Generic remote reads of Machine Bridge control-plane files are correctly denied, so incident tooling must project the running runtime's own audit and waiter state rather than infer a profile path after the fact.

**A second local amplifier was independently observable.** One interactive unbounded `pytest` request had remained before child spawn for roughly eighteen minutes. Its bounded request asked for six CPU units/3 GiB and its evaluated effective CPU reservation was seven cores; at the sampled instant roughly 4.44 cores were already busy, so admission returned `cpu_pressure_window`. The waiter was protected and still inside the existing thirty-minute durable admission bound. This was not a local deadlock and not an execution timeout. The prior diagnostics exposed only waiter count/age and a separately sampled overall pressure state, which made that causal distinction unnecessarily difficult.

**A later event-loop pause has a different proven cause and must not be conflated with the host interruption.** The runtime later reported one maximum pause of about 947.954 seconds ending at 2026-08-24T17:04:10.810Z. A fixed macOS `pmset` review shows a 954-second `Sleep Service Back to Sleep` interval ending at 2026-08-24T17:04:11Z, after an earlier idle-sleep assertion process had ended immediately before the machine began its sleep cycle. Duration and resume time match within seconds, so this pause is high-confidence operating-system suspension, not evidence that the Node.js event loop synchronously blocked for sixteen minutes. The sleep episode occurred more than two hours after the severe host interruption described above; the immediate snapshot for that interruption had zero event-loop stalls and no active relay outage. The two failure classes therefore remain separate.

**Beta.120 converts all three findings into durable evidence and reduces the common call multiplier.** Owner/local diagnostics aggregate recent audit call density/tool frequencies without logging arguments/results, expose bounded waiter request shape plus current admission reason without waiter identity/token/PID/path/contention key, keep older unreadable/active/staged jobs ahead of terminal history in the 50-record inventory, and on macOS project only bounded sleep intervals plus runtime-stall correlation. The remote idle-sleep guard now records coarse activity/grace/release timestamps and release reason so a future suspension can be compared with Machine Bridge assertion ownership without publishing command content or host conversation identity. Remote one-step process tools still persist a durable job first but then use a two-second initial settlement window; a short helper can return terminal state in the original response instead of forcing the common second `read_job` event. Long helpers and six-hour managed-job steps retain their existing execution/recovery paths. The change therefore attacks event amplification and improves causal diagnosis without trading away long-task lifetime or blindly extending the thirty-minute idle-sleep grace.

**Beta.118 passed the owner-machine frozen suite and live candidate checks but failed two independent required PR gates, so it is not eligible for source or npm publication.** GitHub CodeQL reported one `js/bad-code-sanitization` finding in `tests/managed-jobs-test.mjs`: a test-only marker path was serialized into a `node -e` source string. The production dependency implementation was not implicated, but the repository's zero-unaccepted-SARIF policy correctly blocks release on test code as well. The repair uses constant JavaScript source and supplies marker paths through child argv. It does not add a CodeQL suppression, baseline exception, or sanitizer claim.

**Windows exposed a test-oracle defect rather than evidence of the old blind artifact polling architecture.** The failing assertion measured local wall-clock time from one read until dependent settlement and required it to be below ten seconds. Under the Windows hosted runner's full platform workload, scheduling/resource contention can exceed that number even though dependency state is durable and state-driven. The revised test removes that speed assertion while retaining stronger semantic evidence: the dependent reaches `dependency_wait`, does not materialize private runtime files while blocked, terminal upstream failure yields structured `dependency_failed`, no downstream main child writes its marker, and the declared finally step receives its just-in-time private input and succeeds. Those observations test the causal property directly instead of inferring architecture from machine speed.

Beta.119 must repeat the frozen suite, exact candidate/install-only verification, persistent Worker/login-daemon activation, activated-package OAuth canary, live runtime observation, candidate acceptance, guarded branch update, and required PR checks. The authorization model itself is unchanged: npm publication remains the sole separate conversational approval boundary.

## 2026-08-24 beta.118 release-authorization model review

**The owner changed the release authorization model after beta.117 acceptance.** The new rule is intentionally simple: npm package publication is the only task step that requires a separate current-conversation authorization. Candidate activation, same-name Worker deployment/replacement, login-daemon/service replacement, local/global installation required by the release flow, ChatGPT Action control refresh/review when it is genuine product-governance evidence, guarded GitHub push/PR/merge, tags, and GitHub Releases/Prereleases are standing-authorized when they are relevant to the current task and allowed by the effective Machine Bridge/account policy. Automation must therefore continue through those stages instead of returning merely to request another approval.

**The implementation moves the explicit confirmation to the actual remaining authorization boundary rather than deleting safety checks.** GitHub source publication no longer calls an owner-confirmation helper and no longer accepts `--owner-confirm` as part of its supported command contract; it still resolves trusted local Git/GitHub executables, requires the exact accepted candidate and exact main/remote state, holds one common-Git-dir publication lock, reconciles ambiguous remote state, and verifies the uploaded release asset digest. Conversely, the canonical npm publication entrypoint now requires `--owner-confirm` before it calls the publication implementation. The prerelease and stable documented commands are therefore `npm run prerelease:publish -- --owner-confirm` and `npm run stable:publish -- --owner-confirm`. Programmatic publication tests continue to inject the publication implementation directly so they can validate registry reconciliation without fabricating conversational authority.

**Standing authority is not a bypass for unrelated destructive work or ambiguous settlements.** Live activation still uses the detached managed-job carrier because replacing the daemon would terminate daemon-owned foreground/process-session carriers. Worker/service recovery still requires exact identity evidence, browser/ChatGPT UI mutations still require post-state inspection before retry after an unknown settlement, credential/security mutations still require positive causal evidence, and GitHub publication still fails closed on dirty source, mismatched acceptance/digest, incomplete checks, or conflicting remote state. The change removes conversational stop points; it does not weaken integrity, replay-safety, or failure-atomicity boundaries.

**These are new packaged bytes and invalidate beta.117 release evidence for the revised tree.** Beta.117 remains valid evidence for its exact accepted tarball and was successfully activated before this policy change, but the shipped release scripts and documentation now differ. The superseding tree therefore advances to beta.118, retains hosted tool schema generation 8, and requires fresh frozen verification, exact candidate preparation/install-only verification, automatic guarded activation, packaged OAuth canary, live observation, and a new acceptance record before guarded source publication. npm publication remains stopped unless the owner separately authorizes that specific action.

## 2026-08-24 beta.117 independent post-activation review

**This review deliberately treated beta.116 activation and its green acceptance as a baseline, not as proof.** Architecture/module boundaries, managed-job branch behavior, dependency admission/wait/failure/recovery, retention/destructive decisions, private resource lifetime, terminal persistence, public projections, low-cardinality logging, current-tree privacy, tests, and current documentation were reread independently. The six-hour single-step ceiling and same-response hosted continuation rule remain unchanged; none of the corrections introduces an elapsed-time handoff or asks the user to drive progress by sending another message.

**A later delivery failure exposed a separate host-event-density amplifier that the first beta.117 pass had not covered.** The external chat surface reported a send/response timeout even though the local review continued, a later full verification completed successfully, and the relay recovered from its brief connection reset well before that verification finished. Machine Bridge still cannot observe the host's terminal-receipt decision, so the exact host-side cutoff remains unknown. What is observable is that the review produced dozens of one-step durable helper jobs, while an active multi-step job could also wake `read_job` every time `current_step` changed. The correction therefore targets event density rather than wall-clock duration: repository/host guidance now batches coherent non-interactive commands into umbrella commands or multi-step managed jobs, and hosted `read_job` coalesces nonterminal progress for at least 30 seconds while excluding `current_step` alone from the wake signature. Terminal settlement remains prompt on the next five-second internal poll, the ordinary no-change wait remains 40 seconds, and managed execution retains its six-hour per-step ceiling. This is not evidence for a hidden fixed ChatGPT timeout; it is a bounded mitigation for a reproduced amplification mechanism below an external, still-unobservable delivery boundary.

**The most important new privacy finding was pre-execution resource residency.** A dependent runner entered `dependency_wait` only after materializing registered-resource copies and job-scoped temporary files. No business child had started, but sensitive execution inputs could remain duplicated in the private runtime directory for the entire dependency wait. Beta.117 moves that materialization behind the durable dependency gate. If the dependency fails before execution, inputs are materialized only if declared `finally_steps` actually need a cleanup phase; if main/recovery resource reconstruction itself already failed, the runner does not retry the same failure and misclassify it as a second cleanup error. Integration coverage verifies both absence during the wait and just-in-time cleanup input availability.

**Two integrity gaps were also closed.** Dependency retention previously failed closed for unreadable JSON but trusted readable `plan.json` without verifying it against the persisted `plan_sha256`; a syntactically valid corruption that removed `depends_on` could therefore erase the very protection used by destructive pruning. Retention now verifies the canonical plan hash before deriving protected upstream IDs and refuses pruning when the protection state is missing, malformed, or hash-mismatched. Separately, plan hashing and managed-job ID validation had accumulated parallel implementations across admission, runners, recovery and retention. The identity regex now has one directory-domain source, plan hashing/integrity has one shared module, and architecture tests pin those imports.

**Dependency terminal observation now covers the result-first/status-second crash window.** Terminal persistence intentionally writes `result.json` before replacing the active status. A dependent that read only `status.json` could miss already-durable success/failure until some other manager operation reconciled the upstream record. Dependency polling now accepts a valid terminal result as a read-only terminal projection without rewriting upstream state. Public dependency counts are bounded, terminal projections clear the stale pending count, and dependency error labels are normalized before publication rather than trusting arbitrary persisted strings.

**A separate proactive review found that daemon-lifetime runner recovery was not autonomous enough, and that simply making it autonomous would have been unsafe.** A downstream dependency reader reconciles only the downstream job. If an upstream runner died while the daemon itself stayed alive, no component necessarily touched that upstream record, so the dependent could continue seeing an old active status until another read/list/restart happened. Adding a timer alone would have been wrong because managed-job business children are detached process groups: killing the runner does not prove its child stopped, and cleanup/recovery could otherwise race a still-running old side effect. Beta.117 therefore couples the fix to persisted child ownership. The daemon observes exits only for runners it launched, schedules one reconciliation after the established crash-settlement window rather than scanning the job store periodically, and the runner persists an owner-only active-child claim containing PID, process start identity and process-group semantics immediately after spawn. A recovery runner must verify and terminate that exact old process tree before it releases the recovery lock, marks itself terminal-authoritative, or executes cleanup. PID reuse or otherwise unverifiable ownership fails closed; terminal maintenance also refuses to erase unresolved child evidence. A real fault-injection test kills an upstream runner while its detached child is still scheduled to write later, never reads the upstream during recovery, and proves the old child is gone and the dependent business child never runs.

**Publication privacy and documentation were not actually clean despite the previous privacy suite passing.** The beta.116 changelog/audit text contained an external project codename, a local task label, and unrelated implementation-function names. Those details are unnecessary for a reusable package and violated the project's own synthetic/publication-data rule even though the machine-private denylist had not yet learned them. The published text is now generic, while the local ignored denylist contains the private vocabulary so future current-tree/history checks can catch a recurrence. Current `CLIENTS.md` also carried beta.114/beta.115 limits as present-tense guidance (`50` retained states and generation-6 hosted submission); it now states the 512-state store, 50-record inventory window, and current generation-8 contract. Historical version-specific audit/changelog claims remain intact where they describe the old release rather than current behavior.

**The history check also changed the local branch shape, not just the current files.** The private vocabulary existed only in one unpushed local tip; no remote ref contained that commit and its parent was clean. The review therefore removed that local tip from reachable branch history while preserving all file changes in the working tree for the superseding beta.117 review. `privacy:history` then passed over the reachable blob/message set. No remote/shared history was rewritten, and no old private term is being hidden by weakening the denylist or excluding Git history from the gate.

## 2026-08-24 beta.116 long-workflow interruption review

**The incident was not a short host-call timeout and the repair does not shorten the task.** Live Machine Bridge state during the failed external workflow showed a ready daemon with zero relay outages, no event-loop stall, Green resource pressure, and durable jobs that continued for more than twenty minutes after the ChatGPT response stopped. The earliest deterministic local failure was an implementation precondition failure that exited before writing its intended change. Subsequent scope verification observed no expected file differences, qualification commands failed to materialize their expected test-report artifacts, and already-submitted descendants spent roughly 15–22 minutes waiting for prerequisite files that could never appear. The host's exact final-response termination trigger remains unobservable from Machine Bridge and is not guessed; `host_turn_deadline_observable=false` and `host_terminal_receipt_observable=false` remain the honest boundary.

**The local orchestration defect was dependency representation, not long execution itself.** The previous workflow encoded cross-job causality as shell/Python loops over filesystem artifacts. Once an upstream job had failed terminally, the downstream runner had no durable knowledge of that terminal state and could only keep sleeping until its own arbitrary guard expired. Generation 7 adds optional `depends_on` to `stage_job`/`start_job`. Acceptance binds each dependency to its durable job identity and same authority; staged or already-failed dependencies are rejected before the dependent is accepted. An accepted dependent remains `queued/dependency_wait` without spawning its main child or entering process resource admission. All-success releases normal execution; later upstream failure settles the dependent with `dependency_failed` and bounded dependency evidence. Progress in `dependency_pending_count` is part of hosted read-job change detection, so one upstream settlement wakes the existing server-side long-poll without adding a host-side spin loop.

**Crash recovery preserves the pre-side-effect distinction.** Ordinary dead runners that may have executed business steps retain the established interrupted/cleanup-only recovery model. A dead runner whose persisted state is still `queued/dependency_wait` is different: no dependent main child has run. Reconciliation therefore verifies the plan and relaunches the same pre-execution job rather than manufacturing an interrupted business execution. The integration test force-kills that runner, waits through the existing stale-runner observation window, and requires the original job ID to resume dependency waiting and then execute successfully with `recovery_attempts=1`; no resubmission and no user input are involved.

**Frozen full verification exposed one independent runner-publication race and the fix remains fail-closed.** The parent publishes `runner.pid` in two atomic generations so an incomplete provisional claim never becomes execution authority. During the local self-test, a manager read opened one generation while the path was atomically replaced by the next and the secure-file identity check correctly raised `MBM_IDENTITY_CHANGED`; the claim adapter incorrectly treated that expected publication race as a permanently invalid runner. The fix is deliberately scoped to `readManagedJobRunnerClaim`: it retries only `MBM_IDENTITY_CHANGED` for four attempts with 1 ms pauses, and every attempt repeats the full regular-file, no-follow, single-link, path-identity and JSON-shape validation. Generic secure-file and destructive snapshot readers were not relaxed. A deterministic regression injects the identity transition, while the original local self-test now completes its resource-CLI managed-job phase.

**Long workflows also exposed a recovery-depth mismatch in the previous 50-state store.** The reproduced workflow generated about fifty durable records within one hour, so an explicit long-running job could approach eviction simply from diagnostic/helper churn. Beta.116 raises the retained-state bound to 512 while preserving the public `list_jobs` response maximum of 50. Transient one-step carriers remain first in the terminal eviction order. Active or staged dependency plans additionally pin referenced upstream records against age/capacity retirement; an unreadable active dependency plan disables capacity eviction rather than allowing a fail-open guess. Terminal retention remains seven days and staged plans remain 24 hours, so the larger count bound increases recovery depth without creating indefinite retention.

**The anti-regression requirement is explicitly long-task preserving.** Hosted `read_job` remains a 40-second default server-side long-poll with bounded same-response follow-up, not a one-read-per-turn handoff; a client that can carry it may still request the documented longer wait. Managed-job main/finally steps remain capable of six-hour execution. No few-minute aggregate conversation limit, elapsed-time handoff trigger, or requirement for `continue`/`继续` was added. The internal dependency orphan guard is measured in days and is not a host task-duration budget. Managed-job regression coverage now fills the 512-state capacity, verifies dependency pinning, proves no dependent child spawns early, proves failure propagation instead of long artifact waiting, and proves dependency-wait runner recovery. Architecture limits were kept intact by extracting dependency admission, wait, retention, and relaunch responsibilities rather than raising module-size ceilings.

**Release consequence.** These runtime, tool-schema, persistence, tests, and packaged-documentation changes require package/runtime beta.116 and tool schema generation 7. Beta.115 live state remains valid diagnostic evidence only for the incident. Full frozen verification and exact candidate/install-only preparation may proceed without altering the active service; persistent Worker/daemon activation remains a separate explicit owner-authorized boundary.

## 2026-08-22 post-activation independent beta.114 interruption, architecture, and privacy review

**This pass deliberately re-opened the beta.114 conclusions instead of treating the earlier green suite or live activation as proof.** The process/session cancellation paths, managed-job runner, Worker response proxy/cancellation tombstones, daemon-ready handover, relay result retention/redelivery safety, logging projection, public error boundaries, package inventory, and release documentation were reread independently. Targeted privacy, architecture, managed-job, process-cancellation, non-replayable-settlement, MCP response-proxy/controller, structured-logging, security-audit, tool-documentation, current-tree/history privacy, and package-dry-run checks all passed. Several plausible failure theories were also falsified during source tracing rather than converted into speculative fixes: valid hosted MCP calls cannot bypass the stream-cancellation proxy because the HTTP contract requires both JSON and event-stream acceptance; invalid private stream identities do not widen cancellation; the Worker capacity-check -> pending-registration -> daemon-send critical section contains no `await`; and runtime-alarm scheduling absorbs its own persistence failures instead of converting an already-dispatched call into an early public failure.

**The local interruption fix remains correct, with an important distinction between same-isolate and cross-process cancellation.** One-shot execution and process-session startup perform their final `AbortSignal` check after asynchronous resource admission/launch preparation and immediately before synchronous child dispatch. JavaScript run-to-completion prevents an abort callback from interleaving between that check and `spawn`, while the post-spawn path still registers its abort listener and rechecks sticky `signal.aborted`, so the earlier lost-listener theory remains disproved. Managed jobs instead use owner-only cross-process marker state. Their final synchronous marker read immediately before `spawn` is the documented launch decision point: a marker visible there prevents spawn, while a marker written after that decision is post-dispatch cancellation and the runner polls/terminates the owned child. This is intentionally not described as the stronger wall-clock guarantee that `cancel_job` returning must precede every possible racing OS spawn syscall; implementing that different contract would require a serialized cross-process launch/cancel protocol. Existing source-order plus full integration coverage protects the current contract, and the long-form managed-job documentation now makes the boundary explicit so future reviews do not reintroduce the incorrect same-isolate race model.

**The review found maintenance and publication-hygiene improvements rather than another runtime cancellation defect.** `src/local/job-runner.mjs` had grown to nearly seven hundred lines without the line-budget guard applied to the surrounding managed-job modules, so architecture tests now cap it near its current size and force the next material expansion to justify extraction. A historical beta.114 audit sentence that read like present-tense beta.113 runtime state is corrected below to preserve its original review-time meaning without misleading later readers. Tracked privacy/audit/release text also no longer names one machine's ignored application-state directory; the reusable rule is that ignored application databases remain archive-sensitive local metadata, while machine-specific names belong only in local ignored evidence. The npm package intentionally retains the engineering/security audit record, so its size is treated as an explicit publication tradeoff rather than "optimized" by silently dropping audit history.

**One availability-safety path needed observability rather than looser semantics.** The bounded pre-open MCP cancellation registry intentionally enters a temporary global fail-closed mode if its tombstone capacity is exhausted, because evicting cancellation evidence could let a delayed request dispatch after its public response disappeared. That state previously had no persistent-in-isolate diagnostic signal: an owner could observe a temporary wave of cancelled requests without evidence that the cancellation safety valve had tripped. The registry now invokes a non-authoritative callback only when entering a new fail-closed window, and the Worker maps that edge to the existing low-cardinality `mcp_request_cancellation_fail_closed` error counter. Repeated overflow inside the same window is coalesced, callback failure is swallowed so observability cannot weaken cancellation, and the metric contains no stream ID, account identity, request arguments, paths, or result bytes. The fail-closed policy and bounded automatic recovery remain unchanged.

**Release consequence.** This follow-up changes packaged documentation, so the already activated beta.114 tarball, OAuth canary, subscription proof, and interrupted long-soak evidence remain diagnostic evidence for those exact beta.114 bytes but cannot authorize promotion of the revised tree. The corrected tree advances to beta.115 without changing tool schema generation 6. Beta.115 must receive a fresh frozen-tree verification receipt, exact candidate/install-only preparation, new explicit owner authorization before live activation, activated-package canary, schema/validator/subscription checks when applicable, and bounded live interruption/recovery evidence before `release:accept`, commit, or guarded push. A fixed >100-minute live managed-job soak is no longer an acceptance prerequisite; long-running continuity is verified through the host-safe long-poll, same-response follow-up, exercised daemon/relay or real host/tool boundary recovery when relevant, cancellation cleanup, and replay-safety properties that directly cover the failure modes.

## 2026-08-22 beta.114 independent interruption hardening review

**A second independent pass treated interruption safety as an acceptance/replay problem rather than only a long-poll problem.** Three high-severity gaps remained even after beta.114 had already introduced explicit-job retention priority and typed `read_job not_found`. First, hosted `start_job` still allowed omission of `idempotency_key`; if the daemon persisted/started the job and the host then lost the acceptance response, the caller could not deterministically recover the logical submission and a blind retry could create a second job. Second, private response cancellation was edge-triggered against the pending-call map: a cancellation control that arrived before the matching direct request reached pending registration could be forgotten, and the controller also did not preserve an already-aborted public request signal when opening direct stream ownership. Third, the initial relay-result repair used one permanent owner-wide `automatic_redelivery_safe=false` fuse after any single lost completion proof, preventing duplicate replay but unnecessarily disabling missing-call recovery for every unrelated later call in that daemon lifetime.

**Hosted managed-job acceptance is now idempotent-by-contract before dispatch.** Generation-6 remote `start_job` requires a caller-held `idempotency_key`; the actual Worker invocation validator, not only the description, rejects a missing key before daemon dispatch. Worker timeout/disconnect recovery metadata carries the same key and instructs a retry of the same tool arguments with the same key. The local/stdio plan API remains capable of optional keys because it does not cross the same hosted acceptance boundary. The runtime still treats the deduplication window as bounded by retained job state rather than claiming permanent exactly-once execution.

**Response cancellation now has sticky pre-dispatch ownership.** A bounded `McpRequestCancellationRegistry` records a private cancellation that arrives before the direct request opens, immediately propagates an already-aborted public request signal, and aborts an active request lease. The Worker still rechecks that signal before pending-call registration and daemon send. Cancellation tombstones contain only random stream-control identity and expiry state, are capped, and expire; if the cap itself is exceeded, the registry temporarily fails closed rather than dropping cancellation evidence and later dispatching an orphaned side effect. Tests cover cancel-before-open, active cancel, already-aborted request, expiry, bounded overflow fail-closed, and automatic recovery after the tombstone lifetime.

**Relay replay safety is now per-call rather than owner-wide.** Lost completed-result ownership moves the affected call ID into a bounded private `RelayRedeliverySafety` tombstone set. Reconnect can still classify an unrelated resumed call as daemon-proven missing while refusing to do so for the unsafe ID. A tombstone retires once the Worker no longer resumes that ID; only exhaustion of the bounded safety set escalates to global fail-closed redelivery disablement. Retained result bytes/lifetimes, replay-safety policy, capacity policy, and diagnostic projection were split into separate modules instead of expanding one recovery class. Diagnostics expose only aggregate tombstone count and global-disabled state, not identities or content.

**The review also reproduced two different interruption classes outside the durable job itself.** A composite deterministic lifecycle/fault verification reported its internal scenarios and post-fault action sweep as successful, then its outer `exec_command` carrier was terminated at an arbitrary 300-second budget. That is orchestration-budget failure, not a verifier verdict; project testing/release rules now require multi-stage or unknown-duration system verification to use separately terminal managed-job steps with credible per-step budgets. Separately, one ordinary read-only repository `read_file` call hit a daemon tool timeout during this review. At that review point the activated runtime was still beta.113/generation 5, so that live symptom was evidence that interruption work remained release-blocking, not evidence that the then-unactivated beta.114 source had solved every transport/runtime timeout. Beta.114 therefore still required fresh frozen verification and renewed live interruption acceptance after explicit owner-authorized activation before any release claim; the post-activation review above records the later evidence and its release consequence.

**The process-launch review then narrowed one remaining local race to the resource-ownership handoff itself.** The earlier concern that an abort between child spawn and listener registration could be lost was incorrect because `AbortSignal.aborted` is sticky and the active-process path already performs a post-registration check. The real pre-side-effect boundary was after asynchronous resource admission returned a lease but before child dispatch. One-shot execution and process-session startup now make a final synchronous `throwIfCancelled` call after launch preparation and immediately before `spawn`; because no `await` separates those operations in the same Node isolate, an already-observed abort cannot cross into child dispatch. Cancellation at that point uses the existing pre-spawn cleanup path and releases the admitted lease without creating process ownership. The managed-job runner applies the same rule to its durable cancellation marker after admission and delegated-command preparation: the final synchronous marker read is the launch decision point, so a marker visible there prevents spawn, while a marker that becomes visible only after that decision is treated as post-dispatch cancellation and follows the existing termination/cleanup path rather than being misreported as definite non-execution. Deterministic resource-admission regressions prove zero-spawn/exact-release behavior for one-shot and session launches; a runner boundary assertion prevents asynchronous work from being inserted between the managed-job marker check and `spawn`, and the full managed-job cancellation/cleanup integration remains the behavior-level guard.

**One product boundary remains intentionally explicit.** MCP can keep a detached managed job alive and preserve a recovery identifier, but it cannot create a new ChatGPT assistant turn after the host has terminated the current response. Increasing `wait_ms` cannot remove that external boundary. The repository's responsibility is to avoid amplifying it: acceptance must be recoverable, cancellation must not be lost before dispatch, completed-result evidence must not become false non-execution proof, and a later available turn must recover the same durable operation rather than resubmit its side effect. No documentation or test is permitted to imply that Machine Bridge itself can autonomously originate the next product turn.

## 2026-08-22 host query-cache freshness review

**The apparent mixed-generation catalog was traced to the host's filtered tool/resource search layer, not to Machine Bridge runtime state.** Repeated unfiltered discovery of the complete `machine-mcp` catalog returned all 54 tools at schema generation 5, including the hosted `read_job` default of 40,000 ms and maximum of 300,000 ms. Immediately before and after those complete snapshots, a filtered host search for `read_job` could reproducibly return a prior generation-4 bundle whose `read_job` description still claimed a 300,000 ms omitted default. The two result families alternated without replacing one another. Machine Bridge's Worker cannot generate that query-dependent split: `tools/list` receives no host search query, the catalog is constructed once from one `server-metadata.json` generation, every public definition is tagged from that generation, and both discovery TTLs are zero.

**Legacy local execution state was independently excluded.** Live `server_info` reported beta.112/schema generation 5, one authenticated ready daemon and no previous daemon connection, while the current Worker isolate was fresh; the retained managed-job inventory contained no active legacy job capable of owning a schema. A control experiment then loaded the stale generation-4 filtered definition, started a 45-second durable sleep, and called `read_job` with `wait_ms` omitted. The actual invocation remained inside Machine Bridge for the generation-5 40,000 ms default and returned `tool_schema_generation=5`, `managed_job_read_wait_ms=40000`, `status_polling_mode=bounded_followup`, and `host_turn_handoff_recommended=false`; a second same-response read reached terminal state. The stale filtered definition therefore affected model-facing search metadata, not the invocation path, daemon validator, managed runner, or live Worker catalog.

**The cache's exact host-internal scope remains unobservable and is not guessed.** It may be conversation-, account-, workspace-, or search-backend scoped; Machine Bridge receives neither the filter query nor cache metadata and has no protocol operation that can purge that secondary index. The repository-side correction is therefore evidentiary rather than a fictitious cache-clear mechanism: schema freshness is established from one complete unfiltered host catalog snapshot plus harmless invocation-validator/behavior probes. Filtered host tool/resource search remains useful for routing, but a stale filtered result that conflicts with the complete unfiltered catalog is recorded as host query/search-index cache staleness and cannot, by itself, classify the live catalog as mixed-generation or block release acceptance. Architecture release-contract tests prohibit the superseded multiple-filtered-search freshness rule.

## 2026-08-23 beta.115 host-cache audit removal from release acceptance

**Release-process decision.** ChatGPT-internal `api_tool` / tool-loader cache inspection is removed from release and upgrade acceptance. Future acceptance must not query, compare, grade, or remediate those host-internal cache views. The evidence boundary is limited to the live Machine Bridge runtime/discovery contract, the real `toolsListChanged` subscription behavior when that contract changed, the governed Workspace Action control snapshot when ChatGPT workspace publication governance applies and owner-authorized inspection is available, and harmless changed-generation invocation behavior that reaches the activated runtime. Historical loader-cache observations below remain postmortem evidence for why the process was simplified; they are not executable release steps, freshness gates, or remediation triggers.

## 2026-08-22 beta.115 ChatGPT tool-loader freshness correction

**Later beta.115 evidence falsified the stronger conclusion above that an unfiltered ChatGPT resource load is the product-level publication authority.** After the beta.115 Workspace app had been refreshed in Action control and displayed the current 54 generation-6 actions, the same connected ChatGPT session could still return a generation-5 bundle from an unfiltered `api_tool.list_resources(paths=["machine-mcp"])` load while a query-targeted `api_tool.list_resources(..., query="server_info")` load immediately returned generation-6 definitions. Live `server_info` independently reported beta.115/schema generation 6 with zero discovery/tool-list TTLs, and a syntactically valid but deliberately unretained `read_job` reached the generation-6 runtime and returned typed non-retryable `not_found`. Reconnecting the connector did not change the old unfiltered loader bundle. Those observations are incompatible with treating that bundle as a faithful read of the Workspace approved snapshot.

**The corrected model separates three authorities instead of promoting one ChatGPT loader path.** Machine Bridge `server/discover`/`tools/list`, `server_info`, subscription behavior, and invocation probes establish the live MCP/runtime contract. When ChatGPT workspace governance freezes approved actions, Workspace Settings -> Apps -> Action control is the product-level publication snapshot and may be inspected/refreshed only with current-task owner authorization. ChatGPT `api_tool`/resource loading is a model/tool-loader cache above those layers; filtered and unfiltered loads can retain different generations and are diagnostic only. The exact cache key/scope remains unobservable and is not guessed. A stale loader result can justify treating the current model/tool view as cached, but it does **not** prove the Workspace app is stale and must not trigger reconnect, recreation, or republication by itself.

**Release consequence.** The beta.113 “complete unfiltered host catalog” rule is superseded. Freshness for a governed ChatGPT app now requires the current Workspace Action control snapshot (when applicable), live runtime/discovery convergence, and harmless changed-generation invocation behavior. Product-level recreation/republication is reserved for a Workspace approved snapshot that the supported refresh/review path cannot update in place, or for explicit workspace governance requiring republication. Architecture release-contract tests enforce this separation so a future stale `api_tool` bundle cannot be misclassified as a reason to rebuild the app.

## 2026-08-22 beta.113 interruption-retention follow-up

**The first beta.113 >100-minute acceptance attempt exposed a second continuity dimension that the 40-second per-call fix did not cover.** The activated beta.113 Worker/daemon, OAuth canary, complete unfiltered 54-tool generation-5 catalog, previous-generation validator probes, deployed `toolsListChanged` subscription proof, and 55-second unchanged-status fixture all passed. The long soak then stayed in one real assistant response for roughly 125 consecutive default `read_job` calls, each returning a structured generation-5 `running` result after the full 40-second server wait with `bounded_followup` and no forced handoff. The host nevertheless imposed a real response/tool boundary before the 100-minute job reached terminal state. A later explicit 60,000 ms active `read_job` also returned normally, proving that the host's per-call lifetime can exceed the 40-second default. Together these observations falsify the stronger assumption that per-call survival or a simple `duration / wait` response-count estimate proves aggregate same-response continuity. The exact aggregate host limit remains unobservable and is not inferred from elapsed time or call count.

**The more serious defect appeared when the same soak job was inspected after that real boundary.** `read_job` no longer returned terminal state; it collapsed to generic `execution_failed`. The retained inventory showed that the job record had disappeared and that the current 50 retained slots were occupied by later helper work. Source tracing found that explicit `start_job` workloads and the durable one-step jobs created by `exec_command`, `run_process`, and `run_local_command` all shared the same 50-state terminal FIFO. Every later diagnostic/build helper therefore competed directly with the long workload's terminal recovery evidence, and capacity pruning could delete the explicit result before the next host response had a chance to read it. The job itself had been durably owned while running; the interruption defect was loss of the post-boundary recovery record.

**The corrected retention policy preserves the same privacy/disk ceiling while changing eviction priority.** One-step process carriers are now written with an internal `retention_class=transient_process`. When capacity pressure has removable transient terminal history, pruning reclaims that helper history before explicit managed-job terminal records. The global 50-state cap and seven-day/24-hour time bounds remain unchanged, and the low-cardinality marker is not part of public job projection and contains no path, argv, output, resource value, or credential. A valid job ID whose directory is no longer retained now maps to non-retryable typed `not_found` with a fixed privacy-safe message instead of generic `execution_failed`. That error explicitly does **not** prove the underlying side effect never executed, so recovery guidance forbids blind resubmission.

**A separate replay-safety review found an exactly-once proof gap behind relay result expiry.** The daemon retains a completed result in memory until Worker acknowledgement, but bounded acknowledgement retention eventually expires. Before this review, expiry removed the result while `RelayCallRecovery.redeliverySafe` remained true. A later reconnect could therefore see the Worker's resumed call ID absent from both active calls and retained results, emit it as `missing`, and let the Worker interpret that absence as proof that the daemon never executed the call. Suspension or delayed timers make it unsafe to rely on independent Worker/daemon deadlines always settling in the intended order. The first beta.114 repair conservatively cleared automatic redelivery for the entire recovery owner after such a loss. The independent beta.114 review above refined that fail-closed policy to bounded **per-call** replay-safety tombstones so the affected call cannot be replayed while unrelated calls retain automatic recovery; only exhaustion of the tombstone evidence capacity disables redelivery globally. Diagnostics remain aggregate-only.

**The public response cancellation path had one smaller unbounded wait.** Request/reader cancellation intentionally waited for the private Durable Object cancellation control before closing the public SSE response, but a private `bridge.fetch()` that never settled could therefore keep the public response open forever. The control fetch now has a fixed two-second internal settlement deadline and its own abort signal. Normal cancellation still preserves private-before-public ordering; a stalled control path closes at the bound while the daemon-side operation retains its independent operation timeout. The regression fixture uses a never-resolving private fetch and proves bounded public closure without adding content logging.

**Release consequence.** Beta.113 remains useful live diagnosis but is superseded for acceptance: its package does not contain the retention-priority, typed-recovery, replay-safety, or bounded-cancellation fixes above. The next candidate advances package/runtime identity and the hosted orchestration contract. Its acceptance must treat per-call pacing and aggregate host-response lifetime as separate evidence. For an actual >100-minute job, either the same response reaches terminal state or a real host boundary is observed; in the latter case the next available response must recover the same job ID and terminal result after realistic helper-command churn. A response-count calculation alone no longer counts. No beta.113 `release:accept`, tag, publication, or push is authorized by the earlier green canary/short fixtures.

## 2026-08-21 independent beta.109 follow-up review

**The critical question was not whether the beta.109 candidate still passed its existing tests, but whether the new continuity/freshness paths preserved the repository's older trust invariants under the failure branches those tests were intended to improve.** The initial `check:fast` baseline passed all 104 tasks, so this review deliberately targeted semantic gaps that a green happy-path suite would not disprove: publication races, runner-identity trust, host/schema evidence boundaries, timeout arithmetic, ignored privacy surfaces, and release-proof validity.

**Four source-level corrections were required.** First, the resource transaction lock had grown a second asynchronous `MBM_MULTIPLE_HARD_LINKS` retry that could continue until the whole transaction deadline. That contradicted the repository's fixed four-attempt/1 ms publication-window invariant and could turn a persistent ownership-integrity failure into ordinary waiting; the outer retry was removed and an architecture regression now forbids it. Second, the new hosted runner-liveness module read `runner.pid` through a generic bounded reader instead of the established runner-claim reader, losing path-identity verification, multiple-hard-link rejection, and the same fixed publication retry. Liveness now reuses the secure claim reader and tests prove multiply-linked ownership evidence fails closed there too. Third, subscription diagnostics previously used an `observed` name even though the Worker marks the state when it constructs the open SSE response and cannot prove that an external client read either frame. The field is now `tools_list_change_subscription_opened_for_account`, paired with `tools_list_change_subscription_client_receipt_observable=false`, and release documentation no longer treats that server-side fact as host-refresh evidence. Fourth, the five-minute `read_job` wait had only five seconds of daemon headroom even though a non-current/ambiguous runner can spend up to three seconds in the async process-generation probe and then enter the legacy reconciliation path, whose defensive process-generation recheck can spend another three seconds. The dedicated execution/settlement envelope is therefore 310/315 seconds; ordinary relay tools remain capped at 50 seconds.

**Live hosted evidence exposed a second schema-freshness boundary that discovery text alone cannot prove.** In the same session, the model-facing `read_job` definition described the generation-4 300,000 ms maximum while an attempted `wait_ms=300000` call was rejected by the host invocation layer using the former 40,000 ms maximum before Machine Bridge received it. Release/upgrade guidance now requires harmless previous-generation boundary probes in addition to whole-catalog description checks: beta.109 uses a terminal `read_job` with `wait_ms=40001`, plus a non-executing staged step with `timeout_seconds=3601`, so both the host's displayed catalog and its actual invocation validator must cross the old limits before acceptance.

**A second live probe then reproduced the remaining interruption instead of inferring it from the stale validator.** A durable ten-minute sleep fixture stayed healthy in the managed-job runner. An explicit `read_job(wait_ms=40000)` remained in one MCP call for the full forty seconds and returned a normal running status. The same read with `wait_ms` omitted selected beta.109's then-current 300,000 ms server default and the host terminated the tool call with an unstructured `TimeoutError` while the durable job continued. This falsifies the premise that reducing host-call count monotonically improves continuity: the server must satisfy both a per-call host-lifetime bound and an aggregate call-density bound. The default is therefore 40,000 ms, which is directly demonstrated on this host and is compatible with the stale 40,000 ms invocation validator, while the public maximum remains 300,000 ms for clients that have independently demonstrated a longer request lifetime. A 100-minute unchanged job is bounded to 150 default reads—about 1.5 calls/minute, still an order of magnitude below the earlier roughly 20 Machine Bridge calls/minute incident class. The five-minute value is now an opt-in capability, not an assumption about every host.

**Recovery-budget review found one related failure amplifier.** Pre-dispatch daemon recovery and same-ID non-delivery redelivery correctly consume the original absolute tool deadline, but once fewer than ten seconds of `read_job` execution budget remained the argument clamp could previously reduce the wait to zero and dispatch anyway. That discarded the very reconciliation headroom reserved for async/legacy runner-generation checks and could turn one recoverable relay interruption into a second daemon timeout. The Worker now rejects such an under-budget initial dispatch before side effects and declines such a redelivery; callers receive a retryable recovery error instead of an internally self-contradictory immediate read.

**The wait implementation itself also hid latency outside the advertised bound.** `waitForManagedJobRead()` formerly started its monotonic deadline only after the initial authoritative `readHosted()` reconciliation and, on an unchanged timeout, unconditionally performed another full `readHosted()` after the deadline. A nominal forty-second wait could therefore become `initial reconciliation + 40 seconds + final reconciliation`, while the explicit five-minute path had the same structural overrun. That was incompatible with the claim that the default was selected to fit a hosted per-call lifetime. The deadline now starts before the initial full read. Initial reconciliation therefore consumes the same bounded call window, periodic full reconciliations occur only at the thirty-second cadence inside that deadline, lightweight persisted-status reads keep the returned active status current, and an unchanged timeout does not append another heavyweight post-deadline reconciliation. A synthetic regression makes the initial full read consume twelve seconds and proves the entire hosted wait still ends at forty seconds rather than fifty-two.

**A separate authority/continuity review removed a redundant asynchronous ownership state.** The candidate had introduced `PendingAdmissionGate` to serialize Worker capacity decisions, but the actual capacity check, pending-record registration, and daemon `send()` are all synchronous operations in one Durable Object JavaScript turn. The gate therefore added no needed atomicity. Under contention it instead created a third pre-dispatch state: a request could be queued after authorization while belonging to neither the authority-bound ready-daemon waiter set nor the pending-call registry. An authority revocation in that interval could not identify the queued request. The gate is removed. An already-ready daemon now takes an explicit synchronous fast path, and the capacity check -> pending registration -> daemon send critical section contains no `await`; when no daemon is ready, the request remains in the separately authority-bound ready-waiter registry. Architecture tests prohibit both the deleted queue module and any future async interleaving point in the register/send critical section.

**Daemon handover was tightened so reconnect cannot create another ownerless or capacity-leapfrog window.** WebSocket and HTTPS fallback channels become internally ready before all previous-channel cleanup/alarm bookkeeping has settled. Releasing `daemon-ready` waiters at that first internal-ready edge removed their authority-bound waiter ownership and then yielded during cleanup, leaving revocation unable to find them before they registered as pending calls. It also let fresh traffic see the ready socket and consume capacity ahead of waiters that had already been admitted under the shared reconnect-capacity calculation. Both transports now call `notifyReadyDaemon` only at the final no-`await` point after their asynchronous handover settlement. While any ready waiters remain, the ready socket is treated as handover-in-progress: fresh calls join the same waiter set, formal notification releases the complete batch, and waiter timeout returns retryable `unavailable` rather than bypassing a missing final notification. Regression coverage proves a waiter remains revocable even after the replacement socket is visible and that fresh calls cannot leapfrog its reserved capacity.

**Completed-but-unacknowledged relay results are now part of the same bounded ownership model instead of an unbounded side cache.** Each normalized tool result can be several MiB, and `RelayCallRecovery` retained a result until Worker acknowledgement while removing the corresponding call from the active-call set. If acknowledgement traffic were lost while the relay remained otherwise usable, new calls could continue entering and the retained-result Map could grow without bound, increasing both memory pressure and private-result residence time. Local relay admission now combines active relay tools with unacknowledged result count under the existing 16-total / 14-ordinary / 2-reserved-control capacity algebra. Retained results consume ordinary capacity; once ordinary recovery ownership reaches 14, another ordinary call is rejected before execution with retryable `limit_exceeded` and `side_effects_started=false`, while `diagnose_runtime`/`list_roots` retain their reserved slots until total ownership reaches 16. The normal retained-result store independently refuses to grow past 16 entries as a last-resort invariant guard and never evicts an already-executed older result to make room. A later independent review found one unsafe fallback behind that guard: if the invariant were nevertheless violated, `RelayCallRecovery` sent the next completed result without retaining acknowledgement ownership. A lost acknowledgement followed by reconnect could then make the Worker treat an already-executed call as daemon-proven missing and eligible for redelivery. Retention is now encapsulated rather than exposed as a mutable Map, and exactly one non-admission emergency slot preserves ownership for that first invariant breach; using it emits an error-level capacity event, it never raises normal admission above 16, and a second overflow is not sent. A second audit had also found that count-bounded retention was still time-unbounded when send succeeded but the Worker acknowledgement alone was lost while the relay stayed ready: the reconnect timer is intentionally not armed on a healthy transport. `relay-result-retention.mjs` now records the monotonic first-retained time and prunes a still-unacknowledged result after the 315-second maximum Worker tool-settlement lifetime on the next live relay pulse; disconnected transport cleanup still uses the shorter reconnect grace. `diagnose_runtime.runtime.relay_result_recovery` projects only aggregate active/retained/capacity counts, not call IDs, tool names, arguments, or result contents.

**Public HTTP disconnect was found to be an unreliable subscription-ownership signal in the Workers execution model.** The direct stream proxy can issue a private stream-scoped cancel when its own `AbortSignal`/ReadableStream cancellation path fires, and a unit regression now waits for that private cancellation to settle before exposing public stream closure. Real Wrangler integration nevertheless showed a stricter platform boundary: aborting the public `fetch()` closed the client-side body, but the Durable Object still reported one active `toolsListChanged` subscription after one second and again after more than a public heartbeat interval. Repeated heartbeat enqueue therefore cannot be treated as disconnect detection either. Because one Worker deployment has immutable tool definitions and every subscription receives an immediate level-trigger `notifications/tools/list_changed`, there is no later same-deployment freshness edge that requires an indefinitely open request. `mcp-subscription-contract.ts` now defines a 10-second server lease (two public heartbeat intervals). Explicit cancel, authority revocation, or a genuinely observable request abort still releases earlier; otherwise lease expiry aborts the Durable Object stream and returns capacity. `server_info.tool_delivery.tools_list_change_subscription_lease_ms` exposes the bound. Worker integration proves a vanished public client cannot retain the per-account/global subscription slot beyond the lease, while `opened_for_account` remains only bounded server-side diagnostic history.

**Privacy and dependency checks remained clean, but ignored local state still requires operational discipline.** `privacy:history` passed across 735 current tracked/unignored files, 4,299 reachable historical blobs, and 170 commit messages with the owner-local denylist active. Both production-only and complete `npm audit` runs reported zero vulnerabilities. An ignored application-local SQLite database plus WAL/SHM state was present during that review and therefore remained a local privacy surface even though ordinary Git/npm flows excluded it; such local application state must stay out of workspace/support archives, and the machine-specific directory name is intentionally not retained in publication documentation.
