The existing `.release-candidate/` beta.109 tarball and canary records describe the exact pre-review candidate bytes and contain only integrity/boolean canary evidence in their JSON metadata, but every source change in this review makes those candidate bytes stale for promotion. They are historical evidence, not authorization to release the revised tree.

## 2026-08-20 beta.109 mixed-schema and long-duration continuation correction

**Beta.108 fixed one real amplifier but did not close the reported continuity defect.** Its 40-second `read_job` coalescing materially reduced rapid status loops, yet two later observations disproved the stronger claim that long autonomous work was fully repaired. First, the same ChatGPT connector exposed a mixed tool catalog: one discovery query returned generation-3 descriptions while another query still returned old `read_job`, `start_job`, `run_process`, and `start_process` guidance that referenced a guessed host response/execution budget or the beta.104 checkpoint boundary. The live beta.108 source contained none of that positive wording, so this was not a missed source-string cleanup. Second, a 40-second default still permits about 150 `read_job` responses over a 100-minute unchanged task. The exact host interaction quota remains unobservable, but Machine Bridge still had avoidable interaction density and an explicit protocol signal telling the host that its tool list never changes.

**The schema-freshness defect is protocol-level.** Beta.108 set both discovery TTLs to zero but deliberately left current Worker capability `tools.listChanged=false`; `subscriptions/listen` acknowledged an empty supported subset and immediately completed. A long-lived host was therefore allowed to retain stale descriptions, and partial refresh could create the mixed-generation state observed above. Beta.109 implements the MCP 2026-07-28 subscription contract for the current remote surface: `tools.listChanged=true`, `subscriptions/listen` accepts `toolsListChanged`, acknowledges the supported subset, emits a correlated level-trigger `notifications/tools/list_changed`, and keeps the SSE subscription open until cancellation. The initialization-era 2025 compatibility surface remains `listChanged=false` rather than falsely promising the older unsolicited-notification model. The subscription stream is isolated from ordinary tool-response streaming so the two lifecycles cannot silently share completion assumptions. Because a request-rate limiter does not bound already-open SSE streams, a dedicated Durable Object subscription-capacity component caps active streams at 32 globally and 8 per authenticated account; admission occurs before stream creation and abort/cancel releases one slot exactly once. The per-account ceiling prevents a delegated account from starving the owner's freshness channel, while the global ceiling bounds aggregate resource retention without adding persisted subscription state. A later independent pass also corrected the diagnostic semantics: stream construction can prove only that the server opened a subscription response for the authenticated account, not that the external client read its acknowledgement or notification. The diagnostic is therefore named `tools_list_change_subscription_opened_for_account`, and `tools_list_change_subscription_client_receipt_observable=false` makes the unobservable client-receipt/catalog-refresh boundary explicit instead of encoding an unsupported `observed` claim.

**The initial beta.109 pacing experiment used a 300,000 ms default; the August 21 follow-up above supersedes that default after live host timeout evidence.** The retained 300,000 ms maximum still receives a 310-second daemon execution budget and 315-second settlement deadline when an explicitly capable client requests it. The ordinary hosted default is now 40,000 ms with a 50/55-second daemon/settlement envelope. The ten-second execution headroom remains intentional: the non-current/ambiguous-runner branch can first spend up to three seconds in async process-generation sampling and then enter the legacy reconciliation path, whose defensive recheck can spend another three seconds. Local/stdio status reads remain immediate by default.

**A separate execution-lifetime audit found a hard one-hour ceiling below the status layer.** Managed jobs were durable across MCP disconnects and runner-owned idle-sleep assertions already remained active for the runner lifetime, but each individual main/finally step was rejected above 3,600 seconds. That directly contradicted the requirement that one continuous command be able to run beyond 100 minutes. Beta.109 keeps the ten-minute default but raises the explicit per-step maximum to 21,600 seconds (six hours) in the local plan validator and both published job schemas. The runner starts the child execution timer only after cooperative resource admission, so a pre-spawn wait does not consume the six-hour execution budget. A non-executing staged-plan regression retains a 6,100-second step and the upper-bound regression rejects 21,601 seconds. One-step `run_process`/`exec_command` remain capped at 600 seconds and must route genuinely longer continuous work to `start_job`.

**The reported `host_pressure_red / disk_iops_critical` episode was also a Machine Bridge classification defect, not evidence that the Mac was saturated.** The Darwin sampler uses `iostat -Id`, whose transfer-count and MB/s fields describe activity rather than device busy percentage, queue saturation, or service-time exhaustion. The old policy nevertheless promoted >=5,000 IOPS or >=220 MB/s directly to Red, and `resource-host-cache.mjs` could carry that one-second I/O peak into later quick samples for thirty seconds. Live diagnostics after the reported block showed an otherwise Green 8-core machine with about two busy cores, 41% free memory, zero leases/waiters, and a direct one-second `iostat` interval of only 75 transfers / 0.62 MB. Beta.109 therefore treats fresh raw Darwin I/O throughput only as Yellow concurrency evidence; hard Red requires an independent critical signal or a severe load backlog corroborated by current CPU/I/O evidence. I/O hints now expire after the same five-second freshness window, and an expired peak is not copied into a current quick sample. Tests prove a fresh 230-MB/s / 5,100-IOPS sample remains admitted Yellow, while a genuinely severe corroborated backlog still becomes Red.

**A second independent pass removed two residual hot-path/privacy weaknesses rather than accepting them as low frequency.** Resource lease/waiter pruning was already async-only, but a healthy hosted managed-job read still reached `runnerProcessIsCurrent()` during the thirty-second full reconciliation interval, and that helper can invoke synchronous `ps` to verify process generation. Relay `read_job` now uses `readHosted()`: it checks the current runner through `inspectProcessInstanceAsync`, returns directly when ownership is current, and falls back to the existing recovery reconciliation only for non-current or ambiguous ownership. Tests keep the event loop runnable while the async process-start observation is deliberately held pending, and architecture gates require relay reads to stay on that path. Separately, the browser request registry previously forwarded a synchronous lower-level `transport.send()` exception into an exposed read-only `BridgeError`; beta.109 replaces that raw text with a fixed public send-failure message so a future transport implementation cannot leak paths or implementation diagnostics through this boundary. Mutation dispatch continues to use the stronger unknown-outcome/non-replayable settlement.

**Live beta.110 acceptance probing exposed one remaining Machine Bridge projection defect.** The exact beta.110 candidate activated successfully as the Worker/login daemon, live `server_info` reported beta.110/tool-schema generation 5 with the 40,000 ms hosted default and the bounded subscription diagnostics, and the canary executed from the activated package root successfully proved authorization-code exchange, authenticated MCP, refresh rotation, refreshed MCP, and cleanup. A 55-second durable fixture then entered `running`; its first parameter-omitting `read_job` remained inside one MCP call for the full 40,000 ms as intended, but the unchanged timeout checkpoint omitted `host_turn_handoff_recommended=false`, `status_polling_mode=bounded_followup`, `tool_schema_generation=5`, and related hosted metadata. Source inspection showed that every lightweight `readProgress()` probe assigned its reduced public projection over the richer `readHosted()` result; the timeout path therefore returned the last lightweight projection, whereas the later terminal read performed an authoritative full read and regained the fields. The repair merges unchanged lightweight progress into the existing full state, and a deterministic regression supplies hosted metadata only from `readCurrent()` so the former replacement behavior fails for the same reason as the live candidate.

**Beta.111 live retesting proved that projection repair, then exposed a distinct daemon-local schema split during the required cross-generation validator probe.** The exact beta.111 candidate activated successfully, live `server_info` reported beta.111/generation 5, the activated-package OAuth canary again passed authorization-code exchange, authenticated MCP, refresh rotation, refreshed MCP, and cleanup, and the 55-second fixture now returned a full 40,000 ms unchanged checkpoint with `host_turn_handoff_recommended=false`, `status_polling_mode=bounded_followup`, `tool_schema_generation=5`, `host_turn_deadline_observable=false`, and `managed_jobs_detached_from_mcp_response=true`; a second `read_job` in the same assistant response reached terminal state. Host-side discovery had also refreshed far enough to expose generation-5 long-running tool definitions, and a non-executing `stage_job(timeout_seconds=3601)` crossed the former one-hour boundary and was then cancelled before start. The terminal `read_job(wait_ms=40001)` probe nevertheless failed with `maximum: 40000`. Source inspection changed the causal classification: `src/worker/tool-catalog.ts` correctly overlays hosted discovery to `maximumManagedJobReadWaitMs=300000`, and `managed-job-read-wait.mjs` already accepts that relay maximum, but `src/local/tool-executor.mjs` compiled its defense-in-depth validators from the static local catalog where `read_job.wait_ms.maximum=40000`. The invocation therefore reached Machine Bridge and failed inside the daemon, rather than being blocked by the external host. The repair adds a relay-only validation extension analogous to the existing hosted durable-process timeout extension. It accepts only the static `/wait_ms` `maximum` mismatch when the relay value is an integer from 40,001 through 300,000; local/stdio remains capped at 40,000 and malformed or over-hosted-limit values still fail.

**Release consequence.** PR publication remains paused. Beta.110 and beta.111 were both successfully activated and canary-proven generation-5 runtimes, but each live acceptance cycle found a different blocking packaged-runtime defect before acceptance, so their candidate/activation/canary evidence is diagnostic only. The corrected bytes advance to **beta.112 while remaining tool schema generation 5**; the public schema is unchanged, but package/runtime identity must change because the daemon validation implementation and shipped documentation differ from beta.111. Beta.112 requires a fresh frozen full verification receipt, exact candidate, install-only preflight, explicit live activation, activated-package OAuth canary, the 55-second continuity fixture, and the cross-generation validator probes. The terminal `read_job(wait_ms=40001)` probe must now return immediately through the live beta.112 daemon, while the non-executing 3,601-second staged job must remain accepted and cancellable. At the time of beta.112, acceptance also conservatively required current Worker/daemon generation 5, the live `toolsListChanged` subscription edge, and agreement across several independent filtered host discovery queries covering the long-running tools. Beta.113 live evidence later disproved filtered-query agreement as a valid freshness authority: query-keyed host search snapshots can remain stale while the complete unfiltered catalog and actual invocation path are generation 5. The current acceptance rule therefore uses one complete unfiltered catalog plus validator/behavior probes and treats filtered-query disagreement as diagnostic host search-index staleness. The final long-duration acceptance target remains an actual >100-minute managed workload under the generation-5 host schema, including repeated host-safe 40-second reads, not only a synthetic call-density calculation.

## 2026-08-20 beta.108 long-task host-boundary and schema-freshness correction

**The reported roughly 20–30-minute stops were initially misclassified as a fixed external wall-clock boundary.** The owner supplied a decisive counterexample: before the recent orchestration changes, the same Machine Bridge/host combination repeatedly sustained workflows beyond 100 minutes. No Machine Bridge tool, daemon, Worker, managed-job runner, relay diagnostic, or retained job result reports a 26-minute total-task limit, and the host's exact internal quota/termination algorithm is not observable from this repository. What *is* observable is a product-created interaction amplifier. Beta.104's retained incident evidence already recorded 497 settled Machine Bridge calls in 25 minutes, including 91 `read_job` calls. Beta.106 correctly removed beta.104's forced later-user-turn handoff, but `read_job` itself remained an immediate status read, so an agent autonomously following one unchanged durable job could consume host tool interactions far faster than the underlying task advanced. The evidence therefore supports host-interaction amplification as the Machine Bridge defect; it does not support a universal 20–30-minute ChatGPT wall-clock limit.

**Beta.108 moves waiting into the server instead of the hosted reasoning loop.** A relay-origin active `read_job` with omitted `wait_ms` now waits inside Machine Bridge for up to forty seconds and returns early on meaningful status/phase progress or terminal state. `wait_ms=0` remains an explicit immediate checkpoint. One-second probes read only the secure persisted status record; full runner/recovery reconciliation is bounded to ten-second intervals plus the authoritative return path, avoiding a new synchronous process-identity hot loop while the host call is coalesced. The wait uses a monotonic deadline, checks cancellation between samples, and receives a 45-second daemon execution budget plus the existing Worker settlement envelope. The Worker's five-second SSE heartbeat keeps that one response stream active while the daemon waits. At the forty-second default, a synthetic 100-minute unchanged durable job is bounded to at most 150 hosted `read_job` calls; the regression suite fixes that call-density ceiling so autonomy cannot silently regress into rapid checkpoints again. The same composition review found `read_process` still had beta.104's opposite failure mode: after one blocking read, another would-block call inside the fifteen-second cooldown returned an immediate running status. Under beta.106 same-turn autonomy that can itself become a rapid tool-call loop. Beta.108 keeps the actual output/exit blocking stage capped at one second but paces a repeated would-block request inside that same MCP call until output/exit or the cooldown boundary; the Worker reserves a 20-second execution / 25-second settlement envelope for that path. For `wait_for_exit=true`, ordinary output notifications are deliberately ignored by the cooldown stage: they are accumulated for the final readback but do not turn an exit wait back into one-second hosted checkpoints. These changes materially lower host tool-call density while preserving same-assistant-response autonomy; stale host schemas benefit immediately because omitted `read_job.wait_ms` selects the relay default. `server_info.tool_delivery` exposes the managed-job read wait bounds, `host_turn_deadline_observable=false`, and `managed_jobs_detached_from_mcp_response=true`. Those fields state only what Machine Bridge can verify: durable execution is decoupled from one MCP response, while any external host turn quota remains unobservable and must not be guessed from elapsed minutes.

**Host schema caching was a second concrete continuity gap.** After beta.106 activation, the daemon and Worker were on the new runtime while the current ChatGPT connector continued exposing the beta.104 `read_job`/`read_process` descriptions that mandated one checkpoint and later-user-turn handoff. A relay interruption or daemon replacement does not prove an external host discarded its cached tool/action snapshot. Beta.108 therefore makes both discovery surfaces non-reusable: `server/discover` and `tools/list` advertise `ttlMs=0`, descriptions carry tool schema generation 3, and `server_info.tool_delivery` reports `tool_schema_generation`, `tool_schema_server_version`, `discovery_ttl_ms`, `tool_list_ttl_ms`, `host_visible_schema_known_to_server=false`, and whether generation changes require host refresh. The server deliberately keeps `tools.listChanged=false`: the native request-scoped transport has no persistent subscription registry through which it could honestly deliver that notification. If a host ignores the zero TTL or does not re-fetch after activation, that remaining cache is an external-host boundary and release verification must report it rather than treating backend convergence as host-visible success.

**The independent code review also found event-loop blocking and one publication race in resource ownership recovery.** The obvious synchronous host/Darwin/process-parent probes were removable dead APIs, but deeper tracing found `pruneAndReadLeases`, waiter pruning, and staging recovery could still reach synchronous process-start identity checks while the machine-user resource transaction lock was held. With multiple retained owners that could serialize several `ps` children inside the daemon event loop and extend admission contention. Beta.108 uses a one-second cached async process-start snapshot outside the transaction lock; lock-held pruning performs only `kill(0)` and in-memory generation comparison. Missing snapshot evidence fails closed by retaining a numerically live owner, while an observed start-time mismatch proves PID reuse and remains reclaimable before process-group liveness can preserve the lease. Explicit lease release performs one fresh async per-PID start-time check so an intentionally stale generation is not hidden by the short cache. Current lease/waiter staging recovery consumes the same lock-external snapshot, and the beta.104 legacy transaction-owner migration branch now awaits `inspectProcessInstanceAsync` before destructive recovery. The current owner-state writer publishes complete bytes with the shared staging-file/hard-link primitive; another process can therefore observe a very short `nlink=2` interval between `link(staging,target)` and staging unlink. The transaction reader now uses the same bounded transient-multiple-link retry already used by other owner-state readers: it does not parse, reclaim, or delete multiply-linked state during that interval, while a persistent multiply-linked lock still fails closed after the bounded retry. The anti-contention changes do not weaken exact-file/token/inode revalidation or persisted ownership authority.

**Privacy and security consequence.** The new process-start snapshot is local in-memory scheduler evidence only; it is not added to public job/status results, logs, or persisted resource records. Hosted schema-generation/deadline fields contain only versioned contract metadata. The review retains the beta.104 legacy transaction reader because immediately newer persisted state can still contain an untouched old generation even after the writer changed; removing that reader without an eager migration would turn reachable state into a future availability fault. Its liveness check is async instead of deleting the migration boundary prematurely. Regression tests require live-owner fail-closed behavior, positive PID-reuse reclamation, isolated process-group generation precedence, snapshot coalescing, async-only resource probe transport, current staging snapshot use, async legacy owner recovery, zero discovery/tool-list TTL, generation-tagged descriptions, uncached result-generation metadata, and explicit non-observability of the external host turn deadline.

## 2026-08-20 hosted autonomous follow-up correction

**Beta.104 fixed a real polling amplifier but over-constrained the orchestration contract.** The August 19 incident showed that one hosted assistant response could spend 25 minutes issuing 68 `read_process` and 91 `read_job` calls instead of settling a visible response. Beta.104 correctly capped relay-origin blocking `read_process` waits at one second and added a fifteen-second would-block cooldown, but it also made a stronger policy change: tool descriptions, shared server instructions, and relay result metadata required every active job/session read to hand the hosted turn back after the first checkpoint. That stronger rule is not required for durable execution safety and prevents an agent from autonomously following a legitimate long-running task to completion even when the host still has response/execution budget.

**The correction separates pacing from forced user interaction.** Durable process acceptance remains principal-bound, idempotency-keyed, and independent of the initiating MCP response. Relay-origin `read_job` now reports active work as `status_polling_mode=bounded_followup` with `host_turn_handoff_recommended=false`; terminal work reports `terminal`, and `list_jobs` remains an `inventory` surface rather than a polling substitute. Relay-origin live process-session reads report `status_polling_mode=paced_followup` with `host_turn_handoff_recommended=false`; terminal reads report `terminal`. Worker descriptions and shared server guidance explicitly allow bounded same-response `read_job`/`read_process` follow-up when the current task needs terminal state or more output, while requiring callers to stop before exhausting the host response/execution budget rather than after an arbitrary first read.

**The anti-amplification controls remain in enforcement.** Remote blocking `read_process.wait_ms` is still capped at one second, a live session that consumed a blocking read still enters the fixed fifteen-second would-block cooldown, and stale cached schemas are still rejected before daemon dispatch with guidance to respect `next_blocking_poll_after_ms`. Non-interactive work still routes to durable `run_process`/`exec_command`/`run_local_command` or `start_job`; repeated `list_jobs`, `server_info`, and `diagnose_runtime` calls remain invalid substitutes for following one known background operation. The regression target is therefore the forced hosted-turn handoff itself, not the bounded wait/cooldown or durable ownership mechanisms that addressed the original polling storm.

## 2026-08-20 beta.105 resource transaction lock writer migration

**The beta.60-era directory writer outlived the rolling condition that originally justified it.** The current upgrade contract supports the immediately preceding published release plus a bounded exact live-candidate transition; beta.104 is therefore the relevant predecessor for beta.105, not beta.60. Nevertheless `resource-transaction-lock.mjs` still created every new claim as `transaction.lock/owner.json`, with a comment saying the shape was retained while beta.60 could still be the live daemon. That implementation remained safe under its extensive revalidation rules, but it kept a deliberately temporary directory-quarantine restore exception and an incomplete-owner publication state in the active writer even though the generic complete-before-visible owner-state file primitive already exists.

**beta.105 changes the writer without breaking the beta.104 handoff.** New claims use `createExclusiveFileSync()` to publish one owner-only `transaction.lock` regular file containing the process-generation owner record. Release and stale-file reclamation use the shared generation/token-checked removal primitive. The previous directory reader remains: if a beta.104 daemon or durable runner still owns `transaction.lock/owner.json`, beta.105 waits; if a crashed legacy generation is provably stale, the existing bounded incomplete-owner/staging/revalidation logic recovers it before beta.105 publishes the file. The other direction already works in beta.104 because its lock reader accepts the same owner-state regular-file record. Both versions therefore contend on the identical final pathname while only beta.105 stops creating new directory generations.

**Security consequence and remaining transition.** The ordinary beta.105 writer no longer exposes the mkdir-before-owner publication window and no longer needs the legacy directory quarantine/restore path to release its own claims. The same-user replacement race documented for restoring a quarantined directory is therefore confined to consuming a beta.104 legacy generation during the bounded rolling transition; it is not part of the beta.105 current writer. Regressions keep the old directory cases until that reader can be removed, and current-wire tests require a regular file with the stable `resource-coordinator` purpose, ownership token, and process identity. Because source, tests, documentation, Worker identity, and browser-extension identity change, beta.104 acceptance cannot authorize beta.105 activation or publication.

## 2026-08-19 beta.104 hosted-turn polling continuity correction

**Current live evidence falsifies another sleep/relay-only diagnosis.** During the newly reported no-response interval, the beta.103 launchd daemon remained the same live service process, macOS still held the daemon's `PreventUserIdleSystemSleep` assertion, and the contemporaneous daemon warning log did not record a relay outage. The only retained August 19 relay warning preceded the incident and recovered after about 26 seconds. The local security-audit chain is more discriminating: from `2026-08-19T02:20Z` through `02:45Z` it records 497 settled Machine Bridge calls, with no individual request hanging for minutes. It does record 68 `read_process` calls, many consuming exactly the old 5,000 ms hosted wait, plus 91 `read_job` calls. The largest gap between audit settlements in that interval was under twenty seconds. Therefore the user-visible “no response” state was not one unresolved MCP request; the server contract allowed a hosted assistant turn to be monopolized by repeated status polling, with blocking process reads providing the strongest per-call delay and rapid managed-job reads keeping the foreground turn attached to work that was already durable.

**The causal amplifier existed in both wording and enforcement.** Beta.91 deliberately reduced remote `read_process` from 30 seconds to five seconds, but its current Worker description still said “poll again.” Process sessions were also recommended broadly for long work even though remote `run_process` had since become durable-first. A model following that contract could start a non-interactive process and repeatedly spend five seconds in `read_process` until exit. Each poll settled correctly, so transport deadlines and SSE heartbeats did not fire as errors; the defect was composition across many individually valid calls. Beta.104 changes the remote contract rather than merely shortening another global timeout. Hosted `read_process.wait_ms` is capped at one second in the Worker schema and independently clamped in the local relay-origin handler. After one remote read actually blocks while the child remains running, that session enters a fixed fifteen-second blocking-poll cooldown; another would-block read during the cooldown returns immediately. Every relay-origin result is explicitly a `status_polling_mode=checkpoint`; `host_turn_handoff_recommended` is true for any still-running session, while `blocking_poll_throttled` and `next_blocking_poll_after_ms` describe only the blocking-wait cooldown. Tool guidance reserves process sessions for interactive stdin/incremental-output use, says to perform at most one live-session `read_process` checkpoint in one hosted assistant response even when that checkpoint returned output, and routes non-interactive work to durable `run_process`/`read_job`. A final rolling-upgrade review found that the maximum-only stale-schema compatibility response still said “use short polling”; that path is especially relevant immediately after lowering the advertised maximum because an already-open host may retain the old five-second schema. Beta.104 gives stale `read_process` callers the same one-live-session-checkpoint/handoff/durable-work guidance, including the case where output is already available, and never suggests repeated short polling. Owner-local stdio/CLI waits retain the existing 30-second local schema and behavior.

**Durable acceptance must also end foreground waiting.** The same audit window contains 91 `read_job` calls. Those calls are individually fast and are not classified as an MCP hang, but a durable job exists specifically so its execution is independent of the initiating response stream. Repeatedly reading its status in one assistant response defeats that separation at the orchestration layer. Beta.104 therefore changes the hosted contracts for `start_job`, remote durable one-step process tools, and `read_job`: one active-job status read is a checkpoint, not a loop; if the job remains active/non-terminal (`queued`, `running`, `cleaning`, or interrupted recovery state), the caller returns the `job_id`, status, and current phase to the user and stops polling until a later user turn or explicit request. Relay-origin `read_job` results also expose `status_polling_mode=checkpoint` and `host_turn_handoff_recommended` while preserving local read shape unchanged. Independent review found one bypass: `list_jobs` exposes job lifecycle status too, so an agent could replace repeated `read_job` calls with repeated listings. Beta.104 therefore makes relay-origin `list_jobs` an inventory checkpoint under the same handoff rule; it computes handoff only from the already-authorized returned job set, while local listings retain their prior shape. Shared hosted instructions also state the general invariant that read-only status/diagnostic surfaces (`server_info`, `diagnose_runtime`, job/process status) are evidence checkpoints rather than substitute wait loops for the same background state, so changing which status tool is called cannot legitimately reintroduce same-response waiting. This metadata is principal-scoped through the existing managed-job ownership gate and contains no command, path, resource, or cross-workload scheduling identity.

**“Message send timed out” remains partly outside the server’s observable boundary.** Machine Bridge cannot observe a client message submission that fails before an MCP request reaches the Worker, so this audit does not claim that every host UI “message send timed out” event is caused or fixed inside this repository. A proposed outer-proxy first-byte heartbeat was explicitly rejected after tracing the real tool stream: `jsonRpcResponseStream()` synchronously enqueues the SSE comment `: connected` when the Durable Object response body is created, before the tool Promise settles, and `mcp-response-proxy.ts` immediately pumps that already-open upstream body. The synthetic silent-upstream fixture that motivated an extra outer heartbeat therefore did not represent the normal `tools/call` path. Beta.104 removes that speculative change. The demonstrated server-side mechanism that can keep a conversation unavailable for a new visible exchange is instead the hosted assistant turn remaining attached to repeated `read_process`/`read_job` polling after the underlying work has become session- or job-owned; the checkpoint/handoff repair closes that mechanism. A send timeout that occurs with no preceding active assistant/tool turn or before any MCP request reaches the Worker still requires host/client-side evidence.

**Independent architecture/privacy review found additional hardening opportunities beyond the original incident.** First, `process-sessions.mjs` and `managed-jobs.mjs` had both drifted to within a handful of lines of their explicit responsibility ceilings. Beta.104 moves read/wait/output orchestration to `process-session-read.mjs`, keeps the remote blocking-poll policy in `process-session-remote-poll.mjs`, and finalizes hosted process projection synchronously after the async read returns so an exit at that boundary cannot produce a settled `running=false` summary with stale handoff/cooldown metadata. It also moves hosted managed-job handoff policy into `managed-job-hosted-status.mjs` and directory scanning/authority-visible listing projection into `managed-job-listing.mjs`; neither lifecycle manager nor the ordinary managed-job result projection needs host-policy/list-formatting logic to stay under its architecture limit. The same instruction review also found the generic configurable-foreground advice “use process sessions or managed jobs for longer work” in both the shared tool catalog and the Worker projection. That is semantically wrong for browser/application operations and capable of reintroducing process-session overuse even if one projection layer is corrected. Beta.104 removes the advice at the shared source and Worker layers, regenerates the 54-tool reference, tells browser/application callers to split longer workflows into independently terminal foreground calls, limits durable process/job routing to process-based work, and keeps `start_process` interactive-I/O only. Architecture checks reject the obsolete advice in both the live catalog source and generated reference. Second, a live beta.103 WSS outage during this review recovered through the authenticated HTTPS fallback with no local event-loop stall and Green resource pressure, but an in-flight read failed with “reconnect grace expired.” Source tracing showed pending calls actually wait for `min(reconnect grace, original remaining call deadline)`; because hosted tool deadlines are shorter than the full reconnect grace, the old message could mislabel ordinary deadline exhaustion as a reconnect-grace failure. Beta.104 distinguishes those causes without pausing or enlarging the original deadline. Later, after full verification created heavy shared-host contention, `diagnose_runtime` briefly projected `resource_admission={healthy:false,error_class:execution_failed}` while the transaction-lock owner was demonstrably changing between live processes; a subsequent snapshot returned normal Red pressure with active leases/waiters. The coordinator was contended rather than corrupt. Beta.104 therefore maps bounded transaction/staging snapshot contention to retryable `unavailable`, `reason=coordinator_busy`, and `snapshot_available=false`, while leaving the admission policy, lock protocol, pressure limits, and fairness behavior unchanged. Third, ignored Wrangler-generated type files can embed the local workspace path in their generator comment when Wrangler receives an absolute output argument. They are excluded from Git and the npm publication surface, so this was not a published-secret finding, but it is unnecessary local metadata exposure if generated artifacts are copied or archived. The generator now supplies a working-directory-relative output and refuses targets outside that working directory; the package/privacy gates continue to exclude `.wrangler` artifacts. Fast verification also exposed a deterministic-time defect in device-session construction: callers could supply `now` to create a certificate, but final validation silently switched back to real wall time, so historical/synthetic clocks eventually expired at creation time. Beta.104 threads the same explicit clock through finalization, including the secure-root provider; production callers that omit `now` still use `Date.now()`. Review of runtime debug logging and the security-audit projection found no new content leak: routine tool logs remain argument-free, audit principal identifiers are keyed fingerprints, and `content_logged=false` remains the persisted audit contract.

**Deeper WSS review found three server-created continuity defects in addition to a genuine residual network-failure class.** First, the transport contract advertises a five-second protocol probe interval plus a ten-second Pong response timeout, but the old generic heartbeat monitor measured the ten seconds from the preceding inbound frame. With no inbound traffic it sent the first protocol Ping at five seconds and then terminated the socket at ten seconds total, so the nominal ten-second Pong timeout gave that probe only about five seconds to receive a response. A red regression reproduced the premature termination. Beta.104 now separates local Ping queueing from actual dispatch: the full ten-second Pong deadline starts only when the `ws` sender write callback confirms that the Ping left the local queue. Bidirectional proof that arrives while Ping dispatch is pending—protocol Pong or the explicit application `pong`—is remembered only for that dispatch token: if the local write callback completes inside the thirty-second dispatch budget, its later completion cannot arm a stale future Pong timeout. Unrelated application inbound does not satisfy that token because it proves only the Worker-to-daemon receive direction. Because data-frame compression/backpressure can legitimately delay a control frame, the local dispatch phase has a separate thirty-second bound rather than consuming the remote-response deadline; if the write callback still has not completed at that bound it is `relay_transport_send_timeout` even when unrelated inbound traffic continues, while an actually dispatched Ping with no response remains `relay_transport_timeout`. Sender `bufferedAmount` plus dispatch delay/timeout age are retained only as bounded numeric evidence to distinguish local outbound backlog from a missing remote response. Cloudflare protocol Pong handling does not require the Durable Object JavaScript handler, so Durable Object hibernation is not used as an explanation for that missing response. Second, HTTPS fallback takeover was bound only to daemon `instance_id`. A previously dispatched takeover could therefore arrive after a newer same-instance WSS had already recovered and retire that newer socket. The Worker already generated a per-WebSocket connection identity internally; beta.104 now includes that `connection_id` in the welcome contract, requires it in same-version handshake validation, signs it into fallback takeover requests, and permits HTTPS takeover only when the exact targeted ready WebSocket generation is still the sole incumbent. A Worker integration regression proves a stale takeover returns standby and leaves the newer WSS ready. Third, the daemon's root-signed session certificate has an intentional 24-hour lifetime, but the runtime previously created it once at daemon startup and reused that same expired session for every later WSS/HTTPS reconnect. An established socket could survive beyond expiry, yet the next disconnect after 24 hours made both preflight and signed fallback authentication unusable; the old reconnect loop never renewed the session and could therefore remain permanently offline. Beta.104 gives local expiry a stable `device_session_expired` code and treats it as `relay_device_session_expired` fatal continuity state rather than a retryable transport/authentication error. Runtime cleanup runs first, then installed launchd/systemd/Windows supervision restarts the nonzero daemon so the default portable root signs a fresh 24-hour session; manual daemons require operator restart, and Secure Enclave mode preserves its existing user-presence requirement for a new daemon start instead of weakening certificate lifetime. The review also corrected an observability defect in which event-loop-stall timestamps converted monotonic `performance.now()` values into wall-clock dates, and adds privacy-bounded current-attempt plus last-failed DNS/TCP/TLS/upgrade milestone timing, transport-error authenticated/ready context, bounded probe-dispatch/backlog evidence, and Worker close code/clean-close metadata so a future residual outage can be localized without logging peer close reasons, addresses, DNS answers, endpoints, or credentials. A later live beta.103 episode remained independently classified as `relay_transport_error` / `network_error`, lasted roughly 274 seconds across 16 reconnect attempts, and then recovered with a final successful WebSocket connect in roughly 1.35 seconds. The signed HTTPS fallback's final successful poll was observed within roughly 0.4 seconds of WSS readiness, while the local event loop had no stall and the OS route was carried by a VPN/TUN. That is strong evidence of a shared system-network episode rather than a WSS-only server rejection, but beta.103 both overwrote failed-attempt stage timing after recovery and cleared fallback `last_transport_error_class` on each later success, so the exact historical errno cannot be recovered honestly. Beta.104 retains the last failed connect separately, preserves fallback last-error evidence after successful polls, and carries only strict allowlisted reasons such as `connection_timeout`, `network_unreachable`, bounded DNS/TLS classes, or `unknown`; until candidate evidence records one of those reasons, the residual episode is not assigned more narrowly to DNS, TCP, TLS, a particular VPN node, Cloudflare, or another upstream component.

**The remaining WSS instability had a second deterministic amplifier, not a second proven physical root cause.** A fresh review of the August 19 beta.103 warning stream found repeated ready-channel `relay_transport_timeout` episodes after about 12–15 seconds without inbound transport proof, plus successful later WSS connection attempts that took about 13.8 and 14.4 seconds and a separate roughly 4.5-minute/16-attempt `network_error` recovery episode. The daemon's event-loop diagnostics did not show a corresponding local scheduling stall. This establishes two facts without overclaiming a third: the persistent WSS path can genuinely become impaired, and beta.103's ten-second single-strike Pong policy plus fifteen-second connect ceiling made that impairment more disruptive; the available application evidence still does not identify which VPN/TUN implementation, route, edge, ISP, DNS/TCP/TLS component, or upstream device initiated the impairment. Beta.104 therefore does not relabel a missing Pong as healthy. Instead, after a dispatched Ping reaches its full ten-second response deadline, a ready WSS enters a separate fifteen-second application-confirmation state. The daemon emits one JSON heartbeat through the same socket and starts the signed HTTPS path only in standby. A subsequent protocol Pong or explicit JSON application `pong` clears suspicion, stops standby prewarm, and records bounded `relay.transport.recovered` timing; ordinary tool/control inbound does not, because receive-side traffic alone cannot prove the daemon-to-Worker direction. Only a second-stage miss records `relay.transport.confirmation_failed` and permits hard termination as `relay_transport_timeout`. If WSS closes first, the standby fallback is promoted to the existing exact-generation takeover; any old in-flight standby request is aborted so its seven-second request deadline cannot delay takeover. WSS connect attempts receive a thirty-second outer budget while fallback activation remains independent. The same review found and closed two state/observability traps: application heartbeat/confirmation is now gated on fully verified readiness rather than merely `hello_ack` authentication because the Worker probing state accepts only the readiness-probe result, and HTTPS `last_ready_at` no longer aliases every successful standby request. Fallback diagnostics now expose separate `last_success_at`, true `last_ready_at`, and bridge-level `https_fallback_warming`. A Ping write callback that completes inside the dispatch budget can consume only proof bound to that same round; if the callback itself remains stuck for thirty seconds it is a send failure regardless of unrelated inbound traffic. The same adversarial review found two additional generation/recovery bugs in the beta.104 candidate and fixed them before release: a delayed application-send callback from a superseded WSS previously lacked a socket-generation fence and could record an error against, or reset confirmation state belonging to, the replacement connection; and the signed HTTPS standby/failure loop carried a `consecutiveFailures` counter that did not affect scheduling, so successful standby prewarm and fast request/protocol failures could retry near the 750 ms hard floor. Send completions are now exact-generation fenced, confirmation callbacks are token-owned, standby prewarm uses a five-second cadence, and request/session/protocol failures use bounded one/two/four/five-second backoff while exact-generation takeover can preempt a later timer without bypassing the hard minimum request interval. These changes target the proven product amplification and leave only the separately observed long `network_error` episode's external physical trigger unresolved until candidate/live telemetry classifies DNS/TCP/TLS/system-route evidence more narrowly.

**Regression and release boundary.** Test-first changes bind the demonstrated failure surfaces: process-session coverage requires a zero-wait relay checkpoint not to arm the blocking cooldown, a stale/oversized blocking read to clamp to one second, a repeated would-block read to return immediately with precisely named blocking-only cooldown/handoff metadata, natural exit during a checkpoint to clear handoff/cooldown, `wait_for_exit` to retain the one-second clamp even when output already exists, the post-await completion step to use the final session state rather than a stale pre-exit snapshot, and a microtask-scheduled cancellation between helper resolution and manager continuation to be observed by the manager's post-await cancellation re-check; managed-job coverage requires active relay-origin `read_job` and visible-active `list_jobs` projections to carry checkpoint/handoff metadata while local reads/listings retain their prior shape; Worker runtime coverage requires the one-second schema/budget, global/process/job anti-loop descriptions, unambiguous `server_info.tool_delivery` blocking-poll field names, and the distinction between original-deadline and true reconnect-grace expiry; MCP-controller coverage requires a cached pre-beta.104 five-second `read_process` request to fail before dispatch with one-live-session-checkpoint/durable-work guidance instead of “short polling”; Worker-type generation coverage requires Wrangler to receive a relative output path and rejects escape outside its working directory; package coverage requires the extracted runtime modules to ship while ignored `.wrangler` artifacts remain excluded; and the architecture release contract rejects reintroduction of obsolete polling guidance or ambiguous field names. The proposed extra outer SSE heartbeat and its synthetic regression were removed after tracing proved the real `tools/call` stream already emits `: connected` before tool settlement. These are packaged runtime/Worker/documentation changes, so beta.103 verification and any acceptance evidence cannot authorize beta.104. Persistent activation still requires explicit owner authorization after a fresh frozen verification/candidate cycle.

## 2026-08-18 beta.103 idle-sleep continuity correction

**Live beta.102 disproved the five-minute post-activity lease.** After beta.102 had passed candidate activation, live acceptance, cross-platform PR checks, and merge, owner use still produced a severe whole-control-plane interruption. The retained relay state showed several recovered transport episodes, but the longest contemporaneous event was not an awake network black hole: macOS power records show the final `PreventUserIdleSystemSleep` assertion disappearing and ordinary `Idle Sleep` beginning about five seconds later for roughly 632 seconds. The daemon recorded an aligned roughly 626-second event-loop pause and long inbound silence while launchd retained one continuously running service process with no termination. This proves the host was suspended rather than the daemon crashing. Because both preferred WSS and signed HTTPS fallback execute in that suspended userspace, transport redundancy cannot recover the machine while it is asleep.

**Causal defect is the inactivity-policy boundary, not the assertion primitive.** Beta.101 deliberately avoided permanently caffeinating the login daemon and chose a fixed five-minute grace after the last authorized relay activity. The reference-counting state machine itself behaved as designed: authorized/concurrent calls acquired one assertion, the last settlement armed the grace timer, and later activity would cancel that timer. The live failure demonstrates that five minutes is not long enough to model a real multi-turn remote workflow: model reasoning, user-visible waiting, and host scheduling can create a legitimate gap longer than five minutes between Machine Bridge calls. Once the timer released, the operating system was free to enter Idle Sleep immediately, making the bridge unreachable until physical wake. The earlier statement that the five-minute policy was sufficient for ordinary remote-work continuity is therefore falsified.

**Beta.103 uses a bounded thirty-minute rolling lease.** The daemon still acquires the macOS assertion only after policy/account/operation authorization and schema validation; rejected traffic and heartbeat traffic cannot keep the host awake. Concurrent authorized handlers remain reference-counted. After the last daemon-side activity settles, the assertion remains for thirty minutes; any later authorized activity cancels pending release and begins a fresh full thirty-minute inactivity window only after the new activity settles. Remote process sessions still extend ownership through child settlement, and remote account managed-job runners retain their independent runner-lifetime assertion. This is intentionally not relay-lifetime ownership: an idle but connected daemon eventually releases the assertion, avoiding permanent automatic-sleep suppression. Explicit sleep and lid-close remain unrecoverable host-power boundaries.

**Regression and release consequence.** A deterministic runtime regression first required the default constant and default constructor timer to retain the assertion for thirty minutes; it failed against beta.102's five-minute implementation, then passed after the product constant changed. The architecture release contract now requires the same thirty-minute source policy and current architecture/operations documentation. README, testing, and logging guidance describe the rolling renewal semantics and explicitly avoid heartbeat-based renewal. Because runtime source and shipped documentation change npm-package bytes, beta.102 acceptance cannot authorize beta.103. No beta.102 tag, GitHub Prerelease, npm publication, or formal published-prerelease soak had begun, so the correct recovery is a new beta.103 candidate and acceptance cycle rather than publishing or soaking the known-defective beta.102 bytes.

## 2026-08-18 beta.102 end-to-end interruption re-audit

**Live beta.101 falsified “fast detection equals recovery.”** A normal daemon-backed read reproduced the user-visible interruption while Worker-local `server_info` remained available. The Worker had no authenticated, ready, probing, or candidate daemon channel and the call exhausted the bounded recovery wait. After the daemon returned, retained evidence showed a 323,839 ms outage with 13 reconnect attempts ending in `relay_connect_timeout` and 9,830 ms of inbound silence before the previous ready WSS closed. launchd still reported the same PID, `runs=1`, and no termination signal; the matching interval contained no macOS Sleep/Wake event. A second live beta.101 interruption later recovered after about 10.6 seconds without a daemon restart. This proves the remaining product defect was not Computer Use, launchd crash, or idle sleep: beta.101 detected a broken WSS promptly but still depended on successful WSS reconnection as its only remote-daemon recovery transport. The host default route was carried by a tunnel-like interface during investigation, but available evidence does not identify a particular VPN/TUN product, node, Wi-Fi component, Cloudflare edge, ISP, or upstream device as the physical trigger.

**Independent signed HTTPS transport removes WSS as a single recovery point.** Beta.102 keeps WSS preferred but adds one root-certified ephemeral P-256 daemon HTTP channel using the same daemon instance and authority. Every fallback request signs the fixed Worker origin/route/server/version, a bounded one-time timestamped nonce, and the exact body SHA-256. Requests, responses, queues, sequence numbers, and retry cadence are bounded; request timeout is seven seconds, verified-channel liveness is twelve seconds, ordinary poll cadence is one second, minimum request-start interval is 750 ms, and the shared new-call recovery ceiling is fifteen seconds. Bidirectional contiguous sequence/ACK state makes a lost HTTP response retransmit the same transport envelope; duplicates are discarded before business handling and gaps fail closed. A response from an old/reset HTTP session cannot commit its sequence into the replacement session. The first authenticated request enters probing directly; no obsolete challenge state or public MCP recovery/session model is introduced.

**Asymmetric black holes require explicit same-instance takeover.** A local transport watchdog can know WSS is dead while the Worker has not received the close and still considers that socket ready. The first fallback draft therefore failed precisely where it was most needed: Worker HTTP admission returned standby whenever any ready WSS existed. The corrected protocol marks takeover only after an established active WSS fails locally. The Worker verifies the signed request, new-session preconditions, and exact daemon instance before retiring a Worker-side zombie WSS; malformed, stale, or different-instance requests cannot preempt the incumbent. In-flight pending ownership is rebound without replaying the business call. A real Wrangler regression holds an in-flight call on WSS, performs same-instance HTTPS takeover while the old WSS is still Worker-ready, settles the original call through HTTPS, then verifies a newly ready WSS reclaims primary ownership. A separate regression proves an invalid signed takeover receives a bounded rejection while the healthy WSS remains ready.

**Daemon-proven non-delivery now recovers transparently without weakening mutation safety.** Beta.101 added `resume_calls_ack.missing_ids`, but used that proof only to return retryable `unavailable`. Beta.102 tightens handshake order first: the daemon reconciles Worker `resume_calls`, processes `ready_ack`, commits replacement readiness, emits sequenced `https_ready` where applicable, and only then sends `resume_calls_ack`. A missing ID is generated from the union of local active calls and retained unacknowledged terminal results, so it proves the same daemon never acquired execution ownership for that call and that the replacement channel is ready. Only that state can trigger transport redelivery. The Worker retains the original pending record and retransmits the same call ID, tool arguments, and authority with a timeout reduced to the original remaining execution budget; it refuses the optimization when less than one second remains or the ready channel cannot accept the envelope. Request cancellation still removes pending ownership. Active calls, retained results, a different daemon instance, post-dispatch unknown outcomes, and any mutation that may have executed are never automatically replayed. Repeated transport recovery therefore cannot convert uncertainty into duplicate side effects.

**Additional race and observability closure.** HTTP `tool_call` handling now reaches the local ownership path before an asynchronous control-handler yield can let the transport ACK outrun `activeRelayCalls`. Inbound sequence commit also revalidates the same authenticated HTTP session after handler settlement so an old response cannot poison a freshly reset session. HTTPS global readiness clears the bridge-level outage projection while retaining WSS background reconnect diagnostics separately; the Worker reports the correct twelve-second fallback liveness rather than the ninety-second WSS fallback. Browser broker/extension liveness independently negotiates matching Ping/Pong sequencing and reconnects a half-open local extension socket; ambiguous trusted input quarantines that extension generation and never authorizes mutation replay. Process-session retention, Computer Use end-to-end deadlines, remote idle-sleep ownership, managed-job restart/finally behavior, resource admission, launchd handoff, release activation, and privacy/logging paths were re-reviewed independently and retained their existing safety boundaries. Finally, a live all-channel outage no longer erases the previous transport evidence: `server_info.daemon.previous_connection` retains only one in-memory last-verified transport/timestamp/sanitized-relay snapshot, excluding policy, tool membership, account/client identity, daemon instance/connection identity, call IDs, arguments, and results, and never using that snapshot as authority or routing state.

**Release boundary.** These are packaged beta.102 bytes. No beta.101 candidate, activation, OAuth-canary, or acceptance evidence authorizes them. The final tree must pass fresh architecture/lint/type checks, browser and Computer Use regressions, security/privacy checks, repeated relay fault loops, frozen `check:fast`, frozen `check:full`, exact candidate creation, and install-only verification. Persistent beta.102 activation still requires a new explicit owner authorization; only the activated package's OAuth canary and live WSS/HTTPS/browser/Computer Use acceptance can support final release acceptance.

## 2026-08-18 beta.101 interruption root-cause and independent hardening review

**Two interruption mechanisms, not one.** Live beta.100 observability had already separated `computer_act` failures from successful low-level application operations: the compound tool accumulated timeout/conflict failures while direct application mutation completed. Deterministic timing instrumentation then showed Chrome/application capture and mutation stages completing individually inside their own local budgets, exposing the composition defect instead of an Accessibility `set_value` hang. The hosted projection defaulted configurable foreground work to twenty seconds while local Computer Use defaulted to thirty, and the orchestration could hand nearly that full timeout to preflight, dispatch, and post-capture sequentially. Beta.101 gives `computer_act` one monotonic deadline across preflight, its one mutation attempt, verification, and post-observation; `computer_observe` likewise shares one deadline across screenshot, Accessibility inspection, and fallback window revalidation. Verification is bounded by the smaller of its own window and the remaining operation budget. A preflight deadline error is computed outside broad stale/unavailable catches so a definite no-side-effect timeout cannot be misclassified. The review additionally caught two edge conditions after the first repair: a final mutation-budget check originally happened after snapshot claim, so a definite pre-dispatch timeout could consume one-shot mutation authority without side effects; that check now precedes claim. The millisecond remainder also used `ceil` when projected into integer child seconds, which could grant an internal stage nearly one second beyond the actual end-to-end remainder; projection now uses `floor`. A proposed additional 500 ms local settlement reserve was removed during the same review because hosted calls already have a separate five-second Worker reply-settlement margin outside daemon execution, and double-charging that headroom unnecessarily shortened valid first-stage and verification budgets. Worker defaults for only the two compound Computer Use tools move to thirty seconds; the shared explicit remote maximum remains forty-five seconds and ordinary configurable foreground defaults remain twenty.

**Whole-daemon outage falsified as a crash.** When the local control plane later became unreachable even for a read-only file call, Worker `server_info` still answered but showed no ready daemon. After the service returned, launchd evidence showed the same daemon PID, `runs=1`, and no process restart. `pmset -g log` supplied the missing causal timeline: the Mac entered Sleep at 06:50:08 for about 509 seconds, Maintenance Sleep at 06:58:42 for about 980 seconds, and Sleep again at 07:15:04 for about 741 seconds, returning to full wake near 07:27:30. Daemon records line up with those intervals: an event-loop stall of about 505 seconds on the first wake, a relay outage recorded as 979/981 seconds across the second sleep, and an event-loop stall of about 718 seconds on the final wake. The apparent sixteen-minute `relay_heartbeat_timeout` therefore occurred while the host was suspended, not while the daemon was awake and network-stalled. This evidence does not support a crash, launchd restart, or an awake sixteen-minute Cloudflare outage.

**Awake interruption root cause and amplification.** A later incident supplied the missing non-sleep case. Around 09:12:30 local time macOS unified network state reported route/reachability recovery and primary connectivity up while targeted `pmset` evidence showed no Sleep/Wake transition. The launchd service still had the same PID, one lifetime run, and no exit. The ready relay did not produce its close `1006` / `connection_interrupted` transition until about 09:13:39 and was ready again at about 09:13:41. The roughly two-second close-to-ready outage therefore understated the user-visible interruption: beta.100 had no fast transport-only probe, so its twenty-five-second application heartbeat and seventy-five-second local silence decision (with a ninety-second Worker fallback) could leave an OPEN-but-black-holed WebSocket eligible for dispatch far longer than the ordinary twenty-second and compound Computer Use thirty-second hosted budgets. A deterministic regression first failed because no protocol probe existed at five seconds. Beta.101 adds `RelayLiveness`: protocol-level WebSocket Ping every five seconds with ten seconds without inbound proof before `relay_transport_timeout`, while preserving the separate twenty-five-second JSON application heartbeat and its seventy-five-second application-silence decision as `relay_heartbeat_timeout`; the Worker retains its wider ninety-second fallback. Protocol Pong therefore cannot mask a Worker application path that has stopped replying. For a scheduling-responsive daemon, the executable contract guards the full transport-detection horizon—one probe interval plus the silence threshold, fifteen seconds—below the ordinary twenty-second hosted execution default, leaving reconnect/result-settlement headroom instead of comparing only the timeout value. A diagnosed local event-loop stall follows the separate recovery-grace path and can intentionally extend that interval without being misclassified as remote transport silence. Diagnostics now preserve `previous_ready_inbound_silence_ms` across reconnect so operators can distinguish the silent pre-close interval from `outage_duration_ms`, which measures only close through verified readiness. This evidence establishes the Machine Bridge amplification defect; it does not identify which VPN/TUN, Wi-Fi, intermediary, edge, or upstream component caused the underlying network-path transition.

**Post-reconnect lost-dispatch settlement.** The transport watchdog repair exposed one more delivery-state hole that is independent of reconnect speed. The Worker registers a pending call before writing `tool_call` to the current ready socket. On a half-open transport, `WebSocket.send()` can return successfully even though the frame never reaches the local dispatcher. Before beta.101, a same-instance reconnect rebound that Worker pending record and sent the one-way `resume_calls` list, but the daemon only cancelled local work that the Worker no longer wanted; it did not tell the Worker which requested resume IDs it had never owned. Such a call could therefore remain pending until its original deadline even after the relay itself had recovered. The safety boundary for repairing this without replay is explicit: every relay call that reaches local dispatch enters `activeRelayCalls` before execution, and a completed result enters the bounded `pendingResults` ledger before delivery and remains there until Worker acknowledgement. Reconciliation now snapshots the union of those two ownership sets before cancelling or discarding anything. A Worker-resumed ID absent from both is therefore daemon-proven non-delivery, not an ambiguous mutation outcome. The daemon returns only those IDs in pre-ready `resume_calls_ack.missing_ids` on the same authenticated relay generation; failure to send that acknowledgement interrupts the generation instead of claiming successful reconciliation. The Worker strictly validates at most thirty-two unique bounded call IDs, rejects only pending records currently owned by that replacement socket, and returns retryable `unavailable` with `side_effects_started=false` and `reason=daemon_call_not_received_after_reconnect`. Active calls and retained terminal results continue under their existing ownership and are never replayed automatically. Local and Worker logs expose only aggregate missing-call counts; raw resume IDs, tool names, arguments, account identity, and results remain excluded.

**Bounded runtime mitigation.** Verification already had a whole-run macOS `caffeinate -i` wrapper, but normal remote daemon workflows had no equivalent. Permanently caffeinating a login daemon would be an unacceptable power-management change, so beta.101 separates the fixed macOS primitive from its owners. `macos-idle-sleep-assertion.mjs` is the only runtime adapter that starts `/usr/bin/caffeinate -i -w <owner-pid>` with `shell: false`, ignored stdio, and process-lifetime binding. The daemon-side `remote-activity-idle-sleep-guard.mjs` composes it only after policy/account/operation authorization and argument validation, so rejected or malformed relay traffic cannot keep the host awake. Authorized handlers hold reference-counted activity for their full execution lifetime: concurrent calls share the assertion, no inactivity timer runs while any handler is active, the fixed five-minute grace begins only after the last daemon-side activity settles, a new activity invalidates stale release timers, and surplus end calls are idempotent rather than extending the grace. A remote process session acquires an additional logical hold only after resource admission succeeds and releases it on child settlement or startup failure, so a child that outlives `start_process` is not reduced to the handler grace. Remote account managed-job runners cannot depend on daemon ownership because they intentionally survive relay/service replacement; after confirming the runner claim and validating persisted account ownership, each remote runner acquires its own runner-PID-bound assertion and retains it across recovery handoff, resource admission, steps, `finally_steps`, and terminal persistence before top-level `finally` releases it. Local managed jobs do not acquire this remote-continuity assertion. Runtime shutdown terminates process sessions before releasing the daemon assertion. The five-minute relay grace is fixed rather than exposed through a shell environment variable because the launchd service definition persists only its controlled `PATH` and offers no durable configuration channel for arbitrary shell overrides. Assertion/spawn/timer/logging failures remain fail-open for tool/job settlement and expose only a coarse error class; owner `diagnose_runtime` projects only daemon-side supported/enabled/active/grace/error-class fields and does not enumerate sessions or jobs. These mechanisms prevent ordinary Idle Sleep during owned remote work but intentionally do not promise to override explicit sleep or lid-close behavior.

**Independent architecture/documentation/privacy review.** A fresh current-state pass found two stale pieces of guidance in addition to the timeout implementation defect: README still summarized remote foreground process/shell/browser/application work as a blanket sixty-second daemon envelope even though remote process one-shots have been durable 10-second-acceptance/1–600-second-execution jobs since beta.93, and `docs/COMPUTER_USE.md` still claimed the first application verification capture could use the normal full action timeout. Both are corrected and now guarded. The same review challenged the first sleep-guard draft rather than accepting a green unit test: lease renewal was moved after authorization/schema validation to prevent rejected traffic from becoming a power-resource DoS; the inactivity timer was moved from handler start to the last concurrent handler's settlement; surplus end calls became idempotent; logging failures became fail-open; unexpected assertion-child exit gained coarse observability; an implicit `spawn` shell default became explicit `shell: false`; credential/path-shaped synthetic test strings were replaced with neutral markers; and a proposed environment grace override was removed after service tracing showed launchd does not persist arbitrary shell environment as configuration. The review then found that handler-only ownership still left long process sessions and detached jobs vulnerable after the five-minute grace, producing the separate session/job lifetime ownership described above. Computer Use/tool metadata explicitly describes one end-to-end deadline, architecture/operations/testing distinguish the two thirty-second compound defaults from ordinary twenty-second foreground defaults, and logging/diagnostics document power-management state without recording commands, tool names, paths, PIDs, arguments, results, session identities, or job identities. Every runtime assertion launch uses the same fixed absolute executable and flags with a numeric owner PID and never invokes a shell. Existing privacy/history checks remain the authoritative repository-wide sensitive-data gates.

**Verification and release boundary.** Focused Computer Use and Worker timeout tests pass after the deadline repair; runtime infrastructure and runtime self-tests pass after the sleep-guard integration. The remaining architecture, privacy, generated-doc, fast/full, candidate, install-only, live-activation/canary, browser-extension, and acceptance gates must be regenerated from the final frozen beta.101 tree. Beta.100 was already live-activated and its activated-package canary had passed, but browser extension acceptance remained incomplete; packaged beta.101 changes make all beta.100 candidate/acceptance evidence stale for release authorization.

## 2026-08-17 beta.100 independent architecture/privacy review

**Starting evidence and release boundary.** The review began from the beta.99 working tree after its local targeted checks, 103-task fast plan, 130-task full plan, candidate preparation, and disposable install-only preflight had succeeded. Beta.99 had never been live-activated or accepted. The owner runtime remained the previously activated beta.98 service. The review therefore treated beta.99's green verification as a baseline to challenge, not as evidence that the current design or documentation was complete. Any packaged change found here invalidates the beta.99 receipt/candidate and requires a new prerelease rather than silently reusing prior evidence.

**Obsolete internal process-call shape.** `runtime-tool-handlers.mjs` dispatches public `exec_command` as one validated argument record, but `runtime-process-routing.mjs` and `LocalRuntime.execCommand()` still supported an older `(commandString, timeout, context)` overload. Repository search found no production consumer of that shape: only runtime-self/full-access fixtures called it. The overload duplicated timeout/context interpretation and maintained a second branch beneath an otherwise record-shaped MCP contract. Beta.100 removes it, updates the fixtures to `{ command, timeout_seconds }`, and adds an architecture guard rejecting `legacyCall`/`timeoutOrContext` reintroduction. This is internal compatibility cleanup; the public MCP schema, shell limits, resource admission, and durable relay-origin process routing are unchanged.

**Audit shutdown and catch-hygiene edge cases.** `SecurityAuditLog.close()` previously computed its deadline with `Math.max(1, Number(timeoutMs) || CLOSE_TIMEOUT_MS)`. A direct control experiment passed `Infinity` through a non-settling synthetic Worker; Node emitted `TimeoutOverflowWarning: Infinity does not fit into a 32-bit signed integer` and reported that the timer duration was set to 1. The fake Worker did not keep the event loop alive and the deliberately unref'ed timer therefore did not provide a terminal close result, so this audit does not claim a measured one-millisecond production settlement. The causal defect is nevertheless explicit: an invalid internal delay could reach `setTimeout` and be inverted into Node's overflow behavior; the same Node timer boundary also rejects oversized finite delays. Beta.100 normalizes non-finite/uncoercible/non-positive delays to the existing five-second close default and caps every positive override at that same five-second shutdown window before timer creation. Regression coverage checks both `Infinity` and `Number.MAX_SAFE_INTEGER` and keeps the close pending until a synthetic worker acknowledgement instead of allowing timer overflow to decide settlement. The same review found that repository hygiene rejected empty `.catch(() => {})` but not optional-chained `.catch?.(() => {})`; `security-audit-log.mjs` contained exactly one such unexplained best-effort termination catch. The production catch now documents why termination cannot change the already-failed audit settlement, and the hygiene detector plus self-checks cover both syntaxes.

**Package-impact semantics and historical wording.** Direct inspection of the beta.99 tarball showed no `package/tests/**` entries. The current `package.json.files` manifest likewise excludes `tests`, and `release-impact-check.mjs` computes package relevance from that manifest plus package/lock metadata. This falsifies the current beta.98 shorthand that described its regression-test change itself as packaged bytes. The release policy was conservative rather than unsafe—beta.98 also changed real packaged source/version/documentation—but the wording could mislead future maintainers into treating every test-only change as a package mutation. An attempted check of older beta.74–86 package manifests could not establish their historical tarball policy because those version tags are not retained in the current local repository, so older incident wording is deliberately left untouched rather than inferred from today's manifest. Beta.100 corrects the current beta.98 statement, documents that tests are verification evidence rather than tarball entries, adds an explicit release-impact regression proving a `tests/**`-only change does not require an npm bump, and adds architecture guards so silently adding `tests` to `package.json.files` requires a deliberate policy/documentation update.

**Verification concurrency documentation.** `docs/TESTING.md` said the parallel-safe fast worker pool used four workers by default. Source tracing shows the actual default is `Math.max(1, Math.min(4, availableParallelism()))`; the explicit environment override remains 1–16. This matches beta.99 runs that reported two or three available workers and means the prose had promoted a cap into a fixed default. Beta.100 documents the dynamic `availableParallelism()` bound and protects the source/doc relation in the release-contract suite.

**Redundancy and maintainability.** Agent-context, full-access, and runtime-self tests carried byte-for-byte identical synthetic healthy-host functions. They now import one test fixture so deterministic resource assumptions have one source. The production-size review also confirmed several intentionally broad modules remain close to executable ceilings (`runtime.mjs`, `relay-connection.mjs`, `computer-use.mjs`, browser operations/page automation, and app automation). No ceiling was raised and no Strategy/archive refactor was introduced: there is no demonstrated failure that justifies a wide rewrite. The practical maintenance constraint is that the next feature on those surfaces should extract a coherent owned lifecycle before adding more responsibility rather than increasing the cap.

**Release/JSON and privacy review with falsified branches.** Because beta.96 failed after an inner persistent activation produced non-JSON stdout, the review re-traced the current persistent `activate --json` path instead of assuming the beta.97 Wrangler fix closed every equivalent channel. The installed CLI invocation remains captured by the outer wrapper; JSON activation routes service logging through quiet/stderr-only loggers; managed-job authentication cannot start interactive Wrangler login; inner JSON Worker deployment is non-interactive. The remaining inherited-stdio execution is the separate non-persistent foreground-candidate path and does not feed the parsed JSON child. No second stdout-contamination mechanism was found, so no speculative release rewrite was retained. A targeted sensitive-metadata scan across source/scripts/extension/docs also found no concrete owner home path, account identifier, real `workers.dev` endpoint, private-key header, bearer header, or `client_secret`; the repository privacy/history gates still remain authoritative and are rerun on the final frozen tree.

**Recurrent message-timeout symptom and resource-admission evidence.** During this review the owner again observed the client message “send timed out; retry.” The live beta.98 control plane did not support a blanket relay/tool-timeout explanation: `server_info` reported a ready authenticated daemon with no pending calls, Worker observability reported zero timed-out tool calls, and the recent close-1006 relay interruption had recovered after roughly 4.5 seconds. `diagnose_runtime` did, however, show a distinct machine-user scheduling condition: sampled resource pressure was Green while one heavy lease remained active, nine roots were queued, six had aged into protected rank, and the oldest waiter was already about twenty-one minutes old. Reading a durable `pgrep` job later provided stronger terminal evidence: it failed after the complete thirty-minute resource-admission ceiling with `resource_error` and an empty `steps` result, proving the child never spawned. A later process query was still queued.
