These facts establish severe pre-spawn resource waiting during the same broad period; they do not establish that the ChatGPT client message error itself was caused by that queue rather than a host/UI/network layer that Machine Bridge cannot observe.

The scheduler's protected-drain behavior is intentional, not reclassified as a pressure-policy defect. Existing tests require an aged feasible rank-zero waiter blocked by coordinator-owned reservations to stop later fitting backfill until those reservations drain; consequently `pressure.state=green` can coexist with fairness blocking because the pressure state and queue fairness answer different questions. Beta.100 leaves admission limits, priorities, aging, and backfill semantics unchanged and repairs observability instead. `ResourceCoordinator.snapshot()` now exposes only the Boolean `waiters.drain_active`, calculated by the exact same selection state machine used for admission, and owner diagnostics project that Boolean without command text, path, PID, waiter identity, or reservation details. Managed-job status switches to the fixed low-cardinality phase `resource_admission` before acquisition and restores `steps`/`finally_steps`/`recovery-cleanup` after successful admission, so `read_job` can distinguish a process that has not spawned from a slow child. The runner retains numeric `resource_admission_ms` while preserving `duration_ms` as the total orchestration duration. Local/owner `read_job` projection exposes that pre-spawn portion for delayed-success postmortems, but delegated non-owner reads strip it because isolating machine-user admission delay would otherwise sharpen a cross-workload contention side channel beyond the existing total-duration signal. A status-write failure after lease acquisition but before spawn triggers an explicit lease-release attempt before the status error propagates; if that release also fails, the runner surfaces both failures together instead of silently suppressing the cleanup fault. The thirty-minute ceiling is now one shared durable-delivery contract value used by the runner, Worker tool descriptions, and `server_info.tool_delivery`; the separate 1–600-second process timeout remains child execution time after admission.

**Additional race/hygiene closure.** The bounded daemon-fixture exit helper still had a narrow event-registration race: it checked `exitCode`/`signalCode` before attaching the `exit` listener, so a child exiting between those operations could lose the event and turn into a false thirty-second timeout. The helper now attaches the listener, installs single-settlement timeout handling, and immediately rechecks child state; the architecture contract binds that post-registration check. Repository catch-hygiene was also widened without broad pattern matching: an empty Promise rejection handler is rejected across direct or optional-chained `.catch`, harmless whitespace, `async`, and parameterized arrow forms, while a non-empty explanatory comment or handling body remains accepted.

**Verification and release consequence.** The first targeted pass exposed only an extra final blank line left after fixture extraction; repository hygiene caught it and it was fixed. Focused resource-admission, security-audit, release-impact, agent-context, and full-access regressions passed. The correct `tests/local-self-test.mjs` entry completed every named phase in about thirty seconds, including the listener/recheck teardown boundary. Managed-job integration then passed twice: the second run exercised the final projection in which delegated operator reads omit `resource_admission_ms` while local/owner reads retain it. Worker runtime-infrastructure tests passed with the shared 1,800,000 ms admission contract and tool-delivery projection, and architecture, lint, typecheck, package, privacy, version, tool-reference generation, and whitespace gates have passed during the review; gates rerun before the final freeze remain authoritative. Attempts to start the broader Worker integration while unrelated machine-user work held capacity were rejected before spawn as `host_pressure_red` or `fairness_wait`; those responses are resource-admission evidence, not test failures, so Worker integration still requires an admitted run in the final full plan. Because beta.100 changes shipped local runtime and documentation bytes, beta.99's local full receipt and candidate are stale. Beta.100 requires a fresh frozen fast/full run and candidate/install-only cycle, and a later explicit owner authorization before live activation; no beta.100 acceptance, commit, push, npm publication, tag, or GitHub Release exists at this point.

## 2026-08-17 beta.99 pre-acceptance shipped-testing-contract drift

**Observed beta.98 live evidence.** The exact beta.98 candidate was owner-authorized and activated through the guarded persistent-candidate command. The durable activation job completed successfully after re-verifying the exact candidate tarball, and post-handoff `server_info` reported `3.0.0-beta.98` with one authenticated/ready daemon socket, readiness verified, and no pending calls. The canary executed from the package root derived from the activation record `runtime_entry`; authorization-code exchange, authenticated MCP, refresh rotation, refreshed MCP, and synthetic client/account cleanup all succeeded. `diagnose_runtime` then reported an operational runtime, authenticated/ready relay, zero active/draining processes, green resource admission, and a healthy security-audit worker with a verified chain, `content_logged=false`, and zero dropped records. The browser store had also converged on the beta.98 stable extension bytes, but the currently loaded browser extension still required reload; browser live verification therefore was not complete.

**Blocking pre-acceptance defect.** Before recording acceptance, a source/runtime/documentation cross-check found that shipped `docs/TESTING.md` still claimed `full-access:test`, `local-self-test`, and `agent-context-test` used five-minute real-host-pressure resource-admission waits. Current source contradicts that text: full-access and agent-context use isolated coordinators, synthetic healthy-host samplers, and ten-second process-admission budgets; beta.98 additionally changed runtime self-test to the same synthetic healthy-host isolation and ten-second one-shot/process-session admission, added a thirty-second daemon child-exit bound, and emitted per-phase progress markers. The five-minute managed-job CLI/stdio background-settlement observation windows are separate and remain valid. Because `docs/` is explicitly included in `package.json.files`, the stale prose is part of the candidate bytes rather than an out-of-band note.

**Release consequence and guard.** Beta.98 is not accepted or publishable despite successful activation and OAuth canary. No acceptance record was written. The documentation is corrected in beta.99, and the architecture release-contract suite now both rejects the obsolete five-minute fixture wording and requires the current isolated/synthetic ten-second contract. This is a packaged-byte change, so beta.98 live evidence cannot be reused as beta.99 acceptance evidence; beta.99 must repeat frozen verification, candidate preparation, install-only preflight, explicit owner activation, activated-package canary, observed live verification, and acceptance. The live beta.98 runtime remains a valid operational baseline while beta.99 is prepared; no rollback is justified by this documentation-only defect.

## 2026-08-17 beta.98 exact-head CodeQL API-shape drift

**Exact-head hosted finding.** Beta.97 was locally frozen, owner-activated, canary/live-verified, accepted, committed as `e7bf465790961db203ad751e8d876a60db880f98`, and guarded-pushed to PR #85 before hosted provider validation. Dependency Review, Governance, Workflow Policy, CodeQL's Actions analysis, package-audit, Ubuntu full verification, and Windows platform/install all passed on that exact head. JavaScript/TypeScript CodeQL completed analysis successfully but the repository's zero-unaccepted-SARIF gate rejected five `js/superfluous-trailing-arguments` results at the shared timeout helper and its Worker/test callers. The existing allowlisted direct-process shell-sink result remained accepted and is unrelated to this failure.

**Root cause, not waiver.** `src/shared/foreground-timeout.d.mts` still declared `remoteForegroundMaximumSeconds(name: string): number`, and all call sites still passed the tool name, but beta.97's JavaScript implementation had been simplified to `remoteForegroundMaximumSeconds()` because the maximum is currently uniform across configurable foreground tools. JavaScript tolerates the extra argument, so behavior tests, ESLint, and TypeScript did not expose the runtime/declaration arity mismatch; CodeQL correctly identified the stale contract shape. The repair restores the formal `_name` parameter while leaving the function body and timeout value unchanged. No SARIF exception is appropriate because the declaration/implementation inconsistency is real and trivially repairable.

**CodeQL regression.** A new local assertion binds `remoteForegroundMaximumSeconds.length === 1`. It failed against the beta.97 implementation before the source repair and passed afterward; worker runtime infrastructure, lint, typecheck, architecture, and `git diff --check` also pass.

**Independent macOS hosted timeout.** The same beta.97 CI run exposed a second provider-only failure that must not be normalized into the CodeQL result. The macOS platform job completed setup and tasks 1–117 of the 122-task platform plan by `2026-08-17T09:48:01Z`, then started task 118 (`self-test`). No self-test terminal output followed. At `10:06:42Z` GitHub cancelled that step at the job's 20-minute workflow ceiling, and the subsequent documented global-install step was skipped. Ubuntu full verification and Windows platform/install on the same PR succeeded, so this is not evidence of a general package or cross-platform failure. It is also not evidence that increasing the macOS job timeout is the correct repair: the same self-test normally completes in tens of seconds.

**Harness hardening with bounded causal claims.** The beta.97 self-test did not emit phase progress, so the provider log cannot prove which internal phase was active during the long silence. Source review found two independent harness defects that can make this non-resource test inherit unbounded or multi-minute machine conditions. `runtimeSelfTest()` used a private resource-coordinator root but still sampled the real shared host and set `processResourceWaitMs` to five minutes for four `LocalRuntime` fixtures; multiple process assertions could therefore wait on provider host pressure even though resource-pressure policy is independently covered elsewhere. Separately, `local-self-test.mjs` waited for daemon-fixture child `exit` events without any deadline. Beta.98 follows the already-established agent-context/full-access isolation pattern: runtime fixtures keep their private coordinator but inject the existing synthetic healthy host sample and reduce their harness-only resource wait to ten seconds; daemon child exit is bounded by the existing 30-second fixture budget; and each self-test phase logs start/completion so a future hosted stall identifies its phase. This does not weaken production resource admission or its dedicated pressure/fairness tests. On the owner Mac the hardened self-test completed every phase successfully in 29.3 seconds.

**Coverage ownership after isolation.** The first beta.98 full run then failed one local gate after the self-test fix: `coverage:test` reported `src/local/resource-host-cache.mjs` at 75% function coverage rather than its required 100%. This was a useful consequence of removing accidental real-host execution from the broad runtime self-test, not a reason to restore shared-host sampling. The resource-admission suite already owned explicit cache freshness/cross-scope semantics but did not execute the `withCachedIo()` quick-refresh path. Beta.98 adds deterministic cases for both a non-I/O CPU refresh that retains a valid scope-local I/O hint and an I/O request within the five-second fresh-I/O window. The dedicated coverage gate then passes with `resource-host-cache.mjs` at 100% function coverage (4/4), keeping policy/cache behavior and its coverage in the specialized suite.

**Release consequence.** These repairs change packaged shared runtime, version metadata, and documentation bytes after beta.97 acceptance; the regression-test changes are repository verification inputs rather than npm tarball entries. Beta.97's live acceptance remains valid evidence for the exact beta.97 candidate but cannot authorize beta.98. The live beta.97 service remains unchanged while beta.98 is rebuilt locally and must complete a fresh exact-tree verification/candidate/explicit-activation/canary/live-acceptance cycle before another guarded push.

## 2026-08-17 beta.97 managed-job activation authentication/JSON settlement

**Observed live release-path defect.** The beta.96 source passed frozen fast/full verification, produced an exact candidate, and the candidate later completed owner-terminal activation, activated-package OAuth canary, live Worker/daemon/browser verification, and local acceptance. After the local control plane recovered, however, the earlier activation that the coding agent had launched through the repository-required detached managed-job carrier had a definitive failed terminal record: it ran for about 793.6 seconds and exited with `persistent candidate activation did not return valid JSON`. It had verified and installed the exact beta.96 tarball and entered persistent activation before failing. The later owner-terminal invocation of the same guarded activation command succeeded. A targeted `pmset` review found no Sleep/Wake event during the failed 15:45–15:59 local interval and showed the machine kept a user-idle-sleep prevention assertion, so the beta.95 verification-sleep failure mechanism does not explain this activation incident.

**Source invariant violation and bounded inference.** The failed activation did not record the private Wrangler stage that was active, so this audit does not claim as an observed fact that the first run entered `wrangler login`. Source tracing nevertheless exposed a release-blocking branch whose timing and final parse error match the live symptom. `activatePersistentRuntime()` stopped the verified login provider before `prepareRemoteState()` called `ensureWorkerDeployment()`. That deployment function performed a captured `wrangler whoami`, but on authentication failure it invoked `wrangler login` without capture; the shared executable runner therefore inherited stdout/stderr and allowed login up to ten minutes. The outer release wrapper simultaneously ran the inner candidate as `machine-mcp activate ... --json` and treated the complete child stdout as one JSON document. Interactive Wrangler text could therefore both prolong the service-offline interval and make a successfully converged inner activation look like a JSON parse failure. Independently of whether that exact branch caused the beta.96 run, allowing an interactive login after service stop and inside a machine-readable stdout channel violates the documented managed-job activation contract.

**Repair.** Beta.97 moves Wrangler authentication to a release-wrapper preflight before the release-runtime/service handoff. The preflight uses captured `whoami`. On the `managed_job` execution surface, missing authentication returns typed `worker_authentication_required` with `sideEffectsStarted=false` and never starts login; the operator authenticates Wrangler in an ordinary owner terminal before retrying. On an ordinary local terminal, the wrapper may run interactive login while the existing service is still untouched, then requires a second captured `whoami`. The inner Worker deployment layer independently rejects interactive login whenever `args.json` is true, closing the preflight-to-handoff authentication-expiry race; ordinary non-JSON startup still supports login but also re-verifies authentication before deploy. Tests fault-inject all four branches, and architecture checks require the outer preflight to precede the persistent release-runtime lock and the JSON guard to precede login.

**Release consequence.** The defect was discovered after beta.96 acceptance, but the repair changes packaged release tooling and local runtime behavior. Under the prerelease invariant, beta.96 acceptance cannot authorize these bytes. Beta.97 therefore requires a fresh exact-tree fast/full receipt, candidate, install-only preflight, new explicit live-activation authorization, managed-job activation proof, activated-package OAuth canary, observed live verification, and acceptance before any commit/push/publication decision.

## 2026-08-17 beta.96 independent review-report adjudication and second-pass hardening

**Report adjudication, not checklist acceptance.** A supplied review was written against beta.94 and mixed accurate observations with stale or unsupported conclusions, so each claim was re-tested against the beta.95 working tree before modification. The claimed relay timer leak/double-resolution mechanism was not present: `RelayConnection` already clears connect/handshake/readiness/reconnect/outage timers on the relevant stop/close/fatal paths, heartbeat start is stop-before-rearm, socket handlers bind the captured socket generation, and the first-readiness resolver is nulled immediately after settlement. The claimed unbounded pending/waiter memory path was also not present: the Worker pending map and readiness waiters consume the same thirty-ordinary-plus-two-reserved admission algebra, terminal/timed-out records are removed, detached calls remain capacity-accounted, and Durable Object alarm/event-boundary sweeps expire due records. `outageCount` is a scalar diagnostic counter, not a retained queue. The claimed missing 1002/1008/1009/1012 WebSocket mapping was contradicted by the existing explicit close-category/protocol tests. Changelog size and the large Computer Use/application modules remain maintenance observations rather than demonstrated defects; existing executable line ceilings are retained instead of introducing an unmotivated archive or Strategy-pattern rewrite.

**Actual relay lifecycle defect found while falsifying the timer claim.** A minimal current-source reproduction started a relay with a socket that never reached readiness, called `stop()`, and raced the returned `start()` Promise against a short timer; the Promise remained `pending`. Timer cleanup therefore worked, but the first-start settlement contract did not. Beta.96 captures the pending resolver before shutdown, clears both resolver references, performs the existing timer/socket cleanup, and settles that start as no-readiness. The runtime layer additionally checks lifecycle ownership after the await and in the rejection path, so a concurrent `stop()` that has moved the runtime to `stopping`/`stopped` cannot be overwritten by a late `markRunning()` or `markFailed()`. Regression tests prove stop-before-ready settles and leaves no scheduler tasks, while a late relay-start rejection cannot replace a stopped runtime lifecycle.

**Deadline arithmetic: public paths were already bounded; one internal fail-closed gap was real.** Public foreground timeout parsing, Worker configurable-tool validation, `read_process.wait_ms`, daemon-ready grace, and relay-contract budgets already reject or clamp ordinary malformed user input. Relay JSON cannot represent `NaN`/`Infinity`. However, direct `PendingCallRegistry`/`PendingCallDeadlines` use still normalized with `Math.floor(Number(value) || 1)`, so an internal `Infinity` produced both an infinite timer delay and an infinite next-alarm delay. Beta.96 centralizes strict pending-delay validation: operation deadlines are bounded by `maximumRelayToolTimeoutMs`, reconnect grace by the shared reconnect contract, and the timer layer independently rejects non-finite/non-integer/non-positive/overflow values. Invalid timing is rejected before a call is detached or registered, preserving pending state. This is defense in depth against an internal contract regression, not evidence that a remote client could previously encode JSON `Infinity`.

**Relay envelope validation tightened where the report was directionally right.** The local relay parser already bounded message size, required a plain argument record, validated account/client/family identifier shapes, normalized account roles, and capped timeout values. It was nevertheless too permissive about shape: an arbitrary short string could be a call ID, a syntactically arbitrary tool string could reach dispatch, numeric authorization versions could be coerced from strings, and missing/string/negative/over-limit `timeout_ms` values could be defaulted or clamped. Beta.96 aligns call IDs with the existing `call_...` resume grammar, requires a lowercase bounded tool-name grammar, keeps authorization version/role strictly typed, and requires an explicit integer timeout inside the shared 1–50-second relay envelope. Unknown but syntactically valid tools still fail at the ordinary tool authorization/dispatch boundary; catalog membership is deliberately not duplicated in the transport parser.

**Logging/privacy claim narrowed to the real boundary.** JWT redaction was already present in `sanitizePortableLogText`, synthetic JWT coverage already exercised free-form local/Worker logging, and repository privacy scanning already rejected JWT-shaped bearer values. Compact DPoP proofs use the same JWS/JWT shape and therefore traverse that existing redaction. No production path was found logging `cf-connecting-ip` or a remote socket address; the rate-limiter hashes that address before using it as a state key. A generic IPv6 regex was therefore rejected: it would add false-positive/corruption risk without closing an observed leak. Privacy documentation now makes the design explicit—compact JWT/JWS/DPoP-shaped values are sanitized, while client/network identity should be omitted or coarsened at the source rather than entrusted to a universal IP scrubber.

**Silent-error review found a different maintenance gap.** The named `daemon-sockets.ts` and relay transport paths already classify meaningful transport failures; the report's example location was inaccurate. Repository-wide review did find uncommented best-effort/fail-closed catches in browser DevTools cleanup, snapshot preflight, extension reconnect/UI decoration, application cleanup/probing, release temp cleanup, diagnostics, audit shutdown, and macOS helper cleanup. Those paths now carry explicit comments explaining why the primary settlement remains authoritative instead of emitting noisy or privacy-risking raw exception text. Repository hygiene rejects newly introduced empty `catch {}` and empty Promise `.catch(() => {})` bodies under `src/`, `scripts/`, and `browser-extension/`. The browser extension's intentionally empty WebSocket `onerror` now documents that `onclose` owns handshake failure/reconnect settlement.

**Independent second-pass drift finding.** The previous beta.95 review corrected README/architecture/operations timing, but a fresh cross-surface search found two current-state leftovers that its architecture gate did not cover. `docs/TESTING.md` still said ordinary daemon tools used up to 30 seconds, and `src/shared/server-metadata.json`—which is delivered as server guidance rather than being passive prose—still described ordinary tools and remote process one-shots with the old 30-second foreground model. Beta.96 updates both and expands the release-contract guard. Shared guidance now distinguishes ordinary one-step durable process tools, which already return a `job_id` and should be observed with `read_job`, from owner-only `start_job` for multi-step/resource/finally workflows. This is a concrete contract correction; the historical beta.91/beta.89 audit entries remain untouched as historical evidence.

**Release consequence.** These fixes change packaged local runtime, Worker timing validation, browser extension/script bytes, shared server metadata, tests, and documentation after a beta.95 exact-tree candidate had already been prepared locally. That beta.95 receipt/candidate is therefore stale by construction. The package is advanced to beta.96 and requires a fresh targeted/fast/privacy/full/candidate cycle. No live beta.96 activation, Worker deployment, acceptance, Git commit, push, npm publication, or GitHub release is implied by this source review.

**Verification-carrier boundary found by the fresh full gate.** After the report-driven fixes, one frozen beta.96 `check:full` passed all 130 tasks in 596.0 seconds—only about four seconds inside the 600-second detached execution ceiling of remote `run_process`. The dominant variance was `install:test` at 229.7 seconds; the run itself did not classify why that install was slower than earlier observations, so no network or registry cause is asserted. This exposed an orchestration hazard rather than a product-test failure: a legitimate full suite can outlive the ordinary one-step remote process budget. Guidance now keeps ordinary one-step work on the durable process tools only when it fits 600 seconds and explicitly routes owner-authorized remote full verification (or another legitimate >600-second step) through detached `start_job` with a larger step timeout. The final beta.96 full receipt must therefore be regenerated after this guidance change using the wider carrier; the suite's own contents and pass criteria remain unchanged.

## 2026-08-17 beta.95 independent reply-safety and privacy review

**Independent contract review.** The beta.94 runtime behavior remained consistent with its incident repair: relay-origin `start_process` performs zero-queue cooperative admission, ordinary hosted tools default to 20 seconds plus a distinct five-second settlement margin, durable process one-shots separate ten-second acceptance from up-to-600-second execution, public HTTP/SSE delivery remains request-scoped, and same-daemon relay reconnect consumes the original absolute pending-call deadline rather than extending it. The review found no new SSE replay/session requirement and no evidence that the removed 30-second remote-process foreground model still governed a production relay route.

**Drift removed.** Two implementation/documentation leftovers could have reintroduced misleading behavior later. `daemon-ready-waiters.ts` still carried a private ten-second fallback even though the authoritative relay contract had moved new-call recovery to five seconds, and the shared contract plus `server_info` still exposed a 30-second process-foreground ceiling even though all remote `exec_command`/`run_process`/`run_local_command` calls route through durable job acceptance. Beta.95 derives the waiter fallback and request-owned `start_process` budget from `relay-contract.json`, removes the obsolete process-foreground field from the shared/public contract, and reports the explicit process-session startup envelope instead. The lower process service intentionally retains a private 30-second defense ceiling so an accidental internal bypass of durable relay routing still fails closed; that ceiling is not advertised as a supported remote delivery mode. README/architecture/testing text that still described the beta.93 timings, plus an unrelated stale 1.5-second host-snapshot freshness claim where code uses 500 ms, is corrected and guarded by architecture checks.

**Independent resource-capacity defect.** Live review found one interactive resource waiter that had remained pre-spawn for several minutes even though host pressure was green and there were no leases. Its fixed request was `pytest -n 8` on an eight-core host. Pure policy reproduction proved the request still returned `cpu_pressure_window` with zero observed CPU because the interactive launch policy intentionally retains one core of headroom: the best-case launch window is therefore below eight cores. That condition can never recover by waiting, and the old `selectedResourceWaiter` starvation exception could also treat an empty-lease `cpu_pressure_window` as potentially feasible. Beta.95 classifies fixed CPU requests above the best-case priority-specific launch window as `cpu_request_exceeds_launch_window`, fails them before the retry sleep loop with `retryable=false`, preserves explicit argv/concurrency unchanged, and leaves elastic/unbounded fitting plus genuinely transient CPU-window pressure unchanged. Regression coverage proves an eight-worker fixed request is rejected on an idle eight-core host, a seven-worker interactive request remains admissible, the impossible waiter cannot reserve protected drain, and the public process boundary advises reducing explicit parallelism instead of advertising a temporary retry.

**Verification idle-sleep defect.** A later frozen-tree `check:fast` began at `2026-08-17 12:27:33 +0800`; `pmset` records Idle Sleep at `12:27:39` for 594 seconds and DarkWake at `12:37:33`. The fast runner then reported `worker-types-generator:test` after about 593.7 seconds because a fixture that normally exits with code 7 instead reached its two-second Wrangler hard timeout immediately after wake. The failure duration therefore matched host suspension rather than active test execution. Beta.95 does not relax those short lifecycle timeouts. Instead the macOS verification entrypoint re-executes the complete fast/platform/full run under `/usr/bin/caffeinate -i`, preventing ordinary Idle Sleep for the verification lifetime while preserving the same task semantics and exit status. The guard is verification-only, recursion-bounded, absent on other platforms, and does not treat explicit/lid-close sleep as a supported verification environment.

**Relay/sleep evidence correction.** During this review the live beta.94 relay reported close `1006` at `2026-08-17T03:56:46.879Z` and was ready again at `03:56:51.232Z`. The launchd daemon stayed on the same verified PID with one lifetime run and no termination signal; event-loop lag remained bounded. A targeted `pmset` check then showed `Wake from Deep Idle` at `2026-08-17 11:56:46 +0800`, followed by normal wake activity. This event is therefore a sleep/wake transport reconstruction, not evidence of a daemon crash or hosted-deadline regression. A later outage began at `2026-08-17T04:21:16.022Z`, was classified as `relay_transport_error` / `network_error` with close `1006`, and recovered in about 5.5 seconds; `pmset` recorded another Deep Idle wake at `12:21:07 +0800`, roughly ten seconds before that transport failure. That second event is strongly wake-adjacent but is recorded more conservatively as a post-wake network-recovery interruption rather than a same-instant wake disconnect. Neither later event invalidates the distinct earlier beta.94 incident evidence that host-pressure reply queueing and a separately observed awake relay interruption were independent mechanisms.

**Privacy and observability review.** The beta.94 acceptance record contains only fixed release metadata and cryptographic digests; it contains no machine path, account identifier, endpoint, command/output content, or credential. Operational logs continue to omit arguments/results. To preserve the incident discriminator after pressure has recovered, resource-admission failures may add only an allowlisted reason and coarse pressure state to the existing debug-only tool failure event. Unknown or future reason text collapses to fixed fallback values rather than becoming free-form log data. No new default-level event, path, process name, sampled command output, account identity, or resource identifier is recorded.

## 2026-08-17 beta.94 hosted reply headroom under host pressure

**Observed incident and falsified alternatives.** A live hosted-call investigation reproduced local resource-admission failures while a heavy Xcode/Simulator workload kept the macOS host awake: the coordinator reported red load backlog and returned both `host_pressure_red` and `coordinator_busy`. The Machine Bridge daemon itself was not CPU-bound. The relay close-1006 episode examined during that beta.94 incident also occurred while the host was awake and recovered in a few seconds, so sleep/wake did not explain that incident-window transport event. The installed service was a verified launchd daemon, the private Wrangler toolchain was healthy, and the Worker's call counters did not show a Worker execution-timeout defect; the foreground-candidate/Wrangler hypothesis was therefore rejected rather than patched. Later sleep/wake-correlated relay interruptions are recorded separately rather than rewriting this historical evidence.

**Causal repair.** Beta.93 made remote process one-shots durable, but `start_process` intentionally remained a request-scoped daemon-lifetime session. Its local process-session manager still inherited the owner-local ten-second cooperative resource wait, so a hosted request could queue under known host pressure before spawn even though the caller could safely receive a retryable pre-spawn error instead. Relay-origin `start_process` now uses a zero-queue admission policy: it performs the normal host/capacity check once and, if not admitted, returns the existing retryable `unavailable` classification with `side_effects_started=false` at the Worker boundary. Local/stdio callers keep the ten-second wait, and explicit configured waits remain authoritative. The Worker also reduces `start_process` to a ten-second execution plus five-second settlement envelope, ordinary daemon-backed defaults to 20+5 seconds, and configurable browser/application defaults to 20 seconds without reducing their explicit 45-second maximum. New calls wait only five seconds for daemon readiness; in-flight reconnect still consumes the call's original absolute deadline and can rebind to the same daemon instance.

**Transport boundary retained.** The relay already retains completed daemon results until Worker acknowledgement and replays those results after a same-instance reconnect; the Worker retains detached pending calls through the bounded reconnect interval and sends `resume_calls`. The close-1006 path therefore did not justify a second MCP terminal-result store, replay identifier, or cross-request response session. Public HTTP/SSE closure remains cancellation of transient request-owned work, while operations that must outlive delivery use managed jobs. This repair reduces the probability that Machine Bridge itself consumes the Host's reply window; it cannot make a disconnected Host render a terminal frame, and `host_terminal_receipt_observable=false` remains the explicit external boundary.

## 2026-08-16 beta.93 durable process settlement and disconnect recovery

**Remaining lifecycle defect.** Beta.91 bounded hosted foreground calls and made reconnect consume the original absolute deadline; beta.92 hardened activation rollback. Those changes reduced how long a broken call could occupy the host, but they did not change the ownership model of ordinary remote process one-shots: `exec_command`, `run_process`, and `run_local_command` still spawned work inside the current MCP call. A relay interruption, daemon replacement, host deadline, or cancellation could therefore terminate the child or discard its final result even when the underlying task was otherwise valid. During this repair's targeted verification the ready daemon actually observed a close-1006 relay interruption at `2026-08-16T05:44:26.304Z` and returned ready at `05:44:29.076Z`; the detached managed verification job continued through that transport loss. The event is direct evidence that task correctness cannot depend on an uninterrupted response channel.

**Durable-first process contract.** Remote `exec_command`, `run_process`, and `run_local_command` now validate their original tool/cwd/argv/registered-command authority first, then commit the operation as a principal-bound one-step managed job before execution. The MCP request owns only a 10-second acceptance window plus the Worker's separate five-second settlement margin; the detached step has an independent 600-second maximum and returns a `job_id` with `read_job`/`list_jobs` recovery metadata. Remote calls require a caller-held `idempotency_key` before daemon dispatch, so even loss of the acceptance response leaves the caller with a credential that can be retried against the same retained job instead of duplicating execution. Cached hosts that omit the new field receive a normal no-side-effect tool error requesting a schema refresh rather than a protocol-only validation failure. The durable step uses interactive resource-admission priority so persistence does not silently demote an interactive tool call behind unrelated background builds. `start_process` remains the daemon-lifetime session path for interactive stdin; explicit `start_job` remains the owner-only multi-step/finally/resource API.

**Authority and isolation preservation.** Automatic persistence does not turn operator process authority into owner managed-job authority. The internal durable path authorizes the original source tool, persists the existing principal binding, remains visible/cancellable through the existing account ownership rules, and never exposes `start_job` to operators. Non-owner process execution also keeps the verified macOS delegated workspace sandbox. The runner persists only a boolean delegated-execution requirement and reconstructs the sandbox wrapper at the actual step spawn using the job's own runtime directory, so daemon replacement cannot make the sandbox depend on an expired daemon temp path. Missing or failed sandbox verification still fails closed.

**Diagnostic boundary repair.** `diagnose_runtime` previously used resource-admitted public process paths for its fixed local spawn/shell probes. A long resource-admission wait could therefore make the diagnostic report `local-process-spawn=false` even though the OS spawn boundary was healthy. The fixed spawn and shell probes now use the implementation-owned fixed-process boundary and bypass cooperative resource admission; resource pressure/waiters remain reported independently. This prevents queueing from being mislabeled as an OS/process failure.

**Activation ownership transaction.** Beta.89's execution-surface guard remains intact: managed-job runner launch still overwrites the environment with the `managed_job` marker, so persistent activation may run only from the existing durable release surface or an ordinary local terminal. Beta.92's delayed launchd stop observation and previous-service rollback retries remain. Beta.93 additionally moves `service_owner` commit out of definition installation: activation keeps the candidate owner pending through provider start and exact daemon/Worker convergence, then commits under the still-held machine-service lock. Recovery carries the same pending identity; failed recovery stops an uncommitted candidate, while owner-commit failure stops the verified provider and leaves the pending record fail-closed. This avoids both a false committed-but-inactive owner and an unsafe owner-only rollback after the provider definition may already have changed. No live `release:candidate:activate` is required to validate this lifecycle repair.

## 2026-08-16 beta.92 activation stop-settlement rollback

**Live incident.** The exact beta.91 candidate passed 130/130 local full verification and disposable installation, then was activated through the required durable managed-job surface. The managed activation step failed after about 9.1 seconds with `the existing autostart service could not be stopped before activation (launchd)`. The Worker remained beta.90, but the previously ready local daemon was no longer connected and did not return until approximately 17 minutes later. This proved that the stop operation's reported failure was not equivalent to “no side effect”: a launchd `bootout` can still settle after the helper's short observation window, while the immediate one-shot rollback start can race that in-progress removal and fail to restore persistence. The durable job prevented duplicate activation and retained the terminal error, but availability recovery was too weak.

**Causal repair.** `stopLaunchdService()` now observes safe unload for up to about ten seconds rather than the generic two-second service-convergence window. More importantly, once a bootout is dispatched against a service that was active at preflight, the result carries `restore_required=true` even if post-mutation status is unavailable or still loaded. Persistent activation already records the verified previous runtime before mutation; beta.92 uses that evidence to retry the idempotent previous-provider start/bootstrap operation and inspect the exact previous service-mode daemon after each attempt. Recovery is bounded to 30 attempts. An activation stop failure receives stable code `autostart_stop_failed`; if rollback converges, that original failure remains the candidate result, and if rollback cannot converge its error is aggregated rather than hidden.

**Related reply reliability.** The live incident also reproduced beta.90's old pending-call behavior: calls detached by the daemon shutdown remained pending far beyond their original foreground budget before clearing. Beta.91 already fixes that separate mechanism by preserving the original absolute pending-call deadline across detach/rebind, caps remote synchronous execution and process polling, and emits five-second SSE heartbeats. Beta.92 retains those changes. Historical relay logs also contain long outage durations, but code review confirms reconnect backoff itself tops out at roughly 15.5 seconds; long gaps can include OS/event-loop suspension or network/VPN unavailability, so increasing reconnect frequency was rejected as an unsupported fix. Current heartbeat recovery already forces a stale relay rebuild after a sufficiently long event-loop stall.

## 2026-08-16 beta.91 hosted-reply settlement closure

**Remaining blank-response mechanism.** Beta.90 fixed daemon-replacing activation ownership and narrowed process one-shots, but an independent request-lifecycle review found two remaining ways a valid hosted tool call could monopolize the assistant reply window. First, tools without a configurable `timeout_seconds` field still received a 60-second daemon execution budget, and `read_process` could block a single remote poll for 30 seconds. Second, when the daemon disconnected during an in-flight call, `PendingCallRegistry` paused the operation timer and armed the separate 120-second daemon reconnect grace; a successful same-instance rebind then restarted the saved remaining operation budget. The reconnect design preserved execution opportunity but silently enlarged the host-visible call lifetime, so a short call could remain unresolved for minutes and the external host could time out the whole assistant turn without displaying a final message.

**Repair and boundary.** Hosted synchronous execution now has a separate reply-safe projection instead of reusing the local administrator envelope. Ordinary daemon-backed tools receive at most 30 seconds; configurable browser/application foreground tools are capped at 45 seconds; process one-shots default to 20 seconds and are capped at 30 seconds; `read_process` waits at most five seconds per hosted poll and receives only a 5–10-second execution budget; `start_process` receives 20 seconds to establish the retained session. Local stdio/CLI behavior is unchanged. The public SSE proxy heartbeat moves from ten to five seconds. More importantly, detach/rebind preserves the original absolute pending-call deadline: reconnect grace is capped by the remaining operation budget and reconnect time is no longer paused, so daemon recovery cannot extend a current HTTP/SSE request beyond the deadline it had before the outage. Durable work still belongs in managed jobs, which are intentionally independent of one request or daemon lifetime.

**Rolling-upgrade and residual limitation.** Hosts may cache old tool schemas. Maximum-only version skew for both `timeout_seconds` and `read_process.wait_ms` therefore returns a normal MCP `isError` result with `invalid_request`, `side_effects_started=false`, and schema-refresh/session/job guidance; malformed types and unrelated schema failures remain protocol errors. `server_info.tool_delivery` exposes the ordinary, configurable-foreground, process, poll, and settlement ceilings for diagnosis. Machine Bridge still cannot observe a host UI's terminal-frame consumption (`host_terminal_receipt_observable=false`), so no server-side timeout can mathematically guarantee that a particular client renders the final assistant message. The repair instead removes known server-side paths that could unnecessarily consume or enlarge that external budget and makes long work recoverable through sessions/jobs with short polling.

## 2026-08-16 beta.90 stale-host timeout-schema compatibility

**Live finding.** After beta.89 was activated successfully through a durable managed job and its activated-package OAuth canary passed, a deliberate live probe exercised the new process ceiling through the same ChatGPT connector that had opened the conversation before beta.89 deployment. The connector's local action snapshot still described `run_process.timeout_seconds` as maximum 60, so it accepted a request for 46 seconds. The beta.89 Worker had already published the correct 45-second schema and rejected the call before daemon dispatch, but returned the normal JSON-RPC `-32602` used for input-schema violations. The connector translated that protocol error into a generic `UNKNOWN: ExceptionGroup` rather than preserving the useful validation data. No child process ran, but the user-facing settlement was materially worse than the intended explicit long-task routing guidance.

**Causal boundary.** Tool schemas are cacheable host metadata; a Worker deployment cannot make every already-open host/tool snapshot refresh atomically. Therefore lowering a server-side maximum creates a temporary compatibility interval in which a stale client can construct an argument the current Worker knows is invalid. Treating that expected version-skew condition identically to a malformed request delegated presentation quality to every connector's JSON-RPC error mapping. The problem was not the 45-second limit itself and not a daemon failure; it was an incomplete rolling-upgrade contract between cached tool metadata and current server validation.

**Repair.** `McpController` now recognizes the narrow case where all validation issues are `/timeout_seconds` with keyword `maximum`. It returns a successful JSON-RPC envelope containing a standard MCP tool result with `isError=true`, structured `invalid_request`, `side_effects_started=false`, `schema_refresh_recommended=true`, the bounded validation issues, and instructions to refresh `tools/list` or move longer work to process sessions/managed jobs. The daemon is never called. All other input-schema failures still use `-32602`; tests explicitly keep malformed timeout types, unknown fields, missing names, and unknown tools on the protocol-error path. This gives new clients the strict schema and old clients a useful fail-closed settlement without widening execution authority or accepting the obsolete timeout.

**Release consequence.** beta.89 proved the durable daemon-replacement fix and canary path, but this live connector compatibility defect changes packaged Worker behavior after beta.89 activation. beta.89 is therefore superseded without acceptance; beta.90 requires fresh full verification, exact candidate preparation, durable activation, activated-package OAuth canary, and live stale-schema verification before acceptance.

## 2026-08-16 beta.89 daemon-handoff and long-task settlement repair

**Live activation failure and causal mechanism.** beta.88 passed its local frozen-tree verification and candidate install preflight, then persistent activation was launched through a Machine Bridge process session. The Worker updated to beta.88, but the old login daemon disappeared and `machine-mcp service status` subsequently reported a committed beta.88 service owner with launchd `loaded=false`, `active=false`. Code review established the causal chain rather than treating this as a generic launchd flake: process-session children are registered in the current daemon's `ProcessTracker`; activation intentionally calls `stopAutostart()` during foreground-to-service handoff; `LocalRuntime.stop()` drains tracked process groups with `SIGKILL`; on POSIX the process-tree implementation targets the child's process group. The old daemon could therefore terminate the very activation transaction that was replacing it, after service-definition/owner mutation but before `startAutostart()` completed. Manual `machine-mcp service start` recovered the already-committed beta.88 owner and restored a ready beta.88 daemon, confirming that the broken state was the handoff boundary rather than candidate package identity.

**Repair.** Foreground one-shot processes, daemon-lifetime process sessions, and detached managed-job runners now receive an internal execution-surface marker after environment construction. Persistent release activation inspects that marker before candidate installation, Worker deployment, or service mutation. `foreground_process`, `process_session`, and any unknown non-empty surface fail closed with a no-side-effects error; an ordinary local terminal and the detached `managed_job` surface remain valid. The managed runner overwrites any inherited/spoofed marker even when full environment inheritance is requested. This deliberately reuses the existing durable runner, whose detached/unreferenced process and persisted job state already survive MCP loss and daemon restart, rather than creating a second release-specific detachment mechanism.

**Long foreground calls and blank-response risk.** A separate review found that remote process tools could request the complete 60-second interactive execution window while Worker settlement was only budgeted after that execution deadline. In practice, resource admission, transport delay, process termination settlement, or host response overhead near that boundary can consume the remaining UI/tool-call envelope; the user then experiences a terminated task and, in the worst case, no visible assistant reply. Remote `exec_command`, `run_process`, and `run_local_command` now default to 30 seconds and reject requests above 45 seconds before spawn. The general 60-second interactive ceiling remains for browser/application operations that have their own bounded settlement logic. Work expected to exceed the process foreground budget must use `start_process`/`read_process`; work that must survive daemon replacement or MCP disconnect must use `start_job`/`read_job`. The design intentionally does not auto-detach an already-started one-shot command at timeout, because silently changing one-shot ownership/cleanup semantics after side effects begin would create a different ambiguity class.

**Additional boundary repair.** Execution-surface regression coverage instantiated `ProcessSessionManager` without a resource coordinator and exposed a latent contract defect: `acquireProcessResources(null, ...)` returned environment metadata but dropped the original command/argv, producing an undefined executable at spawn. The no-coordinator path now preserves command/argv exactly. Dedicated resource-admission tests remain responsible for real admission behavior; standalone process-session composition no longer accidentally depends on a coordinator being present.

**Nested resource-wait finding.** A subsequent full run reached `full-access:test` and then waited 331.9 seconds before its internal managed job timed out in `running`. Runtime diagnostics showed the outer `check:full` holding a verification-plan heavy lease while multiple older background waiters occupied the machine-user queue. The full-access fixture already supplied a private coordinator root and synthetic host sampler to its `LocalRuntime`, but that root was used only by foreground/process-session execution; `ManagedJobManager` launched its detached runner from the ambient environment, so the inner job rejoined the global queue. `LocalRuntime(resourceCoordinatorRoot=...)` now propagates that root as a runner-environment override for both normal managed-job launch and recovery relaunch. Production runtimes that do not override the coordinator retain the default machine-user namespace. Re-running `npm run full-access:test` under the same outer Machine Bridge process carrier completed immediately, proving the nested queue dependency was removed rather than masked with a longer timeout.

**Release consequence.** beta.88 cannot be accepted or reused: live mutation occurred, the activation did not complete normally, and this repair changes packaged source, release tooling, Worker timeout metadata, tests, documentation, and synchronized version surfaces. beta.89 therefore requires a fresh full receipt, exact candidate, install-only verification, durable-job activation, activated-package OAuth canary, live service/Worker/process-settlement verification, and a new acceptance record before any guarded source push.

## 2026-08-15 beta.88 workflow-surface terminology closure

**Authorization-layer attribution review.** A nested SSH maintenance probe returned an explicit remote forced-command allowlist after Machine Bridge had successfully spawned the local SSH client. Treating that result as a Machine Bridge policy denial would have widened the wrong boundary. Current shared instructions, runtime diagnostics, README, and operations guidance now make the evidence rule explicit: pre-response blocks are host-side unknowns, true local spawn failures belong to the local execution boundary, and a child exit code with bounded output proves spawn occurred so any remote allowlist/forced-command refusal must be diagnosed downstream. The change adds no new Machine Bridge authority and avoids using a broader local policy as compensation for a narrower target-side restriction.

**Module-responsibility review.** `computer-use.mjs` had accumulated expectation-schema normalization, intrinsic application-state expectations, and Accessibility check/uncheck target validation alongside orchestration, dispatch, post-observation, and settlement. Those pure validation responsibilities now live in `computer-use-expectation.mjs`; the architecture inventory treats it as a first-class boundary and lowers the parent module line ceiling. This is a structural extraction only: existing Computer Use behavior tests remain authoritative for browser/application edge cases and ambiguous mutation settlement.

**CLI responsibility review.** `cli.mjs` was at 947 lines under a 950-line architecture ceiling even though its ready/start presentation was a pure formatting concern. Start JSON, the human/JSON MCP-ready banner, initial-owner one-time credential display, and policy-summary formatting now live in `cli-ready-output.mjs`. CLI entrypoint/service regressions preserve the observable behavior, the parent falls below 900 lines, and the architecture contract lowers its ceiling to 900 while budgeting the new presentation module separately. `runtime.mjs` remains near its 700-line ceiling, but its remaining concentration is stateful runtime composition and lifecycle authority; it was not split merely to improve a line-count metric without a clearer responsibility seam.

**Resource host-sample generation race.** The full verification gate exposed a cross-process cache race rather than a policy failure: `host-sample.json` is deliberately replaced atomically, while a concurrent reader opens the old generation and then verifies the pathname identity. If another coordinator publishes between those two observations, `secure-file` correctly raises `MBM_IDENTITY_CHANGED`; `freshHostSnapshot()` previously propagated that expected publication race as an `execution_failed`. Host-sample reads now retry only this identity-change class for four fresh opens. The path-identity check remains enabled on every attempt, hard links and symlinks remain rejected, malformed JSON is not retried, and persistent generation churn still fails closed. Deterministic tests cover both recovery and exhaustion.

**Structured file mutation generation race.** A separate review of local file deletion found a narrower time-of-check/time-of-use defect in `commitPatchTransaction()`: source content was hashed before commit, then the pathname was renamed to an internal backup, but the moved generation was not revalidated before replacement publication or backup cleanup. An external process could therefore replace or mutate the source in the interval after the preflight hash and before the rename; a delete could then remove that newer generation, and an update/move could treat it as the observed source. The transaction now reads and hashes the exact backup generation immediately after rename. A mismatch enters the existing rollback path before any target publication; if rollback itself cannot restore the pathname because another actor has reoccupied it, the existing non-retryable `patch_recovery_incomplete` settlement preserves the ambiguity instead of deleting competing state.

**Optimistic write convergence.** `edit_file` always writes against the SHA-256 of the text it just read, and `write_file` may accept an explicit `expected_sha256`; both previously used a second check followed by overwrite-capable rename, leaving the same final check-to-rename window. Expected-hash atomic writes now delegate to the generation-safe patch transaction, so patch updates/deletes and optimistic single-file writes share one quarantine, post-rename hash verification, no-overwrite publication, rollback, and cleanup-warning implementation. Unconditional `write_file` without a precondition remains intentionally last-writer-wins. Deterministic fault injection mutates the source inside the injected rename boundary and requires both delete and expected-hash write to reject while preserving the concurrent generation.

**Release verification reuse.** The prerelease handoff previously ran the complete 129-task local gate and then `release:candidate` immediately invoked the complete gate again before packing. That second run added several minutes but did not create an independent authority or environment: both executions observed the same owner machine and same source tree. The workflow now persists a private `.project-local/full-verification.json` receipt only after a stable `check:full` completes. The full-run guard still watches generated `.release-candidate` state so concurrent candidate regeneration invalidates the run, while the reusable receipt binds the exact verified source/configuration generation and deliberately excludes that next-stage generated candidate directory. It also binds package name/version, Node version, platform/architecture, and a six-hour freshness window. Starting any new full run deletes the old receipt before executing tests, so an observed failure or interrupted rerun cannot fall back to prior success. Candidate preparation recomputes the current source generation and rejects missing, stale, runtime-mismatched, or source-mismatched evidence before packing; candidate output or a retry of the pack stage does not by itself force another full run. The optimization therefore removes duplicate execution, not a distinct proof boundary.

**Exact-main finding.** beta.87 was fully live-verified and accepted, then its exact PR head passed Ubuntu/full, macOS/platform+install, Windows/platform+install, package audit, Governance, Workflow Policy, Dependency Review, and both CodeQL analyses. The repository permits squash merges only; PR #81 merged cleanly and the resulting `main` tree hash was byte-for-byte identical to the accepted beta.87 PR-head tree. The newly triggered exact-main CI nevertheless displayed `Verify interactive candidate acceptance` for its portable acceptance steps. Repository search found the same retired concept in the portable verifier's success output, the workflow-policy contract's descriptive label, TESTING documentation, and one architecture-test error message.

**Correction and regression.** Current workflow step names, verifier output, workflow-policy description, TESTING guidance, and test diagnostics now uniformly call this evidence `local candidate acceptance`. Architecture verification reads the actual CI YAML, portable verifier, workflow policy contract, and TESTING guide, rejects `interactive candidate acceptance` case-insensitively, and positively requires the new terminology. This expands the beta.87 regression from selected release scripts/docs to the complete current CI presentation surface. Historical records that describe the former policy remain unchanged.

**Release consequence.** The beta.87 runtime behavior and exact candidate remain valid evidence for the underlying authorization/discovery fixes, but current workflow/docs/tests and synchronized version metadata change in beta.88. The beta.87 acceptance therefore cannot authorize beta.88 publication; the complete candidate/live acceptance cycle must run again.

## 2026-08-15 beta.87 current-contract terminology closure

**Post-push finding.** beta.86 completed its fresh fast/full/frozen-candidate gates, direct-Node install-only verification, owner-authorized activation, exact packaged OAuth canary, live Worker/service/relay/application/browser verification, acceptance, commit, and guarded push. The guarded-push success line itself still said `Verified interactive local candidate acceptance`. A repository-wide follow-up then found the same obsolete adjective in the portable CI acceptance verifier plus live operational guidance that still described an owner-terminal ceremony, real TTY streams, an agent stopping to hand activation back to the owner, and a failed `owner-terminal` activation command. These are not historical entries; they are current execution/operations surfaces and therefore contradict the policy now implemented by the release guard.

**Correction.** Current push/CI diagnostics now call the evidence `local candidate acceptance`. ENGINEERING states the actual publication invariant: explicit current-task owner authorization, the `--owner-confirm` acknowledgement, exact release-integrity gates, and one publication lock holder; TTY presence is optional. RELEASING requires the non-live direct-Node preflight and then permits the explicitly authorized owner or agent to run the exact activation command, while prohibiting an agent from racing a command the owner says they will run. OPERATIONS describes failure by activation outcome rather than terminal shape. Historical changelog/audit passages that accurately describe older owner-terminal releases remain unchanged.

**Regression and review lesson.** Architecture/documentation tests now forbid the obsolete current-contract phrases in `scripts/github-push.mjs`, `.github/scripts/verify-release-acceptance.mjs`, ENGINEERING, RELEASING, and OPERATIONS. This corrects a verification blind spot: beta.85's audit recorded that no current operational TTY contract remained, but the post-push observable disproved that claim. The new gate checks both required positive language and explicit stale phrases rather than relying on a partial search. Since current packaged scripts/docs/tests changed after beta.86 acceptance, a new beta.87 candidate and live acceptance are required.

## 2026-08-15 beta.86 live effective-tool projection repair

**Live finding.** beta.85 completed its final fast/full/frozen-candidate gates, direct-Node install-only check, owner-authorized activation, exact activated-package OAuth canary, and live Worker/service/relay diagnostics. `server_info` correctly reported an authenticated owner with full effective policy and 54 effective tools. A live `list_local_applications` call nevertheless returned `discovery=true` while projecting `open=false`, `accessibility_inspection=false`, `structured_accessibility_actions=false`, and `window_screenshot=false`. The mismatch was therefore inside the local runtime's capability/routing projection rather than the account/Worker authorization layer or the actual ToolExecutor gate.

**Causal mechanism.** `buildAuthorityContext()` correctly intersects the daemon and account policies, but that intersection deliberately carries the synthetic profile name `custom` even when its capability bits are identical to canonical full. `runtime.effectiveToolNames()` then re-ran `toolNamesForPolicy()` over that synthetic policy. The `full` availability class intentionally requires the literal canonical `full` profile as well as full capability bits, so the owner projection collapsed from 54 to 37 tools. Actual execution did not use this derived set for authorization: ToolExecutor first applies the daemon `PolicyGate` and then the account-role gate, so the defect primarily corrupted capability/routing metadata and any consumers that trust `effectiveToolNames()`.

**Repair.** `runtime.effectiveToolNames()` now derives its result from the same two authoritative executable gates: the daemon PolicyGate's allowed names intersected with the authenticated account role's allowed names. Local calls receive the daemon PolicyGate set directly. This avoids inferring tool availability from a synthetic policy label, preserves every custom-daemon restriction, and makes owner/full projection agree with the 54-tool authorization contract. The application integration test now exercises both relay reviewer and relay owner inventories: reviewer keeps read-only discovery, projected paths, and no launch/UI capabilities; owner must retain launch, Accessibility, and window-observation capabilities.

**Release consequence.** beta.85 remains useful live evidence that activation/canary/service/relay paths were healthy, but it was intentionally not accepted after the projection mismatch. The packaged runtime/test/documentation bytes change for beta.86, so all beta.85 candidate evidence is non-transferable to beta.86.

## 2026-08-15 beta.85 authorization and read-only discovery review

**Owner-requested policy correction.** The beta.84 source had completed live acceptance, merge, and exact-head hosted validation, but no Git tag/GitHub Release/npm publication had occurred when the owner rejected two workflow restrictions as too strict. The first required GitHub publication to have TTY-backed stdin/stdout/stderr even when the owner had explicitly authorized the operation in conversation. That rule was an anti-accident ceremony, not a meaningful same-user authentication boundary: code already executing as the owner's OS user can emulate terminal presence. The new contract therefore treats an explicit current-task owner request as sufficient user authorization and requires the repository command's explicit `--owner-confirm` acknowledgement instead of a TTY. This does not turn version changes into standing publication authority; npm publication, global/registry installation, Worker/service mutation, credential mutation, and source publication still require a specific owner request, and an agent must not race an operation the owner says they will execute themselves.

**Publication guard analysis.** Removing TTY presence does not remove the release-integrity state machine. `github-release.mjs` still requires a clean checkout on `main`, fetches the remote, verifies the version channel and changelog, creates an integrity-pinned hardened npm session, runs the complete verification plan and synchronized-version check, verifies exact local candidate acceptance (and soak for stable), requires `HEAD === origin/main`, requires successful CI for that exact commit, stages only the accepted candidate tarball, rejects conflicting local/remote tags, publishes through trusted absolute `git`/`gh` executables, verifies the GitHub asset SHA-256 through REST, waits for release metadata convergence, and serializes publication through the owner-state lock rooted in the common Git directory. Missing `--owner-confirm` fails before fetch or remote mutation. Concurrency, digest, exact-commit, and remote-state guards therefore remain the security/integrity controls; terminal shape is now observational metadata only.

**Application-discovery finding.** `resolve_task_capabilities` previously returned `application_discovery.available=false` with `reason=effective_authority` to delegated accounts because `list_local_applications` was classified as `full`. That bundled a low-impact inventory read with high-impact desktop control. The tool only enumerates installed application/launcher metadata and does not read documents or UI contents; restricted policy also projects every external absolute path through `<external-path:…>`. The catalog now classifies it as `always`, and account-access revision 4 records the resulting reviewer/editor/operator capability change. The outer runtime authorization pipeline remains intact: daemon policy is asserted first, relay calls then pass `accountAccessGate.assert(role, tool)` before handler invocation, and operation/audit middleware still runs normally.

**Delegated path-projection defect.** The first fast-plan run after widening discovery found a stale integration assertion, and strengthening that assertion exposed a real implementation bug rather than merely an outdated test. `AppAutomationManager.listApplications(args, context)` used `context` for cancellation and scanning but projected both application paths and discovery-warning paths through `this.displayPath(path)` without forwarding the request context. The runtime injects `displayPath` as an authority-sensitive function; omitting `context` therefore fell back to local owner/full policy on a full daemon and could expose an absolute installed-application path to a delegated account. All three application result projections now pass the originating context, including `openApplication`'s resolved path for consistency. The integration fixture executes `list_local_applications` as an authenticated reviewer against both a valid application root and an invalid discovery root, then requires application and warning paths to omit the private temporary root while still discovering the named app.

**Capability and routing separation.** Simply widening the catalog exposed a second architectural problem: the old bootstrap returned the raw application backend capability object whenever discovery was permitted, which would have told a reviewer that launch, Accessibility inspection/actions, screenshots, and background visual input were available. A dedicated `application-capability-projection.mjs` now intersects each advertised backend capability with the effective tool set. A further direct-result review found the same raw backend capability object was still returned by `list_local_applications` itself; the runtime now injects the same authority-aware projector into `AppAutomationManager`, so both bootstrap metadata and the direct inventory result agree with the account's effective tool set. `execution-routing.mjs` likewise separates the one-tool `application-discovery` route from the actionable `application` route. Direct regressions prove a discovery-only role receives inventory without UI authority, while full authority preserves the complete backend. A later governance pass deliberately invalidated an in-progress full check after noticing that the new module was only transitively packaged and had no explicit architecture budget. It is now an explicit package-inventory item, architecture boundary module with a 50-line ceiling, strict-JS typecheck root, and 100% function/branch critical-coverage target. Its extraction also preserved the existing 100-line ceiling on `runtime-capabilities.mjs` instead of raising that budget.

**Privacy and scope decision.** The wider inventory intentionally reveals installed application names and bundle/launcher identifiers to authenticated delegated roles; those values are now documented as device metadata rather than hidden capability state. Raw external paths remain projected for restricted authority. The review did **not** generalize this change to every read-only tool: `inspect_local_application`, browser tab/source/semantic inspection, and similar reads can expose window text, URLs, authenticated DOM, or other private content, so retaining `full` authority is a privacy boundary rather than unnecessary ceremony. `MULTI_ACCOUNT.md`, application/context/privacy guidance, generated policy reference, and generated 54-tool reference now describe that distinction.

**Independent review evidence.** Account authorization, agent-context routing, policy, runtime-handler inventory, runtime-boundary behavior, full local self-test, release-publication guard behavior, release-state diagnostics, architecture/source graph, lint, strict local/Worker typecheck, structured logging, security audit, property-based security tests, current-tree privacy, reachable-history privacy, npm package modes, consumer-package security, and CycloneDX validation were rerun while reviewing this change. Current privacy scanning covers 659 tracked/unignored files; reachable-history scanning covers 3733 blobs and 145 commit messages; package inspection contains 462 files; the consumer artifact has 11 production dependencies/8 SBOM components; the workspace CycloneDX graph has 117 components/118 dependencies. The new source file initially had mode `0600`, which package review identified and corrected to the existing `0644` source contract. No current operational documentation retains the removed `--owner-terminal-confirm`/mandatory-TTY rule; historical changelog/audit entries remain untouched as then-true records.

**Release consequence.** These are packaged policy/runtime/tooling/documentation changes, so beta.84's acceptance cannot authorize beta.85. A new beta.85 candidate must run fresh fast/full/frozen-candidate/install-only verification, then be activated and observed with the widened application-discovery contract before acceptance, review, merge, and any explicitly authorized source publication.

## 2026-08-15 beta.84 ChatGPT host-routing and raw-browser-source review

**Host-routing finding.** The repeated statements that Machine Bridge had been "directly disabled by the platform" were tested rather than accepted as root-cause evidence. The running beta.82 server returned owner/full authority with 54 effective tools, a ready daemon and relay, and healthy fixed filesystem/process/shell/resource probes; it also correctly reported that final host tool exposure is not known to the server. ChatGPT workspace administration showed the `machine-mcp` custom app enabled. One fresh Work conversation launched through the app surface successfully invoked `server_info`; a second fresh Work conversation without an initially visible app pill also successfully routed an explicit `machine-mcp server_info` request. Both returned beta.82 with daemon readiness verified. The two quoted failure statements were independently found in the same older conversation. This disproves a blanket current server/platform disable but does not reveal the host's private pre-dispatch decision. Conversation/surface app routing, a stale per-host action/tool snapshot, host filtering, connector-gateway routing, client routing, or platform policy remain possible host-owned causes until host-side evidence distinguishes them.

**Diagnostic repair.** Runtime diagnostics and server instructions now name conversation/surface app-routing state and stale host action/tool snapshots as host-side possibilities for a call that never returns a structured Machine Bridge result. They retain the stronger invariant introduced in beta.79: such a failure is not observable by Machine Bridge and must not be attributed to a platform without evidence. Client guidance adds a differential test: if a fresh supported conversation can invoke the same app/tool while an older conversation fails before dispatch, that is evidence against a blanket Machine Bridge outage and directs investigation to the host conversation/surface/snapshot layer. Workspace app enablement and the live server catalog are documented as distinct from host-cached action definitions; app refresh/review is required after action/schema changes where the host provides that workflow.

**Raw browser-source privacy finding.** A raw-source read against an authenticated application during this investigation demonstrated that serialized DOM can carry hidden bootstrap/session/account/authentication values not visible in rendered text. No live value from that observation is copied into this audit, tests, logs, or release evidence. The runtime continues to return raw site-controlled markup by design rather than applying an incomplete token scrubber that would both break source semantics and miss arbitrary application formats. The safer contract is explicit minimization: `browser_get_source` now warns that the entire result is sensitive and is for markup-required tasks only; `browser_inspect_page` or `computer_observe` is the default for semantic/actionability work. Routing tests pin the warning, and privacy/security/local-automation documentation records the same boundary.

**Independent review.** The follow-up sweep rechecked empty catches, exception-to-success transitions, structured logging, current/history privacy boundaries, destructive path containment, the beta.83 Windows state-root fix, platform-specific tail fixtures, TODO/FIXME/HACK markers, source/documentation drift, and browser-extension growth ceilings. The reviewed empty catches remain cleanup or fail-closed ambiguity paths rather than swallowed success. No second confirmed mixed-Windows-path destructive containment defect or new Windows tail portability defect was found statically. The large packaged browser-operation/page-automation modules remain a maintainability hotspot but are already bounded by executable architecture ceilings; this release does not refactor them without a concrete behavior defect.

**Release consequence.** beta.83 was never owner-activated or accepted. Because these diagnostic, tool-metadata, and documentation bytes are shipped in the package, beta.83 evidence is non-transferable and beta.84 must repeat fast/full/candidate/install-only validation before any owner activation.

## 2026-08-15 beta.83 Windows state-root current-CLI containment

**Hosted evidence.** The exact beta.82 candidate completed local focused review, the 102-task fast plan, two independent 129-task full/candidate gates, direct-Node install-only verification, owner activation, the activated-package OAuth canary, live Worker/service/relay/browser/privacy verification, exact acceptance, commit, guarded push, and PR update. Exact-head hosted Windows then directly vindicated the beta.81 launchd repair: `service-platform:test` completed successfully in 0.5 seconds. The same run also passed tasks 112-116 before the first failure at task 117 `self-test`. The state phase expected `validateStateRootForRemoval()` to reject a synthetic state root after `process.argv[1]` was moved underneath its release-channel runtime tree, but the current-CLI containment guard returned false.

**Causal mechanism.** State-root removal first resolves the existing target with ordinary `realpathSync`.
