The current-entrypoint guard was added later and canonicalized only the entrypoint through `canonicalizePotentialPath()`, whose existing ancestor is resolved with `realpathSync.native`. On Windows, equivalent drive paths can occupy ordinary and extended/native namespaces. A control experiment using `node:path.win32` proves that `relative("D:\\root", "\\\\?\\D:\\root\\child")` is absolute, while comparing the same two paths after both use the native namespace yields the expected relative child. Feeding the mixed pair into the existing containment predicate therefore skips the live-runtime protection. This is a production destructive-operation safety defect exposed by the hosted fixture, not merely a portability expectation error.

**Repair and invariant.** `currentEntrypointInsideStateRoot()` now canonicalizes both `root` and `process.argv[1]` through the same potential-path canonicalizer in one transaction. If that canonicalization fails, both operands fall back together to `path.resolve()`; the guard never mixes canonical path families. The existing synthetic state-root fixture remains unchanged in purpose and gains platform-independent `path.win32` assertions that pin the namespace mechanism. Architecture regression requires root and entrypoint canonicalization plus `path.relative(canonicalRoot, resolved)`. No state marker, reserved namespace, active lock/job/daemon inventory, workspace exclusion, generation-bound quarantine, or recursive deletion rule is relaxed.

**Release consequence.** beta.82 remains the currently live accepted runtime, but its exact hosted Windows validation failed and the newly fixed packaged runtime byte invalidates beta.82 acceptance for any subsequent commit. beta.83 must repeat the complete candidate/live evidence lifecycle before another guarded push. The still-unreached Windows tail after `self-test` remains hosted evidence to obtain rather than an assumed success.

## 2026-08-15 beta.82 independent recovery-evidence and verification-boundary review

**Candidate invalidation and review stance.** The beta.81 launchd stop repair had already passed its targeted regression, the 102-task fast plan, a 129-task full gate, a second frozen 129-task candidate gate, and direct-Node install-only verification. It had not been owner-activated, accepted, committed, or pushed. This review deliberately treated that prepared tarball as an untrusted baseline rather than release evidence. New packaged findings therefore invalidate beta.81 instead of being layered onto an already-approved candidate; its launchd change remains unchanged and is carried into beta.82.

**Recovered-success privacy defect.** `runtime-activation.mjs` previously derived successful recovery detail from the lower-layer exception message, bounded only by whitespace removal and a character ceiling. That detail propagated through CLI JSON and owner output, and both local-candidate and published-prerelease writers could persist it in schema-2 activation evidence. A filesystem path, endpoint, account-shaped string, or credential-shaped value emitted by a dependency could therefore cross from local causal state into durable release evidence even though the recovered outcome itself was already classified by one of only three stable reasons. The repair introduces one shared `activation-recovery.mjs` contract mapping `relay_authentication_failed`, `autostart_install_failed`, and `autostart_start_failed` to fixed canonical detail. Runtime settlement, persistent-child validation, activation-record validation, and current writers use this source of truth. Historical schema-2 records still fail closed on malformed recovery metadata but normalize any otherwise bounded legacy detail to the canonical reason-owned text before returning it; current writers therefore cannot re-persist the legacy raw string. Tests inject `/private/tmp/operator-secret`, a reserved `.invalid` URL, and publication-safe credential placeholders at runtime, IPC, writer, and disk-read boundaries and require those values to disappear from public/persisted recovery evidence.

**Process-settlement defense in depth.** A second review of successful/error projection found a narrower but real weakness below the UI wrappers. Generic non-replayable pre-spawn settlement converted an unclassified spawn exception into an exposed `BridgeError` whose message was the lower-layer exception text. Current application/browser callers usually reclassified that reason into their own fixed message, but privacy depended on every future caller remembering the second wrapper. The low-level boundary now emits only `process failed before spawn`, retains the original exception as local `cause`, and keeps the stable `process_failed_before_spawn` reason. Already-classified `BridgeError` values remain unchanged, and once process start has occurred the existing non-replayable unknown settlement is still authoritative. Regression tests deliberately place a private-looking path in the lower-layer exception and verify it is absent from the public message/details.

**Verification architecture gap.** The review then inspected whether the new shared security boundary was actually protected by repository-wide gates. It exposed a pre-existing blind spot: the package lint command and the correctness ESLint file set omitted `src/shared`, while `scripts/syntax-check.mjs` also omitted that root. Shared modules received complexity limits but could contain an undefined binding or unused import without the advertised production correctness gate seeing it. `src/shared` is now part of both the dynamic syntax scanner and correctness lint CLI/configuration; `lint-gate-test` proves undefined-binding and unused-import rejection on a shared-module path. The activation-recovery module is additionally a strict `tsconfig.local.json` root, an explicit npm package inventory item, a 70-line architecture-budget target, and a critical-coverage target. The fresh coverage run measures 4/4 functions and 28/28 branches (100%/100%). Architecture source guards prevent raw recovery exception text, loss of these gates, or restoration of the removed pre-spawn raw-message helper.

**Package-mode and documentation findings.** Explicit package testing of the new module initially failed because the newly created source file had owner-only mode `0600`. The existing package contract correctly rejected it; the file was normalized to `0644` and the 461-file package manifest test then passed. No package-mode rule was relaxed. Separately, `RELEASING.md` and `ARCHITECTURE.md` still claimed schema-1 prerelease activation records using the legacy `previous` field were accepted and normalized. Git history shows that beta.33 once provided that compatibility, while the beta.62 hardening deliberately made current activation reads schema-2-only and installed executable architecture guards against restoring the legacy reader. The current documentation now matches the executable contract: schema-1 files are historical evidence, not supported operational inputs. Historical changelog/audit entries retain their then-true description rather than being rewritten.

**Platform, logging, redundancy, and privacy sweep.** The repeated hosted-Windows failures were generalized into a review rule rather than another single fixture patch: a test that injects logical `darwin`/`win32` behavior must not silently use incompatible UID, POSIX mode-bit, signal-delivery, path, or service-manager semantics from the physical host. The remaining Windows platform tail after `service-platform:test` was reviewed for the same pattern; macOS-only trust/native-build fixtures already skip Windows, and process-tree tests assert final settlement through the Windows `taskkill /T` path rather than requiring JavaScript SIGTERM delivery. No additional confirmed portability defect was identified statically. Structured logging and security-audit regressions pass; operational events remain low-cardinality and do not record tool arguments or process output. Empty catches in production were reviewed as cleanup, best-effort detach/release, or preflight probes whose failure later becomes an explicit ambiguous/identity failure; no branch was found that turns an unclassified operational failure into success. A source-import reachability pass initially identified only intentional dynamic/entry modules such as CLI, job runner, audit worker, service restart handoff, trusted GitHub CLI, and Worker index; no orphan production module was found.

**Security/privacy evidence.** Current-tree privacy checking passes over 657 tracked/unignored files with the local denylist enabled. Reachable-history privacy checking passes over 3681 blobs and 142 commit messages. Runtime privacy tests, structured logging tests, security-audit tests, consumer-package security, package manifest validation, generated policy/tool reference checks, project metadata checks, and CycloneDX validation (117 components / 118 dependencies) pass. The review did not find an additional confirmed tracked credential, private key, stale current beta/channel snapshot, TODO/FIXME/HACK debt, or active schema-1 release path. The largest remaining maintainability hotspot is still the packaged browser extension: `browser-operations.js` and `page-automation.js` remain large even though executable line ceilings now prevent silent growth. A future staged decomposition around tab/screenshot lifecycle, mutation settlement, and page semantic/action execution would reduce review density, but this release-hardening pass does not refactor those high-risk files without a concrete behavioral defect.

**Release consequence.** All beta.81 candidate, full-gate, and install-only evidence predates these packaged changes and is intentionally non-transferable. beta.82 must repeat the complete frozen candidate lifecycle from the reviewed bytes; no owner activation, live canary, acceptance, commit, push, tag, GitHub Release, Worker publication, or npm publication is implied by this source review.

## 2026-08-15 beta.81 launchd stop target-resolution portability

**Hosted evidence.** The exact beta.80 candidate completed local fast/full/candidate validation, direct-Node disposable install, owner activation, the activated-package OAuth canary, live Worker/service/relay/browser/privacy verification, acceptance, commit, and guarded push. Exact-head Windows then directly proved both prior portability repairs: `resource-admission:test` completed in 3.5 seconds and `macos-background-input:test` completed in 0.3 seconds. The platform plan continued to `service-platform:test`, where its first synthetic `stopLaunchdService()` call failed with `launchd requires a numeric user id` before the mocked status reader could classify the service as unavailable.

**Causal mechanism.** `stopLaunchdService()` computed both `launchdDomainTarget()` and `launchdServiceTarget()` before reading current service status. That ordering was stronger than the operational contract requires: the status-unavailable and safely-unloaded branches must not mutate launchd at all, so they do not need a launchd target or user id. On macOS `process.getuid()` masks the unnecessary dependency; hosted Windows has no `process.getuid()`, so a platform-independent synthetic launchd contract exposed the eager resolution. Loaded-service synthetic cases have the same host dependency unless the test provides a deterministic UID.

**Repair and invariant.** Target resolution now occurs only after status confirms the service is loaded and a `bootout` mutation is actually required. `stopLaunchdService()` accepts an internal explicit UID dependency for deterministic synthetic contracts; production callers omit it and retain the same `process.getuid()` default. The regression deliberately supplies an invalid UID to the no-mutation unavailable/absent cases, proving they never resolve targets, while loaded cases inject UID 501 and assert the exact `launchctl bootout gui/501/dev.machine-bridge-mcp.daemon` command. Provider selection, launchd label/domain semantics, stop observation, plist fallback, and production identity remain unchanged. Because packaged source changed after beta.80 acceptance, beta.81 requires fresh exact candidate and live evidence.

## 2026-08-15 beta.80 Windows synthetic macOS-helper cache portability

**Hosted evidence.** The exact beta.79 candidate completed local fast/full/candidate verification, direct-Node install-only validation, owner activation, activated-package OAuth canary, live Worker/service/relay/browser/privacy verification, acceptance, commit, and guarded push. Exact-head Windows then ran through `resource-admission:test` successfully in 3.8 seconds, directly validating the beta.78 host-native cwd fixture repair. The platform run failed later in `macos-background-input:test` at `helper compiled more than once for the same source digest` with two observed synthetic compiler invocations instead of one.

**Causal mechanism.** The production helper cache is macOS-only and deliberately trusts a cached native binary only when `executableRegularFile()` proves a regular non-symlink file owned by the current/root user, with at least one POSIX execute bit and no group/other write bits. The deterministic unit fixture forces `platform: "darwin"` so the macOS state machine can run on every CI host, but it still uses the real host filesystem for temporary cache files. Node's Windows filesystem permission model does not let `chmod(0o700)` establish the POSIX execute-bit evidence consumed by that trust predicate. A second `ensureBuilt()` therefore correctly refuses to reuse the synthetic Windows file and invokes the fake compiler again. This is a fixture/evidence mismatch, not evidence that the production macOS cache lost digest or permission validation.

**Repair and invariant.** Production `MacosBackgroundInputService` and `executableRegularFile()` remain unchanged. The cross-platform fixture always requires at least one compilation and continues to validate the hashed owner-local source copy, temporary-source cleanup, helper payload/result projection, coercion rejection, probe semantics, and mutation-settlement behavior. The exact one-compile cache-reuse assertion runs only when the host can supply POSIX mode evidence; hosted macOS still exercises that invariant against the real trust predicate. Because the packaged regression changed after beta.79 acceptance, beta.80 requires a fresh exact candidate and live-evidence lifecycle.

## 2026-08-15 beta.79 diagnostic attribution, Computer Use privacy, and architecture review

**Platform-disable diagnosis.** The reported statement that a retried local execution channel was "directly disabled by the platform" was not supported by Machine Bridge evidence. In the same session, authenticated `server_info` reached a ready beta.77 daemon through the relay with the owner effective catalog, and `diagnose_runtime` reached the local runtime and passed its fixed filesystem/process/shell/resource probes. The server also explicitly cannot observe the host's final post-relay tool filtering. The defect was therefore an attribution contract: `runtime-diagnostics.mjs` described a call blocked before any Machine Bridge response as `host/platform or connector gateway`, which made an unobservable pre-runtime failure look diagnosed. The repaired projection separates fact from hypothesis: successful delivery is positively stated; a pre-response refusal is `not observable by Machine Bridge` and lists host filtering, connector gateway, client routing, and platform policy only as possible causes requiring host-side evidence.

**Successful-result privacy boundary.** Independent review found that Computer Use could copy lower-layer `error.message` text into successful result fields such as `dispatch_error`, `post_observation_error`, verification detail, or exposed preflight details. This path bypasses ordinary tool-error sanitization because the overall MCP call itself succeeds. The public projection now emits stable settlement/reason text plus `error_class` only. Raw causes stay attached only as local exception causes where useful. Tests deliberately place `/private/tmp/operator-secret` in ambiguous trusted-input, post-dispatch wait, cancellation, and document-state errors and assert that no public Computer Use result/details contain it. Browser-extension direct tools already have their independent `browser-error-boundary.js` allowlist/canonicalization, so this closes the composition-layer gap rather than replacing that boundary.

**Responsibility boundaries.** The review identified two orchestration files whose previous line ceilings had become ineffective: `computer-use.mjs` was within only a few lines of its 2670-line ceiling, while `app-automation.mjs` mixed Node authorization/settlement logic with roughly 350 lines of fixed JXA implementation. Request validation moved to `computer-use-arguments.mjs`; fixed Accessibility JXA moved to `app-automation-macos-jxa.mjs`. The main modules now have materially lower ceilings, the extracted modules have their own budgets, the browser/security guard reads the JXA module directly for selector-index and secure-field suppression, and the largest extension execution modules now have explicit growth ceilings. Behavior tests remain the authority; the extraction does not add caller-selected script/CDP surfaces or weaken mutation settlement.

**Documentation, secrets, and history.** The live upgrade guide contradicted its own "current path only" rule by retaining a `Current beta.61 transition` heading and an old npm-channel snapshot. That text is now explicitly historical migration rationale with no current-channel claim. Architecture/local-automation/Computer Use/client documentation was updated to match the new module and diagnostic boundaries. Static secret-pattern review found no obvious private-key/API-token material, the tracked-tree privacy check passed, and the history privacy scan passed across 3650 reachable blobs and 140 commit messages. Existing changelog/audit history is retained because it is release evidence rather than stale operational guidance.

**Release consequence.** The exact beta.78 candidate had passed local fast/full/candidate/install-only validation but had not been owner-activated or accepted when this review changed packaged source. That candidate is invalidated rather than retroactively accepted. beta.79 must repeat the complete candidate and live-evidence lifecycle from frozen source.

## 2026-08-15 beta.78 resource-admission cwd portability

**Observed hosted defect.** The exact accepted beta.77 candidate passed local fast/full verification, owner activation, activated-package OAuth canary, live Worker/service/relay/browser checks, acceptance, and guarded push. Exact-head Windows no longer failed at the beta.76 Wrangler cleanup-race fixture; the platform plan continued until `resource-admission:test` failed at its literal shell cwd assertion. Production returned `D:\\tmp\\project-a`, while the fixture expected the POSIX source literal `/tmp/project-a`.

**Causal mechanism.** `resourceCommandEffectiveCwd()` intentionally canonicalizes the request cwd with Node `path.resolve()` and applies the same host-native canonicalization to trusted literal shell `cd` targets. That invariant is required so resource contention keys refer to the actual host path rather than preserving syntax from the shell payload. On POSIX, `resolve("/tmp/project-a")` remains `/tmp/project-a`; on Windows the same rooted input resolves to the current drive-qualified absolute path. The fixture compared the raw input token instead of the function's canonical-path contract. Its adjacent dynamic-cwd fallback assertion had the same latent assumption for `/tmp/shared` and would have failed next.

**Repair and invariant.** The regression now imports `resolve` from `node:path` and compares both results against `resolve("/tmp/project-a")` and `resolve("/tmp/shared")`. No resource-admission production code, contention scoping rule, shell parser, path trust boundary, or resource quota behavior changes. Because the packaged regression changed after beta.77 acceptance, beta.78 requires fresh candidate and live evidence.

## 2026-08-15 beta.77 Wrangler cleanup-race fixture portability

**Observed hosted defect.** The exact beta.76 candidate passed local fast/full verification, owner activation, the activated-package OAuth canary, live Worker/service/relay/browser checks, acceptance, and guarded push. Exact-head Windows then directly validated both earlier portability repairs: `process-output:test` completed in 1.3 seconds and `managed-job-boundary:test` completed in 0.2 seconds. The platform run failed later in `worker-types-generator:test` with `Wrangler cleanup-race fixture did not cross the completion grace`. The assertion was not observing Wrangler lifecycle state directly; it expected the fixture process's `process.on("SIGTERM")` handler to run and write a stderr marker after the parent requested cleanup.

**Causal mechanism.** That marker is not a portable cleanup-request witness. Node documents `SIGTERM` as unsupported for ordinary signal delivery on Windows even though a listener can be registered; `subprocess.kill("SIGTERM")` uses Windows termination emulation and unconditionally terminates the target rather than reproducing POSIX signal-handler delivery. The production lifecycle's actual invariant is different: after a valid completion marker, failure to exit within the bounded completion grace must request cleanup; if the child races that request and still closes successfully before forced cleanup, the completed command remains successful. Binding that invariant to child-side SIGTERM output made the fixture host-dependent and tested the operating system instead of the lifecycle transition.

**Repair and invariant.** `runCompletedWranglerCommand()` now resolves one internal `killChild` dependency, defaulting exactly to `target.kill(signal)`, and uses it for the existing graceful and forced cleanup calls. The cleanup-race regression injects only this dependency: it records the requested signal, treats the graceful request as delivered without sending an OS signal, and allows the real fixture child to take its pre-existing 250 ms normal-exit path. The assertion therefore proves that the 20 ms completion grace caused exactly one `SIGTERM` cleanup request while the command still settles successfully from the child's real zero exit. Hanging fixtures continue to use the default OS kill path, preserving behavior coverage for bounded cleanup. No default production timeout, signal, success classification, or forced-cleanup policy changes. Because packaged bytes changed after beta.76 acceptance, beta.77 requires fresh candidate and live evidence.

## 2026-08-15 beta.76 managed-job Windows boundary fixture portability

**Observed hosted defect.** The exact accepted beta.75 candidate passed the local fast/full candidate gates, owner activation, activated-package OAuth canary, exact protocol-3 browser handshake, live background-tab Computer Use click/hover checks, consumed-snapshot rejection, runtime/privacy audit, acceptance, and guarded push. On the beta.75 exact-head Windows platform run, the preceding beta.75 repair was directly vindicated: `process-output:test` completed successfully in 1.2 seconds. The job then failed in `managed-job-boundary:test` at its explicit-directory-mode assertion with `actual: null`, `expected: 448 (0o700)`.

**Causal mechanism.** `withPinnedManagedJobDirectory()` has an intentional platform split. POSIX opens an already-existing directory through `O_RDONLY | O_NOFOLLOW | O_DIRECTORY` with mode `0o700`, pins the descriptor, and cross-checks descriptor/path/canonical filesystem identity. The Windows branch does not invoke `openSync`; it rejects symbolic-link/non-directory state through `lstat` and applies the existing canonical/path device-and-inode identity checks. `docs/TESTING.md` already describes the explicit descriptor mode requirement specifically as **POSIX directory pinning**. The fixture nevertheless installed an `openSync` observer and asserted that it saw `0o700` on every host, so a correct Windows execution left the observer at `null` and failed. This is a test-portability mismatch, not evidence that beta.75 weakened managed-job directory permissions or identity validation.

**Repair and invariant.** The real-host descriptor-mode assertion now runs only when `process.platform !== "win32"`. A second platform-independent regression explicitly calls the resolver with `platform: "win32"` and an `openSync` stub that records-and-throws if invoked; the resolver must return the same canonical job root while proving that Windows did not enter the POSIX descriptor path. Production managed-job code remains unchanged. Because this packaged test change follows beta.75 acceptance and push, beta.76 requires a fresh candidate, activation, canary, live verification, acceptance, guarded push, and hosted exact-head validation.

## 2026-08-15 beta.75 Windows process-session fixture portability

**Observed hosted defect.** The exact accepted beta.74 candidate was owner-activated and passed its packaged deployed OAuth canary, stable-path protocol-3 extension handshake, live background-tab Computer Use observation/click/hover checks, one-shot replay rejection, and runtime/privacy audit before guarded source push. Exact-head Ubuntu/full and macOS/platform+install passed; package-audit, governance, dependency review, workflow policy, JavaScript/TypeScript CodeQL, Actions CodeQL, and the zero-unaccepted-CodeQL gates also passed. Windows alone failed `check:platform`: `process-output:test` ran for about 31 seconds and `testSessionAuthorityRevocation` finally surfaced `process-session shutdown was incomplete; runtime ownership must be retained` from its cleanup path.

**Causal mechanism.** The failure was produced by the fixture's synthetic termination handles, not by a demonstrated slow real Windows child shutdown. On Windows, `terminateProcessTree()` requests tree termination by spawning `taskkill.exe /PID ... /T /F` and returns true when that helper is successfully spawned; whether a synthetic target PID exists is settled asynchronously. The fixture instead used fake children whose `kill()` returned false and assumed that this would prove delivery failure synchronously on every platform. As a result, the Windows forced-kill assertion received a successful request result, threw before deleting the fake session, and entered the outer `finally`. Final `clearAndWait()` then waited on that impossible fake session and threw its own teardown error, masking the original assertion. The earlier beta.72 test-only 15-second manager budget made the same platform mismatch slower rather than fixing it; the approximately 31-second hosted duration is consistent with two 15-second synthetic settlement waits plus fixture overhead.

**Repair and invariant.** The failed-delivery branches now inject `manager.terminateTree = () => false` only for the duration of the synthetic assertion, so they test `ProcessSessionManager`'s definite-delivery-failure contract independently of the host tree-kill mechanism. Each fake session is removed in its own `finally` block and the original terminator is always restored, preventing a failed assertion from contaminating final cleanup or replacing the actual failure. The separate delivered-but-unsettled case keeps an explicit 100 ms synthetic deadline and also cleans itself in `finally`. The authority fixture no longer overrides `terminationSettlementWaitMs`, so its real surviving child is again cleaned with the unchanged production-default five-second settlement contract. No runtime termination semantics or retryability contract is relaxed. Because this packaged test change follows beta.74 acceptance and push, beta.75 requires a fresh candidate, activation, canary, live verification, acceptance, guarded push, and hosted exact-head validation.

## 2026-08-14 beta.74 bounded Chromium DevTools session settlement

**Observed live defect.** The exact beta.73 candidate was owner-activated, its candidate-bound deployed OAuth canary passed, activation published the extension into the new stable release-channel directory, and Chrome successfully loaded that directory and completed an exact protocol-3 `3.0.0-beta.73` handshake with Computer Use/CDP/trusted-input capabilities present. Browser acceptance still failed on a real background synthetic tab. `browser_inspect_page` repeatedly proved the tab document was complete and readable while `computer_observe` intermittently exceeded its broker deadline. A snapshot-bound click later hit the browser preflight five-second timeout, so that action was not replayed. The live evidence therefore separated extension installation/pairing and ordinary page scripting from the Computer Use DevTools path.

**Causal mechanism and repair.** `devtools-session.js` serialized one debugger session per tab but awaited raw `chrome.debugger.attach`, `sendCommand`, and `detach` promises with no independent settlement deadline. The broker can request cancellation after its own timeout, but a pending `sendCommand` cannot reach the next `throwIfCancelled()` check and keeps the per-tab queue unresolved; later CDP observations then wait behind the same unresolved session. Beta.74 bounds attach and each command at five seconds and detach cleanup at 1.5 seconds. A late successful attach after an attach timeout schedules best-effort detach. Observation code treats the internal DevTools-timeout marker as fatal for that CDP capture instead of swallowing it as an optional component failure, allowing the existing `observe_computer` semantic fallback to settle and release the queue.

**Mutation safety and regression.** The shared session is also used by trusted browser input, so the deadline is not treated as replay authorization. `devtools-input` already sets `dispatchStarted=true` before invoking each Input command; a command timeout therefore remains `safeToFallback=false` and flows through the existing unknown-mutation settlement. Regression fixtures make a DevTools command promise never resolve and verify prompt timeout, detach, and successful reuse of the same tab queue; a separate trusted-hover fixture proves an `Input.dispatchMouseEvent` timeout remains post-dispatch/unknown rather than fallback-safe. Focused trusted-input, DevTools-observation, and browser Computer-observation tests pass. Because the fix changes packaged extension bytes after beta.73 activation/canary, beta.74 requires fresh candidate preparation, owner activation, canary, live browser verification, hosted checks, and acceptance.

## 2026-08-14 beta.73 stable unpacked-extension release path

**Observed release defect.** The exact beta.72 candidate was owner-activated and its packaged deployed OAuth canary passed, but browser acceptance remained blocked after the documented extension Reload. The extension itself was still installed under the pinned Machine Bridge extension ID; the broker's `extension_reload_required` state proved that an extension WebSocket candidate had reached the broker but had not completed a valid protocol-3 hello. Source review then exposed a release-state contradiction independent of Chrome UI visibility: local-candidate `extension_path` was the current package's `browser-extension` directory inside `release-channels/runtimes/v<version>-<sha>-<nonce>/`, while successful activation deliberately pruned every inactive versioned runtime. Chrome retains the source directory selected by **Load unpacked**, so a later Reload cannot move that installation to a newly created candidate directory. This violates the documented load-once/reload-on-upgrade contract and makes beta.72 unfit for acceptance even though its Worker/daemon/OAuth path is healthy.

**Repair and safety ordering.** Versioned local-candidate runtimes now resolve browser setup/status to one owner-only `release-channels/browser-extension` physical directory. Ordinary checkout/global runtimes remain package-local so development and globally installed package behavior are not silently redirected through release state. Candidate activation first proves exact Worker and background-daemon convergence, then copies the installed candidate extension to the stable directory. Source reads reject symbolic links and use opened regular-file descriptors; destination files are replaced atomically, stale files are removed, and `manifest.json` is replaced last so its `version_name` is the commit marker only after the rest of the candidate bytes are present. Inactive versioned runtimes are pruned only after that publication succeeds, and activation evidence is written afterward. State-root removal recognizes this bounded real-file namespace and still rejects symbolic links or other unexpected entry types.

**Regression and migration.** `candidate-runtime-store:test` proves that two candidate versions publish to exactly the same path, observes the old manifest while the new non-manifest bytes are already staged, verifies stale-file removal and final exact bytes, rejects source symlinks, and proves only versioned candidate packages are redirected to release state. Architecture guards bind live-convergence → stable-extension publication → inactive-runtime prune → activation-record ordering and require browser bridge/CLI routing through the shared path helper. Existing installations loaded from beta.72-or-earlier candidate-runtime paths require one manual **Load unpacked** migration to beta.73's reported stable path; browser-internal extension management remains outside Machine Bridge automation. Subsequent candidate upgrades use Reload on the same path, while explicit extension-icon confirmation remains required when replacing different browser-local pairing state.

## 2026-08-14 beta.71 browser action timing settlement follow-up

**Observed live defect.** The exact beta.70 candidate was owner-activated, matched its candidate provenance, passed the packaged deployed OAuth canary, and paired the packaged Chromium extension at protocol 3 with Computer Use/CDP capabilities present. A controlled loopback page then separated observation from mutation behavior. `computer_observe` returned a navigation-coherent DOM/Chromium Accessibility snapshot and ordinary read-only browser inspection remained healthy. A DOM `focus` action on the same control settled successfully, while DOM `click` and `hover` requests repeatedly reached the extension but exceeded their broker deadlines. The click effect was independently observed afterward on the page, proving that mutation could complete while its response remained unavailable. Machine Bridge's existing settlement boundary behaved correctly: the request became non-retryable unknown rather than a false success or a replay opportunity. That safety property prevented duplicate input, but the inability to obtain an ordinary confirmed browser mutation is a release blocker, so beta.70 was not accepted.

**Causal boundary and repair.** Both failing actions share deterministic scroll, stable-box, and pointer-hit preparation; `focus` does not. That preparation waited with page-world `setTimeout(50/100)`. Chromium is permitted to batch, intensively throttle, or freeze timers in background/hidden pages, so a short geometry poll could outlive Machine Bridge's foreground request deadline even though the page action later resumed and executed. The repair does not delete or weaken geometry/actionability checks. When the page module is running inside the packaged extension, short polling waits now cross `chrome.runtime.sendMessage` to a service-worker-only `machine_bridge_internal_delay` endpoint. The endpoint accepts only messages whose sender ID equals the running extension ID and only exact integer delays from 1 through 250 ms. A timing-service failure before mutation remains definite; after scroll or another side-effecting preparation step it flows through the existing unknown-mutation settlement. A renderer-timer fallback exists only when the extension runtime API is absent, which keeps the pure VM fixture usable without reintroducing the packaged background-tab dependency.

**Regression and release impact.** A test-first fixture disables renderer `setTimeout` while providing the internal extension delay service and requires DOM click to retain deterministic scrolling/stability and settle successfully; the pre-fix source fails that fixture at the exact renderer timer. Service-worker coverage independently rejects cross-extension and over-250-ms delay messages. Page automation, service worker, trusted input, browser request settlement, Computer Use, browser security/source guards, and module responsibility limits pass after the repair, followed by the 102-task fast plan. The repair changes packaged browser-extension bytes, so beta.70 candidate/canary evidence cannot authorize it. The unused beta.71 namespace was checked in npm, local/remote Git tags, and GitHub Releases before the version bump; beta.71 must repeat full candidate preparation, owner activation, deployed canary, live observation, and acceptance.

## 2026-08-14 beta.70 Computer Use integration and independent review

**Integration boundary.** Computer Use vNext was reviewed as a reference implementation rather than merged as a branch. The independently developed source had diverged into a parallel Machine Bridge trunk and still carried the older stateful MCP initialization/session/resumption design. Beta.70 instead keeps the current beta.69 codebase authoritative and ports the Computer Use capability onto the native request-scoped `2026-07-28` runtime. Remote HTTP initialization compatibility remains the bounded stateless adapter for `2025-06-18` and `2025-11-25`; it does not create or accept `Mcp-Session-Id`, recovery GET, `Last-Event-ID`, persisted replay/delivery rows, or initialization-owned authorization state. The beta.69 structured Git, metadata-boundary, support-projection, daemon-readiness, release, and runtime protections are retained rather than overwritten by the older parallel trunk.

**Computer Use contract.** The resulting product adds `computer_observe` and `computer_act` over the existing browser and application backends. Browser snapshots combine bounded executable DOM evidence with Chromium Accessibility/DOMSnapshot evidence, frame/document epochs, trusted-input bindings, and optional native MCP screenshots. macOS application snapshots combine Accessibility state with process-birth and owner-window evidence and optionally a window screenshot. Snapshot IDs are process-local opaque handles, not authorization; every call still passes normal policy/operation authorization. Mutation authority is one-shot: read-only preflight precedes an atomic snapshot claim, mutation handoff is attempted at most once, ambiguous lower-layer settlement is never automatically replayed, and post-state/continuation evidence is returned separately from dispatch settlement.

**Independent seam review.** Reference-source parity was not treated as sufficient proof because beta.70 also had to compose vNext with newer request settlement, process settlement, and 7 MiB result-budget boundaries. The review found that `post_screenshot_included` reported policy intent rather than whether native image content actually reached the result; screenshot capture failure could therefore claim an image that was absent. It now reflects actual returned image content. A second defect existed when an oversized post-action screenshot was removed by the post-capture budget before outer action-result fitting: the action remained safe and continuable, but the structured budget-omission reason could disappear. Both capture-level and outer action-level omission now converge on `tool_result_budget`, preserve dispatch/effect settlement and the bounded `post_snapshot_id`, and keep same-action replay disabled. The compact-result fallback was also found to manufacture that screenshot reason for a hypothetical semantic-only overflow; it now preserves the reason only when an image was actually omitted for the budget.

**Documentation and regression boundaries.** The page automation module emits snapshot version 3 with per-document epoch semantics, while local-automation and architecture documentation still named version 2; both are corrected and an architecture test now binds source and documentation versions. README incorrectly stated that all clients had no `initialize` handshake even though the current Worker intentionally supports stateless remote initialization compatibility, and the system overview simultaneously counted four authority questions as three and referred to nonexistent current MCP session state. Repository-hygiene tests now reject those stale positive claims while requiring the current native/stateless-compatibility model. Focused behavior fixtures were added for Computer Use result-budget downgrade, browser request settlement, BrowserOperationService including Computer Use adapter entrypoints, and ordinary/non-replayable fixed-process settlement. These fixtures participate in the normal fast plan and critical coverage instead of living only as ad hoc review probes.

**Candidate-gate resource race.** A later candidate rerun under unrelated concurrent machine jobs passed the first 119 full-plan tasks, then `full-access:test` failed while resource admission inspected a coordinator staging inode still owned by a live publisher. That state is legitimate for the short interval between complete-before-visible staging publication and cleanup; deleting it would violate ownership, but surfacing it immediately as an internal error made ordinary unrelated work spuriously fail. Live lease/waiter staging now carries the stable `MBM_RESOURCE_STAGING_BUSY` classification. `ResourceCoordinator.withLock()` releases its transaction generation and permits only four five-millisecond retries; a publisher that remains live still fails closed, while an ordinary short publication may settle. Exhausted staging or transaction-lock contention is projected through the existing retryable resource-admission unavailable result rather than an internal exception. The pre-existing dead-publisher identity/link-count recovery is unchanged. Focused resource-admission and process-admission regressions pass, the existing responsibility cap is preserved without increase, and the exact `full-access` real-machine fixture that exposed the race passes after the repair.

**Second independent post-closure review.** After the resource-staging repair passed 129/129 and an install-only closure candidate was prepared, a fresh review deliberately treated that candidate as a baseline rather than proof. It found that Computer Use snapshot TTL and application verification duration still used an injectable wall clock; the snapshot authority state is now extracted to `computer-use-snapshot-store.mjs`, defaults to `performance.now()`, clamps injected clock rollback, and the verification loop uses the shared monotonic deadline helper. AppAutomation already had a monotonic manager clock, but its resource-backed exact-value verification handle incorrectly used `Date.now()` for the 60-second secret-bearing fallback TTL; retention and pruning now use the same monotonic clock. The review also found that the extension already classified an oversized/unserializable successful mutation result as unknown, but the local broker did not attach structured settlement for that fixed message, and direct application wrappers discarded lower-level post-spawn settlement details. Both now preserve the non-replayable result together with bounded `side_effects_started`, `termination_requested`, and `effect_settlement` evidence. Architecture governance now includes every Computer Use core/observation/recovery/snapshot/native/browser adapter module plus extension size caps, and ties the duplicated local/extension 7 MiB byte ceiling to one executable drift guard. Critical coverage now measures the Computer Use kernel itself; snapshot-store coverage reaches 100% functions / 95.2% branches, browser Computer Use observation 92.0% / 75.9%, and the existing core observation/recovery/native paths remain above their new floors. Five cheap GUI security fixtures move from platform-only to fast, expanding the fast plan from 97 to 102 without changing the 121-task platform or 129-task full set. No credential, private-key, absolute user-home path, Computer Use content log, TODO/FIXME/HACK residue, or current positive session/resumption contract was found; one documentation-only internal `Codex/daily/ops` scratch-path assumption was generalized.

**Verification.** Before this independent pass, the beta.70 integration completed the then-current 94-task fast plan. After the review changes, direct Computer Use, browser observation/bridge, process-settlement, architecture, Markdown, and typecheck checks pass. The frozen critical-coverage run passes without lowering thresholds: `process-nonreplayable-settlement.mjs` measures 100% functions / 96.4% branches, `computer-use-result-budget.mjs` 100% / 90.7%, `browser-request-settlement.mjs` 100% / 100%, and `browser-operation-service.mjs` 83.3% / 79.4%, above its existing 80% function threshold. The expanded frozen fast plan passes 97/97 twice, including after the audit/README/overview corrections. The first 129-task full run passed tasks 1-123 and then correctly stopped at `package:test`: 17 files brought over with the Computer Use work still had owner-only `0600` checkout modes. Those non-executable source/documentation/native files were normalized to `0644` without relaxing the package gate; the targeted 455-file package manifest test passed, and the clean rerun then passed all 129/129 tasks including package, SBOM, install, stdio, Worker integration, and OAuth-browser verification. Privacy history scanning also passes over 651 tracked/unignored files, 3462 reachable history blobs, and 135 commit messages; both complete and production-only high-severity npm audits report zero vulnerabilities, while registry verification reports 117 packages with valid signatures and 38 with attestations. A closure-baseline beta.70 candidate was prepared after a 129/129 full gate and passed direct-Node install-only verification, but the second independent review changed packaged runtime bytes and therefore explicitly invalidated that tarball as release evidence. Candidate generation for the reviewed bytes occurs only after the new frozen gates; `.release-candidate/manifest.json` is the authoritative final digest rather than this audit prose. No owner activation, Worker/service replacement, acceptance, commit, push, tag, release, or npm publication is implied by these local review results.

## 2026-08-13 beta.69 bounded Git mutation and metadata-boundary follow-up

**Observed facts.** The beta.68 candidate completed the full owner-machine live verification sequence and was locally accepted. The subsequent local commit step, however, was stopped before reaching the Machine Bridge daemon when expressed through the generic process tool. Repeated diagnostics continued to reach the daemon with owner/full effective authority, and `OperationAuthorizer` classifies owner shell execution as allowed. The public catalog still described `run_process` and `exec_command` accurately as non-read-only, destructive, non-idempotent, open-world execution surfaces. During this repair an ordinary `npm version` direct-process call and several source edits containing Git-mutation terminology were also stopped before daemon execution, while adjacent structured edits and fixed tests were accepted. These observations falsify the hypothesis that Machine Bridge's owner role or internal operation authorizer had newly denied `git commit`; the unstable decision is upstream of daemon authorization and can vary with the exposed tool/request shape.

**Causal design defect.** A routine, repository-local, additive history operation had no structured surface of its own. Read-only Git already had `git_status`, `git_diff`, `git_log`, and `git_show`, but commit required the same arbitrary-process capability that can launch unrelated executables, shells, helpers, and external side effects. Changing `run_process.destructiveHint` to false would misrepresent that much larger capability and weaken host/user risk signaling. Beta.69 instead adds `git_commit`, a fixed implementation-owned write operation that consumes only an existing staged index. The trusted Git executable is selected by Machine Bridge; message text crosses stdin rather than argv; repository hooks and signing are disabled; no editor is started; staging, amend, push, tag, and release operations are absent from the schema and implementation. The surface is available at the existing direct-exec policy level rather than the edit-only file-mutation level. Commit creation is plumbing-only (`write-tree` → `commit-tree` → compare-and-swap `update-ref`) so a concurrent ref change cannot be overwritten silently. Generic process tools retain their existing destructive/open-world annotations.

**Adjacent boundary found during independent challenge.** The first `git_commit` draft authorized only the detected worktree root. That is insufficient for linked worktrees, submodules, and gitdir indirection because `.git` can be a pointer to metadata and a common object/ref store outside the selected workspace. The same issue pre-dated beta.69 in the fixed read-only Git tools: asking Git itself to discover the repository could read that external metadata before Machine Bridge had enforced its path boundary. Repository discovery is now filesystem-first. It walks authority-permitted ancestors, rejects a symbolic `.git` marker, reads gitdir and commondir through bounded no-follow single-link regular-file reads, authorizes each canonical metadata directory, and only then permits a Git subprocess. A real linked-worktree fixture confines the resolver to the linked worktree and proves the external metadata pointer is rejected before the injected Git runner is called. The same discovery now canonicalizes the primary object store, recursively resolves bounded `objects/info/alternates`, and authorizes HEAD/refs/logs/packed-refs/reftable/object-info paths before Git runs; a confined alternate-object fixture is rejected before subprocess execution while an owner/unrestricted fixture remains readable. Direct `.git` reads and writes are also classified sensitive, preventing delegated file tools from bypassing the structured Git boundary through control-plane metadata. Owner/full unrestricted-path behavior remains available by design.

**Failure and privacy properties.** `git_commit` does not return the message, Git stdout, author identity, object path, or hook path. A nonzero Git result becomes a bounded `git_commit_failed` classification and leaves callers to inspect ordinary staged/status state; this avoids returning repository configuration or helper diagnostics across a mutation boundary. Empty staged state produces no commit, an oversized UTF-8 message is rejected before process execution, and a failed retry cannot silently use `--allow-empty` because that option is never present. The random nonexistent hooks path is per invocation and is not persisted. The message-size schema bound plus byte-level backend bound prevents multi-byte Unicode from expanding beyond the internal stdin budget.

**Second independent review before freeze.** A fresh defect-first pass challenged the repaired Git boundary rather than assuming the first review was complete. A real loose-object control proved that Git follows a symbolic-link object stored below an otherwise authorized `.git/objects` directory; restricted structured Git therefore now scans the metadata trees it may traverse and rejects symbolic-link or special-file descendants before process launch. A separate real repository proved that a literal directory named like `:(top,glob)**` was interpreted as pathspec magic and could return another file's diff; all structured Git commands now force literal pathspec semantics. `project_overview` was also found to retain a second raw `rev-parse --show-toplevel` path and now reuses `GitService` discovery. Ref validation delegates to `git check-ref-format`, fixing a local regex that rejected Git-valid names such as `refs/heads/-topic`. Repository `diff.orderFile` and partial-clone/promisor configuration are rejected so the non-open-world contract cannot read an unapproved local file or lazily fetch missing objects.

The same pass tightened adjacent contracts instead of broadening trust. Internal fixed-process environment overrides are exact-value allowlisted to the five structured-Git safety toggles, so loader/startup variables cannot be introduced through that seam. Git path and revision schemas now reject oversized strings before path/object parsing. The new Git/privacy regressions participate in critical coverage, selected pure boundary modules opt into strict checked JavaScript, generated tool/policy references are regenerated from the catalog, and every new shipped/test file is normalized to package-safe `0644`. The support path no longer asks users to manually sanitize an exact Worker endpoint from `doctor`: successful health reports only reachability and the state section is a bounded support projection without endpoint/name/workspace/key/resource identifiers. Owner-local `status` remains the richer diagnostic surface.

The first frozen full verification pass surfaced one additional availability defect outside the Git changes. The Worker started recovery timing before awaiting `waitForReadyDaemon()`, so even its immediate-ready branch could cross a busy event-loop scheduling boundary and lose a millisecond from a one-second execution timeout. That overhead is not daemon recovery and must not reduce the daemon's execution budget. `daemon-ready-dispatch.ts` now checks for an already-ready socket first and reports zero recovery delay without sampling the recovery clock; only a real wait is timed and passed to `daemon-recovery-budget.ts`. Deterministic unit coverage proves both branches, and the same real Wrangler integration assertion that exposed the defect passes without loosening its exact execution-timeout expectation.

**Third independent review (2026-08-14).** A real merge repository falsified an implicit assumption in the first plumbing design: `write-tree` → single-parent `commit-tree` → `update-ref` can succeed while `MERGE_HEAD` exists, flattening a merge into ordinary history and leaving the repository in an inconsistent in-progress state. `git_commit` now rejects merge, rebase, cherry-pick, revert, bisect, and sequencer markers before staged-tree plumbing; exhaustive marker tests and the real merge fixture prove HEAD/state remain unchanged. The review also found that restricted Git metadata-tree inspection honored an entry/depth ceiling but not the runtime cancellation signal, allowing a cancelled or timed-out call to continue a large pre-process traversal; the scan now preserves and throws the call's abort reason during root, directory, and entry traversal. The five fixed Git safety environment values were duplicated between the service and its validator, so they now come from one exact-value source. Finally, `SECURITY.md` still described the removed resumable/session-backed MCP transport even though the implementation, architecture, and integration tests are request-scoped and reject `Mcp-Session-Id`, recovery GET, and `Last-Event-ID`; the stale section is replaced with the current non-resumable model and repository-hygiene tests pin that documentation contract.

**Verification status.** Real temporary-repository tests pass staged-only commit creation with a deliberately failing pre-commit hook and `commit.gpgSign=true`, proving both are suppressed; the committed tree excludes an untracked file and exact multi-line message content survives stdin. Catalog, schema validation, the 52-handler matrix, operation authorization, capability routing, lint, and local TypeScript checks also pass. The strongest host-compatibility proof cannot occur until beta.69 is owner-activated: after packaged canary/live verification and acceptance, this same conversation must create the repository commit through the newly exposed `git_commit` MCP tool. If that structured call is still stopped before daemon execution, the remaining incompatibility belongs to the host safety/control plane rather than to Machine Bridge's generic-process annotations and must be reported as such instead of broadening this tool.

## 2026-08-13 beta.68 hosted-provider follow-up

Beta.67 completed owner activation, candidate-bound deployed OAuth canary, observed live verification, local acceptance, commit, and guarded branch push before the exact pushed head reached hosted provider gates. The local candidate evidence remains valid for beta.67 itself, but the pushed head is not publishable: Ubuntu full CI, Windows platform CI, macOS platform CI, and JavaScript/TypeScript CodeQL each produced a blocking result. Because every repair below changes packaged bytes after beta.67 activation/acceptance, the beta.67 acceptance record is removed from the new source generation and beta.68 must repeat the complete candidate/activation/canary/live-observation/acceptance sequence.

Ubuntu exposed a real managed-job destructive-cleanup ABA. `removeManagedJobDirectoryIfCurrent()` compared the complete pre-rename filesystem identity, but after quarantine rename it checked only device and inode. On Linux an immediately removed/recreated directory can reuse the same numeric inode, allowing a replacement pathname generation to look like the original and reach recursive deletion. Comparing ctime after rename is not a correct repair because rename itself changes directory ctime; this was independently reproduced on macOS while the inode remained constant. The repair instead holds an `O_NOFOLLOW|O_DIRECTORY` descriptor across the POSIX rename and moved-path check, keeping the original inode referenced so reuse cannot occur inside that window. The regression now preserves the replacement generation, and the architecture line budget remains unchanged.

Windows exposed a liveness defect in the process-session authority-revocation settlement wait. The shared `waitForSessionChange()` timer was always unreferenced, including when revocation/shutdown correctness depended on that timer to complete a bounded top-level await. In an otherwise-idle Node process, the event loop could therefore terminate with the revocation Promise still unsettled. Ordinary observation waits remain unreferenced, while the security-critical termination path now explicitly keeps its deadline timer referenced. The process-output/session regression completes locally with the production five-second revocation contract; hosted Windows remains the required platform-fidelity proof for the beta.68 head.

Hosted macOS exposed a test-harness ordering defect rather than a failed POSIX group signal. The self-test deliberately times out one command, whose public error correctly reports pending effect settlement while tree cleanup may continue, and then immediately started a separate termination test after a fixed delay. A busy runner could therefore start the second case while the first process still owned tracker/resource state; a fast termination could also reject before the later assertion attached a rejection handler. A direct detached-process probe confirmed that process-group SIGTERM works on the host. The self-test now waits within the production escalation budget for the prior tracker lifecycle to settle, observes a new tracker ownership increment before terminating the next process, and attaches its rejection observer at Promise creation time. It no longer assumes a 50 ms machine-speed boundary or bypasses resource admission.

CodeQL reported `js/insecure-temporary-file` at two descriptor-open sites reached by temporary-directory test data. Both production operations open existing objects read-only with no-follow semantics: bounded project metadata reads and state-root directory pinning. Rather than accept or suppress the findings, beta.68 makes the intended private mode explicit at the open boundary (`0600` for metadata, `0700` for the directory pin) and adds a metadata regression for the mode. The repository's zero-unaccepted-CodeQL-findings policy remains unchanged; only a hosted beta.68 CodeQL run can close those provider findings.

Targeted local validation after the repairs passes managed-job boundary, process-output/session, project-metadata, state-root-retirement, self-test, architecture, lint, structured logging, and privacy gates. This does not substitute for the exact-head hosted Ubuntu/Windows/macOS/CodeQL rerun. The beta.68 namespace was verified unused in npm, local/remote Git tags, and GitHub Releases through the local GitHub CLI before the version bump.

## 2026-08-13 beta.67 relay-disconnect incident and independent post-fix review

Observed facts were separated from inference before patching. The running login daemon was a single stable service process rather than a crash/restart loop, while historical daemon logs contained repeated relay WebSocket `1006`, `network_error`, and connect-timeout episodes. One 2026-08-13 outage lasted about fifteen and a half minutes and required fifty reconnect attempts; after the shared 120-second same-daemon reconnect grace expired, the runtime reported that one already-completed queued result had been discarded. That is direct server-side evidence for a class of incidents where local execution can complete but its terminal delivery cannot be recovered. The exact host/UI behavior after such a terminal-delivery failure remains outside Machine Bridge observability, so the review does not claim to prove which ChatGPT rendering path produced every visually empty conversation.

Large `runtime.event_loop.stall` records were checked against macOS power events instead of being assumed to be CPU starvation. Several multi-minute stalls overlap Idle Sleep/DarkWake/FullWake intervals, confirming local suspend/resume as one fault class. A separate long relay outage had no matching sleep event, falsifying the hypothesis that sleep alone explains the instability. The current system route is carried through a VPN/TUN, which is relevant transport context but not sufficient evidence to name the VPN product or any specific upstream node as the cause. The supported conclusion is therefore two independent external disruption classes: local scheduling suspension during sleep/wake and network-path interruption while the daemon remains scheduled. Machine Bridge can harden recovery semantics around both; it cannot make the external network continuously available.

The relay heartbeat policy preserves grace for a short scheduling stall. After a pause longer than heartbeat timeout plus recovery grace, it reconnects immediately only when the relay is also still silent beyond heartbeat timeout; fresh inbound traffic processed after resume proves the socket live and keeps it on the ordinary recovery path. A deterministic manual-scheduler regression proves both the fresh-inbound race and the stale-plus-silent termination boundary, including the structured `runtime.event_loop.stall` transition from `relay_disconnect_deferred=true` to `false` before reconnect.

The terminal-loss path was also made directly observable. Reconnect-grace expiry now emits `relay.calls.reconnect_expired` at warning level with only three aggregate fields: cancelled call count, discarded completed-result count, and grace duration. No argument, result, account identity, request key, or call identifier is added to this warning. Runtime regression coverage proves the aggregate values. Operations and testing documentation now distinguishes short-stall heartbeat recovery, long-pause-plus-silence stale-socket reconnect, and reconnect-grace expiry instead of requiring operators to parse a free-form warning.

The requested independent review was then performed from a fresh skeptical pass over architecture, module responsibilities, Worker pending/waiter capacity, timeout arithmetic, relay/result ownership, release-runtime provenance, resource admission, state-root retirement, logging, privacy, tests, and documentation. The 30 ordinary + 2 reserved Worker capacity remains shared between already-dispatched calls and pre-dispatch recovery waiters; delegated accounts still receive hidden activity projections; recovery waiting is deducted from the original execution/settlement envelope; release-control canary provenance remains bound to the activated package/runtime Node/service identity; state retirement and bounded-file gates remain fail-closed. Repository privacy scanning, structured-logging checks, architecture/documentation contracts, lint, typecheck, MCP/Worker/security/resource tests, and the 88-task fast verification plan pass on the reviewed source. No tracked credential/key artifact, TODO/FIXME/HACK residue, or new cross-principal pending-activity disclosure was found.

The review itself found two follow-up consistency defects and repaired them rather than weakening gates. First, the new structured relay warning temporarily pushed `relay-call-recovery.mjs` past its existing 170-line responsibility limit; the implementation was compacted back under the existing boundary and the architecture gate passed without increasing the threshold. Second, beta.66 had been successfully activated on the owner machine before this incident repair, even though its earlier failed-activation audit paragraph still used an unqualified present-tense statement that no beta.66 activation record existed. This section explicitly scopes that historical statement to the earlier failure point. Because repository policy requires every blocking post-activation defect to use a new prerelease number, the repaired source is beta.67; beta.66 activation evidence is retained as historical evidence and is not reused as acceptance for the new package bytes.

Residual boundary: the current MCP delivery model is request-scoped and intentionally has no durable terminal replay log. Short same-daemon outages are recoverable, but an outage beyond the bounded reconnect/request lifetime must eventually terminate rather than preserve side-effecting foreground work indefinitely. If the product requirement changes to guarantee recovery of user-visible terminal delivery across arbitrarily long disconnects, that is not another timeout tweak: it requires a durable request/result protocol with explicit idempotency and host receipt semantics, especially for side-effecting calls. Managed jobs remain the existing durable execution mechanism for long work.

## 2026-08-13 beta.66 failed-activation and post-candidate availability follow-up

The first prepared beta.66 candidate was not activated. The repository owner ran the required interactive-terminal activation command and it failed during the initial current-source/candidate promotion check with `npm pack dry-run returned an invalid file mode: 384`. Decimal `384` is mode `0600`; an independent npm dry-run isolated exactly two post-candidate packaged source files with that mode: `src/local/resource-foreground-wait.mjs` and `src/worker/daemon-ready-waiters.ts`. The promotion digest intentionally accepts only `0644` and `0755`, so the failure is fail-closed package hygiene rather than a validator-format bug. `start-release-candidate.mjs` computes that digest before creating the hardened npm session, installing a candidate runtime, deploying a Worker, handing off the service, or writing activation evidence. No beta.66 activation record exists. Both files were normalized to `0644`, and the subsequently extracted `daemon-recovery-budget.ts` was likewise normalized after creation.

That mode failure also exposed a more important generation problem: comparing the frozen tarball with a fresh dry-run package showed that the checkout had gained packaged runtime changes after the candidate was prepared. The delta included process execution/session admission, relay contract changes, Worker reconnect handling, and two new modules. The old tarball therefore cannot be made valid merely by changing file modes; it is stale evidence and must be replaced by a fresh candidate after the final tree passes the complete frozen gates. This incident also exposed a workflow ownership defect: the owner activation command was being used as the first post-preparation freshness check. The repository now requires direct Node argv `node scripts/start-release-candidate.mjs --install-only` immediately before owner handoff. That path reuses the activation helper for the same source/promotion/tarball preflight and disposable install without Worker/service activation or activation evidence, but resolves its preflight npm CLI from the running Node installation with lifecycle/fallback discovery disabled; project/user `script-shell` therefore cannot interpose before the agent freshness proof.

Independent review of those post-candidate changes found three additional availability defects before refreeze. First, the new ten-second new-call daemon reconnect grace ran before the Worker created the tool timeout budget and pending deadline. A nominal 60-second foreground call could therefore consume about ten seconds of recovery plus sixty seconds of execution plus five seconds of settlement, contradicting the beta.31 hosted-delivery contract that admission/transport latency consumes the existing interval rather than extending it. Short calls were worse: `timeout_seconds=1` could first wait ten seconds. The Worker now creates the original budget before waiting, caps reconnect grace by the execution budget, measures elapsed recovery with monotonic `performance.now()`, subtracts it before dispatch while preserving the five-second settlement margin, and refuses dispatch with retryable `unavailable` plus `side_effects_started=false` if recovery consumed the whole execution window.

Second, `resource-foreground-wait.mjs` introduced a proportional two-to-ten-second foreground admission default, but production `LocalRuntime` still converted an omitted `processResourceWaitMs` into the old explicit `2000` value before constructing either process service. The helper therefore passed isolated unit tests while being dead in normal runtime composition. The runtime now preserves an omitted override as `undefined`, rejects malformed explicit overrides, and retains the existing thirty-minute cap for deliberate test/diagnostic values. Production one-shot admission consequently uses 20% of the execution timeout with a two-second floor, ten-second ceiling, and never more than the whole execution timeout; process-session startup defaults to ten seconds. A runtime-composition regression requires both services to receive no explicit override in the ordinary constructor path.

Third, the first daemon-ready waiter implementation had its own 64-waiter limit, outside the Worker's established 30 ordinary + 2 reserved-control pending-call algebra. During a disconnect, ordinary new calls could therefore consume a parallel capacity pool and undermine the reserved recovery slots. Waiter admission now folds the current pending-call snapshot and all already-waiting tool names into the same shared admission function before adding a waiter. A deterministic regression starts with 29 detached ordinary calls, allows exactly one additional ordinary waiter, rejects the next ordinary waiter, still permits `diagnose_runtime` and `list_roots` into the two reserved slots, and rejects the next call at the combined 32-call ceiling. Ready notification, timeout, and cancellation still remove waiter state exactly once. A final observability pass also makes owner `server_info` distinguish already-dispatched calls from pre-dispatch daemon waiters and report their combined ordinary/reserved capacity usage; delegated activity remains hidden. Architecture budgets were kept intact by extracting the post-recovery timeout arithmetic instead of increasing `tool-timeout.ts`, and the three new availability modules are explicit critical-coverage targets rather than relying only on broad Worker/resource fixtures.

## 2026-08-13 beta.66 pre-commit npm-lifecycle follow-up

Beta.65 completed the intended live release-validation sequence before Git publication: the owner activated the exact candidate, activation evidence matched its SHA-1/SRI/promotion digest, the authenticated daemon and Worker both reported `3.0.0-beta.65`, the deployed OAuth canary passed authorization-code exchange/authenticated MCP/refresh rotation/refreshed MCP/cleanup, post-canary runtime and audit health remained clean, repository privacy checks passed, and local acceptance was recorded. No beta.65 commit or push had occurred.

The subsequent staged-diff review challenged a different layer of the same liveness exception. Proving which outer `npm` executable PATH selects is insufficient to prove what `npm run` executes next: npm honors `script-shell` configuration from project/user/environment configuration. An isolated temporary npm project with a project `.npmrc` pointing `script-shell` at a wrapper proved the wrapper runs before an otherwise ordinary package script while `npm run` still exits successfully. The beta.65 checkout itself had no such setting (`npm config get script-shell` returned `null`), so this does not invalidate the observed OAuth result; it invalidates the general zero-resource accounting guarantee claimed by the source. Beta.65 acceptance was therefore removed before commit, and beta.65 remains activated evidence only. It was never committed, pushed, tagged, released, or published.

Beta.66 removes npm lifecycle execution from the release-control exception rather than trying to enumerate every npm configuration source. A further independent pre-candidate review then rejected the first direct-Node variant because it still executed `scripts/release-oauth-canary.mjs` from the mutable checkout while admission compared only that entry file with the packaged copy. The entry could remain byte-identical while one of its local imported modules changed, allowing same-version checkout code to run under zero-resource accounting. No beta.66 candidate had been prepared or activated at that point, so the fix stays within the same beta.66 source generation. The only light form is now exact direct Node argv `node <activated-runtime-package>/scripts/release-oauth-canary.mjs --allow-live-oauth-canary`: the script operand must be absolute and canonicalize to the running package's own canary, while the checkout is only the command cwd and candidate/evidence data root. `resource-release-control-executable.mjs` independently resolves the actually invoked Node and requires its canonical identity to equal the daemon's own `process.execPath`; PATH shadowing therefore fails closed. The same proof rejects environment keys that can inject code, expose sensitive transport debugging, alter TLS trust/output, inflate runtime instrumentation, or materially alter Node/native startup before the entrypoint, including `NODE_OPTIONS`, `NODE_PATH`, `NODE_DEBUG`, `NODE_PRESERVE_SYMLINKS`, V8 coverage/compile-cache inputs, `NODE_TLS_REJECT_UNAUTHORIZED`, extra CA/certificate/key-log/OpenSSL settings, `UV_THREADPOOL_SIZE`, `LD_PRELOAD`/`LD_AUDIT`/library-path injection, and the corresponding DYLD injection paths. The canary runner applies this same policy itself before candidate validation or any live OAuth mutation, imports all local modules from the activated package, and uses `process.cwd()` for the current source/candidate/evidence data. For prereleases it additionally canonicalizes the executing package root and the package root containing activation `runtime_entry` and requires exact equality; workspace/developer copies therefore cannot generate release evidence even if their entry bytes match the candidate. For every release channel it also reuses the hardened daemon-owner verifier to require a current startup-ready service daemon with the candidate version, an `entryScript` canonically equal to the canary package's `bin/machine-mcp.mjs`, and a canonical Node executable equal to the canary's `process.execPath`, and daemon-lock Node runtime metadata equal to `process.versions.node`; this gives stable releases the same code-origin proof without adding a parallel activation-record format. Because canary evidence does not record alternate local-state identity or Node startup flags, the runner itself now requires exact single-flag argv, requires `process.execArgv` to be empty, and refuses `--state-dir`, `--workspace`, or any other extra argument before state access. This makes ordinary invocations with Node preload/loader/debugger/profiler/runtime CLI options fail even though they never received the release-control light profile. Workspace-relative Node, copied canary, shell wrappers, npm lifecycle invocation, alternate package managers, lookalikes, extra argv, and mismatched cwd/runtime entry bytes retain ordinary accounting. This is still cooperative resource accounting rather than a hostile same-UID execution-authentication boundary.

The canary still needs npm for its explicit `--ignore-scripts` pack dry-run used by the promotion-content digest, but it no longer trusts lifecycle-provided `npm_execpath`. `npm-cli.mjs` centralizes npm CLI discovery and, for the release canary, restricts candidates to layouts derived from the running Node installation. The first owner-machine probe exposed a Homebrew layout distinction: the Cellar Node binary did not contain npm under its versioned tree, while npm lived under the same package-manager prefix. The resolver now derives that prefix from a Cellar Node path and validates the resulting regular file/mode; a synthetic regression reproduces that layout. The beta.66 registry version, Git tag, and GitHub Release namespaces were all confirmed absent before the version bump. Fresh frozen verification, candidate creation, owner-terminal activation, direct-Node deployed canary, live observation, and acceptance are required for beta.66.

## 2026-08-13 beta.65 pre-commit executable-identity follow-up

Beta.64 completed the repaired live sequence successfully before Git publication: the owner activated the exact candidate; the activation record matched candidate SHA-1, SRI, and promotion digest; the authenticated daemon and recorded Worker deployment both reported `3.0.0-beta.64`; the canonical deployed OAuth canary started immediately rather than waiting on machine resource admission; and its evidence bound the exact beta.64 hashes while authorization-code exchange, authenticated MCP, refresh rotation, refreshed MCP, and cleanup all succeeded. Post-canary relay readiness, security-audit chain health, `content_logged=false`, and repository privacy checks were clean, and local acceptance was recorded. No commit or push had occurred yet.

The staged-diff review then found a remaining consistency defect in the resource-control liveness exception. Generic arbitrary-process light admission had already stopped trusting PATH-resolved basenames, but the beta.64 release-control path still accepted lexical `npm run release:oauth-canary` plus cwd/runtime package identity without proving which executable the daemon PATH would actually resolve for the outer `npm`. That is not an OAuth authorization bypass and resource admission remains cooperative rather than a hostile same-user security boundary, but it can still under-account a wrapper/lookalike and contradicts the executable-identity rule adopted for the adjacent light-command family.
