Because this was found before commit, beta.64 acceptance was deleted and beta.64 remains activated evidence only; it was never committed, pushed, tagged, released, or published.

Beta.65 adds `resource-release-control-executable.mjs` at the async process-admission boundary. For a bare npm command it walks the actual execution search order and stops at the first executable match; an untrusted first match is rejected rather than skipped in favor of a later safe-looking npm. The selected target is canonicalized, constrained to npm/npm-cli target names, rejected when it resolves inside the command cwd or HOME, and on POSIX rejected when group/world writable. Explicit absolute npm paths go through the same canonical target and ownership-surface checks. Only after this proof, the exact npm `run release:oauth-canary` lexical shape, and the existing runtime-bound package/version/entrypoint proof all pass does the command receive `release-control/light`; every failure falls back to ordinary accounting. Regression fixtures cover a trusted PATH npm, a HOME-shadowing first match, writable and missing executables, a cwd-local executable, explicit absolute invocation, and a non-npm target. The current owner machine's real npm resolves through this proof successfully.

The `3.0.0-beta.65` npm version, Git tag, and GitHub Release were confirmed unused before the bump. Since executable-resolution logic is packaged runtime code and beta.64 acceptance is no longer valid for these bytes, beta.65 must repeat frozen verification, exact candidate preparation, owner-terminal activation, deployed canary, observed live verification, and acceptance before Git publication.

## 2026-08-12 beta.64 live release-admission follow-up

The owner activated the exact beta.63 candidate and repository activation evidence matched its SHA-1, SRI, and promotion digest. The live daemon and Worker both reported `3.0.0-beta.63`, relay readiness was verified, and doctor/Worker health were clean. The next required deployed OAuth canary, however, revealed a separate resource-scheduling defect before any OAuth request was sent. Its managed runner stayed at step zero for roughly twenty minutes while unrelated long-running workflows held the machine-user I/O reservation. OS process inspection showed the canary runner had no spawned step, and cancellation settled with `steps=[]`; `.release-candidate/oauth-canary.json` remained absent. This establishes that no release-canary account/client mutation began, so there is no ambiguous remote cleanup state to carry into the repaired candidate.

The cause is the command-profile lexical heuristic rather than host pressure or OAuth behavior. `packageManagerTokensHeavy()` intentionally treats package scripts containing `release` as orchestration because release candidates commonly fan out into build/test/package work. The documented `npm run release:oauth-canary -- --allow-live-oauth-canary` therefore inherited the generic `js-build` reservation of 2 CPU, 0.5 I/O, and 2048 MiB even though its implementation performs bounded candidate/activation evidence reads, an `--ignore-scripts` npm pack dry-run for promotion identity, and network control-plane requests. On this host two unrelated low-runtime-pressure workflows legitimately held enough conservative I/O reservation to keep that false build request queued indefinitely. `resource-release-control-classification.mjs` now owns one narrow exception for direct npm argv targeting the exact `release:oauth-canary` script; shell-wrapped forms, alternate package managers, `release:candidate`, and lookalikes remain heavy. The release architecture contract also pins the canary script body and forbids `prerelease:oauth-canary`/`postrelease:oauth-canary` hooks, preventing npm lifecycle expansion from silently adding unclassified local work. The exception is runtime-bound rather than globally name-bound: `resource-release-control-workspace.mjs` requires the command cwd package name/version to equal the running daemon package, rejects workspaces and lifecycle hooks, and compares the cwd canary entrypoint with the runtime-packaged entrypoint using bounded descriptor-verified reads. Thus an unrelated same-name script remains heavy, and beta.63 cannot grant beta.64's liveness exception before beta.64 is actually activated. This is a cooperative local resource-accounting correction, not a relaxation of authorization, release evidence, OAuth mutation, candidate identity checks, or a hostile-same-user code-authentication boundary.

Adversarially composing that first repair exposed a broader pre-existing shell classifier weakness before any gate was run. The old `isLightCommand()` accepted shell text by matching a known-light prefix and rejecting only a small set of explicitly named heavy commands after control operators. A payload such as `echo ready; chmod ...` could therefore inherit a zero-resource bypass even though later shell work had never been classified. Trying to prove a growing set of shell syntax safe was itself the wrong abstraction: startup profiles, environment-controlled executable resolution, wrappers, and PATH lookalikes can change execution before or beneath the apparent payload. Beta.64 therefore removes the generic arbitrary-shell light class entirely. Shell commands remain adaptive or are promoted by the existing heavy shell/build classifiers, and the release-control exception is direct npm only. This eliminates the shell laundering family rather than extending a denylist of syntax cases.

Reviewing the positive direct-command allowlist found the same executable-identity issue. A bare `ps`, `uptime`, `sleep`, or other apparently cheap basename is still resolved through the daemon environment PATH and therefore does not prove which executable will run; read-oriented heads such as `find`, `awk`, `osascript`, `open`, ordinary Git, filesystem metadata/lookup tools, search, and file-stream processors can additionally launch helpers or perform work proportional to caller-selected input. Generic zero-resource admission is now identity-bound in `resource-light-command.mjs`: only a small set of standard absolute executables qualify, while the same basenames reached through PATH remain adaptive. This mirrors the existing disk-reclaim rule that already refused PATH-resolved deletion. Implementation-owned Git/diagnostic liveness is unchanged because those operations execute through fixed code-constructed argv with isolated settings and bypass arbitrary-process admission as an internal control-plane boundary.

Re-reviewing the beta.63 Linux state-root fix found a narrower post-rename check/use interval that the original provider regression did not exercise. The POSIX descriptor pin already prevents the original directory inode from being recycled, but the full moved-root safety traversal occurs after the first retired-path device/inode check. A concurrent replacement installed during that traversal could therefore survive verification and become the pathname passed to recursive removal. Beta.64 keeps the descriptor open and rechecks the retired pathname against that pinned device/inode again after the complete safety traversal; a deterministic fixture replaces the quarantine from inside the verifier and requires the replacement to remain as non-reclaimable evidence. Portable Node.js still cannot express recursive `openat`/`unlinkat` deletion bound entirely to a directory capability, so a determined same-user process can race the final identity check and pathname `rm`; the architecture now states that residual explicitly instead of claiming full hostile-same-user race elimination.

Because these changes are packaged local runtime source, beta.63 activation cannot authorize beta.64 publication. Beta.63 remains activated but unaccepted. The repaired source is versioned `3.0.0-beta.64` and must pass a new frozen gate, exact candidate preparation, owner-terminal activation, and the deployed canary. The strongest live regression is the canary itself: after beta.64 activation it must start without waiting for unrelated build reservations and still produce candidate-bound authorization-code/MCP/refresh/cleanup evidence before acceptance.

## 2026-08-12 beta.63 exact-head provider follow-up

Beta.62 completed every local and live release stage before hosted verification: the exact candidate was activated from the owner TTY, its candidate/activation identities matched, the deployed OAuth canary completed authorization-code exchange, authenticated MCP, refresh rotation, refreshed MCP, and both cleanup operations, the verified launchd service/Worker reported `3.0.0-beta.62`, privacy-bounded live log inspection found no credential/raw-home patterns, acceptance was recorded, and guarded push produced exact PR head `7e3bcfe8a6b4fe85f867117ada3a965c7bc2e1a7`. That evidence remains valid for the bytes it observed, but it does not authorize publication after provider failures. The exact head passed Dependency Review, Governance, workflow policy, package audit, and CodeQL Actions, while Ubuntu full, Windows platform, and JavaScript/TypeScript CodeQL failed. Per the release contract, beta.62 acceptance is therefore invalidated rather than grandfathered into a repaired source generation.

Ubuntu failed `state-root-retirement:test` at the replacement-generation fault injection. The pre-rename guard compared full lossless filesystem identity, including change-time generation, but the post-rename guard intentionally compared only device/inode because a legitimate rename may change directory ctime. On Linux the injected remove/recreate could immediately recycle the just-freed inode before the replacement was renamed into quarantine, so the replacement satisfied that numeric post-rename pair and incorrectly reached `verifyMovedRoot`. The repair keeps the original POSIX directory open through an `O_NOFOLLOW|O_DIRECTORY` descriptor after matching its full pre-rename generation. The descriptor pins the original inode allocation across the rename window; the moved pathname must then match that descriptor's device/inode before verification or recursive deletion. A changed generation returns false and remains quarantined. This closes the ABA mechanism without treating rename-mutated ctime as stable evidence.

Windows failed later for a separate fixture boundary. `testSessionAuthorityRevocation` intentionally set `terminationSettlementWaitMs=100` so a synthetic child that reports successful termination delivery but never closes can prove revocation remains retryable. That test-level override also governed the real spawned session left for final `clearAndWait`; Windows process-tree termination and libuv close can legitimately exceed 100 ms, so the fixture manufactured `process-session shutdown was incomplete` even though production uses a five-second settlement budget. The short deadline is now installed only around the artificial never-close record and is restored in `finally`; real session revocation/cleanup continues under the production budget. The focused process-output lifecycle regression passes with that separation.

CodeQL independently rejected ten findings rather than sharing either platform cause. Five were obsolete third arguments to the two-argument resource aggregation API plus the corresponding production coordinator call; one modeled the Promise-valued host-snapshot single-flight entry as a missing-await flow; and three were check/use file-race reports in verification/test code. The dead arguments were removed. The host-snapshot map now stores a stable `{ promise }` flight record and awaits only its Promise, preserving single-flight semantics while making lifecycle identity explicit. Verification-generation hashing no longer performs an ordinary path read after type inspection: regular-file bytes/mode come from the existing bounded descriptor read with post-open path-identity verification. Secure-file and Windows launcher tests likewise use identity-coherent read evidence or separate temporary pathname generations instead of a check followed by later reuse. No CodeQL threshold or acceptance entry was added for these new findings.

An independent clean detached worktree of the same beta.62 head ran `npm ci` and `CI=true npm run check:full` on macOS and passed all 117 tasks, which distinguishes the Linux/Windows provider-specific failures from a general exact-head regression. At this point in the sequence the repaired source was `3.0.0-beta.63`; its npm version, remote tag, and GitHub Release had been confirmed unused before the bump, and beta.62 remained the live-verified runtime pending beta.63 owner-terminal activation. The later beta.64 section above records the subsequent beta.63 activation and canary-admission rejection.

## 2026-08-12 beta.62 rejected-candidate and release-gate concurrency follow-up

The first owner-terminal activation of beta.61 was deliberately not converted into acceptance after the deployed OAuth canary exposed a cross-layer success-status mismatch. `/admin/clients` deletion commits client/code/token cleanup and returns JSON `200`, whereas the activated beta.61 local client required `204` for every DELETE. The repaired local client now distinguishes OAuth-client deletion (`200`) from account deletion (`204`), account creation (`201`), and the remaining `200` operations, with reverse-status regressions. Because that repair changes packaged bytes after activation, beta.61 remains an activated but unaccepted/unpublished candidate and the repaired source is versioned beta.62; none of beta.61's activation evidence can substitute for a fresh beta.62 candidate activation and deployed canary.

That cleanup incident also exposed a broader settlement invariant at the local admin-client boundary. A mutation can commit remotely before the client discovers that an otherwise successful response used the wrong 2xx status or an empty, oversized, malformed, or non-object body. Treating those cases as a bare `protocol_error` omitted the fact needed to prevent unsafe automatic retry. Successful-response protocol failures are therefore split by method: reads keep ordinary protocol-error semantics, while mutations are non-retryable and record only `request_delivery=sent` plus `effect_settlement=unknown`. The response body and remote error content remain excluded from these settlement details. Reverse-status DELETE tests and a malformed successful PATCH response pin this behavior independently of the deployed canary.

The same credential-boundary review found that signed account-administration fetches still inherited the platform default redirect-following policy. Those requests can carry device-session proof headers and mutation bodies, so a Worker or intermediary redirect must not cause a second request to a Location target. Account administration now sets `redirect=error` on every request. The release OAuth canary applies the same fail-closed default to dynamic registration, token exchange, and authenticated MCP calls; its authorization request alone uses `redirect=manual` because the expected `303` callback is itself validated as data. Focused fixtures assert both policies. This is defense in depth against unexpected redirect behavior and does not rely on assumptions about which headers a particular Fetch implementation strips across origins.

The beta.62 freeze then surfaced a separate verification-lifecycle race only because two safe verification jobs intentionally overlapped. The complete 117-task gate passed on one job while the concurrent fast gate failed `worker-types-generator:test` with a normal Wrangler dry-run reported as a completed-but-resident CLI. The fixture itself is process-isolated, so the divergent result was traced to the shared completion wrapper rather than shared test files: after observing the completion marker, its short grace timer could run while a naturally exiting child still had `exitCode === null`; `requestTermination()` set the cleanup flag, and a later `close(code=0)` was classified by that flag before the actual zero exit code. The wrapper now gives a real zero exit precedence over the raced cleanup request and accepts the bounded-hang path only when the completed child actually closes from `SIGTERM`. A deterministic synthetic mode installs a no-op SIGTERM handler, emits the completion marker, crosses the short grace, and then exits zero; the old ordering necessarily mislabeled it, while the corrected ordering does not. The existing resident-after-completion fixture still closes by SIGTERM and must retain the cleanup diagnostic. This is release-gate correctness rather than a runtime feature change, but it invalidates the preceding beta.62 full-gate result as final evidence and requires a new frozen run.

That incident was followed by a second, distinct evidence-integrity failure: independent work on the same checkout changed coverage policy and audit files while a nominally frozen full gate was still running. The resulting output could no longer establish anything about one exact source generation, even if every individual task were green. Coverage already had a narrower source-generation check, but it protected only its own fixture interval and input subset. The fast/full/platform entrypoint now computes a cryptographic generation over the complete verification input surface before starting the plan and again after it settles, including source, scripts, tests, browser extension, GitHub workflows/scripts, documentation, native/bin content, release acceptance/candidate evidence, top-level build/release/privacy configuration, and POSIX mode or symlink topology. Any drift invalidates the whole run with an explicit “discard this run” error. If a task also failed while inputs changed, generation drift wins because the mixed-generation task failure is not reliable causal evidence; if inputs are stable, the original failure object is preserved. Unit and architecture tests pin both cases. The Wrangler lifecycle regression file was separately found at accidental `0600` mode and restored to ordinary `0644`; the mode itself participates in the new generation identity.

The next architecture pass caught a simpler structural regression before that rerun: the account-administration client had grown to 250 lines against its 240-line responsibility limit as response-size, JSON-shape, remote-error, mutation-settlement, and endpoint-status hardening accumulated. The budget was not raised and formatting was not compressed to hide the growth. The HTTP response boundary now lives in `account-admin-response.mjs`, which owns strict endpoint success status, the one-MiB streamed response ceiling, fatal UTF-8/JSON-object validation, bounded terminal-safe remote error text, response cancellation, and delivered-but-unsettled mutation protocol errors. `account-admin.mjs` returns to account command construction, device-session signing, request dispatch, and account identifier normalization. Architecture caps the new helper at 120 lines; the stabilized source measures 162 lines for the client and 92 for the response helper, with the new packaged source normalized to `0644`. The existing account-admin regression remains end-to-end through the client and passes after extraction. An independent coverage review then caught that the extraction had moved security-relevant response semantics outside the old `account-admin.mjs` threshold even though the fixture itself still exercised them. The critical coverage table now names `account-admin-response.mjs` explicitly at 100% functions / 75% branches; the first gated run measured 100% (9/9) functions and 85.7% (36/42) branches while the caller remained 100% functions / 75% branches. No threshold was lowered to obtain that result.

## 2026-08-11 beta.61 hosted OAuth persistence incident and independent re-review

A ChatGPT hosted-connector failure exposed both a production persistence defect and a diagnosis-process defect. The user-visible connector message was a generic account-connection failure and was deliberately not accepted as root-cause evidence. An exact beta.38 rollback control still failed, proving that the current beta.61 feature set alone was insufficient to explain the outage. Fresh dynamic registration and account authorization succeeded, and the resulting client became trusted to the owner account, but admin inventory showed zero active access tokens and zero active refresh tokens. Production Worker tail then showed `/oauth/token` returning an unexpected 500.

The investigation initially spent too much time on plausible branches before adding sufficiently discriminating instrumentation. DPoP interoperability, a cross-request Promise mutex, and refresh-state growth were each investigated. A privacy-bounded request-shape check proved that the failing ChatGPT token requests were form encoded and carried no DPoP header, eliminating DPoP from this incident. Replacing the old instance-level `oauthQueue` Promise chain with a `DurableObjectState.blockConcurrencyWhile()` boundary did not restore connectivity. Compaction of expired refresh-retry payload materially reduces retained refresh-state size and remains useful capacity hardening, but it also did not move the failing stage. These changes must therefore never be cited as the causal repair for this outage; any retained change requires its own invariant and regression evidence.

A fixed-enum authorization-code stage discriminator then located the failure after OAuth-store load, refresh-store load, PKCE verification, and token-pair issuance but during persistence: the deployed Worker reported `oauth_token_stage_persist`. The persistence path used the supported Durable Object multi-entry convenience form to update the main OAuth and refresh stores together. Local Wrangler/workerd integration had already exercised authorization-code exchange, refresh rotation, and concurrent refresh retry successfully, so the production failure demonstrated a real parity gap between that local evidence and the deployed edge behavior. The project does **not** claim that Cloudflare multi-key `put()` is unsupported; current API/runtime sources support it, and the exact provider-internal reason this deployed path failed remains unresolved.

The production repair replaced that security-critical convenience write with one explicit Durable Object storage transaction containing two named single-key writes. It preserves atomicity while avoiding the deployed failing path and attaches only bounded stage plus error-class diagnostics (`oauth`, `refresh`, or `commit`) without request values or raw exception messages. After owner activation of the exact rebuilt beta.61 candidate, the ChatGPT connector completed authorization and an authenticated `server_info(full)` call returned through Worker, relay, and the local daemon. A subsequent fixed `diagnose_runtime` call also reached the local runtime. Worker observability reported `oauth_refresh.rotated=1`, proving that at least one refresh-token rotation had also committed through the repaired persistence path; the result is stronger than a UI-only connection indicator.

The independent follow-up found the same risky convenience-write pattern still reachable through the authority-revocation helper when a protected multi-record write carried no revocation record. That branch is now routed through the same explicit transaction/per-key persistence discipline, and its regression rejects any return to an outer bulk-object `put()` while requiring all protected records to commit. The helper also refuses writes that try to replace its reserved `authority-revocations` key. The test storage transaction was upgraded from callback-on-self behavior to copy-on-commit semantics so rollback/fault-injection tests do not accidentally validate partial state that a real transaction would abort. A worst-shape 1024-entry authority queue is now regression-budgeted below 512 KiB of JSON envelope, providing explicit headroom rather than relying on the count cap alone.

A later state-shape pass found that the same authority queue budget was only true for state produced by current code. Its schema-1 validator checked known field values but accepted and spread arbitrary additional queue/record fields back into memory; damaged or unexpected persisted state could therefore carry an unbudgeted payload through the next transaction despite the 1024-record cap. Schema-1 validation now rejects unknown top-level and record fields, and deterministic tests cover both forms. This intentionally prefers fail-closed repair over silent forward-field preservation for a versioned security-control queue.

The audit then followed that mechanism rather than stopping at the first instance. OAuth's deep validators bounded every known value but likewise treated unknown object fields as harmless. Because current store objects are subsequently mutated and persisted again, that behavior could preserve an unbudgeted or sensitive payload that no current schema owns. The fix centralizes exact field sets in a dedicated OAuth structural contract and applies them to the schema-1 main OAuth store, schema-3 refresh store, refresh tokens/replay markers/revoked-family records, and schema-1 consumed-token shards; identifier/value grammar remains in the separate record contract. The accepted field sets include the beta.61 optional `registration_revision` and `access_scope` additions; the beta.55 persisted shape therefore remains representable, while unsupported same-version fields now fail closed with the existing repair-required path. Regression fixtures cover unknown main-store, client, refresh-envelope, replay-marker, shard-envelope, and shard-marker fields.

The same bounded-state review found a smaller version of the issue in the generic Worker nonce store used by daemon preflight, account administration, and DPoP replay protection. Callers supplied maximum cardinalities, but those maxima governed only new insertion after a full persisted map had already been accepted and copied; the module also exposed an unused read helper with no cardinality parameter at all. Persisted nonce maps are now validated against the caller's maximum before normalization, oversized state fails closed rather than being silently repaired through an unbounded read, and the dead helper is removed so replay state has one validation/mutation path.

That replay-state boundary also needed a temporal cap. The persisted validator accepted any positive safe-integer expiry, so damaged state could stay within the entry-count limit while setting every marker arbitrarily far into the future and permanently denying new proofs. The three callers have explicit proof-time windows: daemon preflight and admin authorization accept timestamps within their TTL, and DPoP within its replay window; in each case a valid proof's farthest expiration is at most twice that window ahead of the current clock. The shared nonce store now requires a caller-supplied maximum future window and rejects persisted expirations beyond it before copying or pruning the map.

The local error-contract pass found one misleading branch family in the account-admin client: malformed or oversized successful Worker responses constructed `BridgeError("invalid_response")`, even though `invalid_response` is not a supported bridge error code and the constructor therefore normalized it to `execution_failed`. No caller depended on the nonexistent code. The response-contract failures now explicitly use the existing `protocol_error` classification, and tests assert the code as well as the bounded message so source intent and wire behavior cannot drift silently again.

That pass also distinguished MCP result sanitization from direct local CLI behavior. `AccountAdminClient` is used by the terminal account command directly, so a non-success Worker JSON `message` or `error` could previously become a large control-character-bearing local exception before any MCP public-error projection existed. Remote admin error text is now normalized and capped at 2,000 characters at the client boundary itself, including removal of terminal controls and Unicode bidirectional/invisible controls. A hostile synthetic Worker response verifies the direct exception is bounded and control-free.

The same branch review found that every admin HTTP status other than 401/404/409 was classified as `invalid_request`. The Worker implementation makes its ordinary domain failures explicit 400/404/409 responses and converts uncaught route failures to 500, so a 5xx is not evidence that the operator supplied invalid input. The client now classifies 5xx as `unavailable`. Read-only admin calls may retry; mutation calls do not, and carry delivered-but-unsettled details because an HTTP 5xx proves the request reached the Worker but does not prove whether a storage mutation committed before the failure.

The response-state review then found that all 204 responses were accepted before method-specific validation. That meant an impossible GET/PATCH/POST 204 could be normalized to `{removed:true}`, and other unexpected 2xx statuses were likewise treated as valid JSON successes. The client now enforces the Worker contract explicitly: DELETE expects 204, account creation expects 201, and current read/update/rotate operations expect 200. An unexpected 2xx is a protocol error and its body is cancelled without being trusted.

The redundancy pass also removed `remoteBridgeError()`. It had no production consumer anywhere under `src/`; the only reference outside its definition was a unit assertion that instantiated it directly. With the old stateful remote/session result paths already deleted, preserving a separately tested error-deserialization contract that no executable route could reach increased maintenance surface and created a misleading signal that remote structured errors were reconstructed through that helper. Current transport-specific error paths keep their own explicit settlement and exposure boundaries instead.

The storage-capacity review also showed that refresh replay compaction by itself did not establish a durable single-value bound. With 500 active refresh records, 4096 consumed markers, 1024 revoked-family tombstones, and a legitimate short retry-window burst, the old aggregate `oauth-refresh` value could still approach or exceed the provider value ceiling even after old markers compacted. The final persistence format therefore keeps active refresh tokens and revoked-family tombstones in the main `oauth-refresh` value but moves consumed-token replay markers into 8 deterministic hash shards, each capped at 1024 records. A worst-shape full retry-window shard is regression-budgeted below 1.1 MiB, while the global replay cap remains 4096, cutting refresh-state write fan-out roughly in half compared with the initial 16-shard draft without approaching the provider single-value ceiling. Existing schema-2/schema-3 main values remain readable; load reconstructs one in-memory `consumed` map, validates shard ownership/duplicates, compacts/prunes as before, and atomically migrates legacy main-value markers into the fixed shard set. Every token/admin/authority save path goes through the same persistence codec, so no branch can silently re-aggregate consumed replay state into the main value. Tests cover legacy migration, empty main `consumed`, shard cardinality/headroom, over-cap fail-closed behavior, refresh retry/replay semantics, and the absence of direct `oauth-refresh` write sites outside the codec.

The incident also changed the project evidence rules. Production diagnosis now records **observed fact**, **inference**, **falsified hypothesis**, and **unknown** separately; privacy-safe stage evidence or a minimal control experiment precedes semantic patching when the cause is ambiguous; a disproved patch is removed before finalization unless an independent invariant justifies it; credentials, accounts, proof-of-possession, state, and remote resource identity are not generic diagnostic reset knobs; and hosted-runtime behavior requires a deployed canary when local emulation cannot prove equivalent semantics. Verification evidence must also come from a frozen source tree. During this independent follow-up, one full local gate was mistakenly allowed to overlap a Worker source edit; Wrangler correctly hot-reloaded and a WebSocket integration assertion failed with close code 1006. That run was discarded as invalid evidence rather than retried or reported as a product failure, and the rule now explicitly requires completing all writes before the final full gate.

The independent documentation pass also found a protocol-contract contradiction introduced during the ChatGPT compatibility repair. An earlier beta.61 audit correctly removed the **stateful** `2025-11-25` session/recovery implementation, but its wording generalized that result into “only `2026-07-28` is executable”. The final Worker now intentionally has a much narrower remote-HTTP interoperability layer for `2025-06-18` and `2025-11-25`: only `initialize`, `notifications/initialized`, `ping`, `tools/list`, and `tools/call`; tools are dispatched through the current request-scoped controller; `Mcp-Session-Id`, recovery GET, `Last-Event-ID`, replay state, and the removed prepare/subscribe/session architecture stay absent. stdio remains `2026-07-28` only. Current engineering, upgrading, testing, changelog, and release-contract documentation now separates this stateless host compatibility from native protocol/state support so a future “legacy cleanup” cannot accidentally delete a proven hosted-client boundary.

The follow-up current-doc scan found one last copy of the earlier over-generalization in `CLIENTS.md`: it still said the product “supports MCP `2026-07-28` only” even though the same beta.61 source deliberately serves the bounded remote-HTTP initialization compatibility dates. The client guidance now distinguishes native/current-only stdio and `2026-07-28` request semantics from the narrow stateless `2025-06-18`/`2025-11-25` HTTP method set, without implying any restored session or replay model.

The compatibility path itself was then checked as a potential policy bypass rather than merely a protocol adapter. It already shares Origin, OAuth/DPoP, bounded body/media validation, request-ID, role-visible tool and input-schema gates, and forces compatibility tool responses through the current JSON-only controller path. One ambiguity remained: because old clients are allowed to omit the beta.61 mirrored routing headers, the adapter ignored those headers even when a caller supplied contradictory values. Compatibility now treats `Mcp-Method`, applicable `Mcp-Name`, and schema-declared `Mcp-Param-*` headers as optional-but-binding: absence remains compatible, but presence must match the body before dispatch. Unit regressions cover mismatched method/name and a matching mirrored tool call.

That routing review found a second ambiguity shared by native and compatibility requests: validation checked every mirror the selected method/tool expected, but silently ignored reserved mirror headers that were not applicable. A direct same-origin or non-browser caller could therefore attach `Mcp-Name` to a method with no name mirror, or attach undeclared/other-tool `Mcp-Param-*` headers, creating intermediary-visible semantics that the Worker did not own. The mirrored namespace is now closed per request: inapplicable names and any parameter header outside the selected tool's declared bindings fail with `-32020` before authorization-dependent dispatch. Compatibility still permits the current mirrors to be absent; it no longer permits them to be semantically extraneous.

The same independent pass found two local lifecycle defects that were not exposed by the green fast gate. Retained isolated POSIX resource leases correctly survive leader exit while descendants remain, but the group-liveness check ran before process-generation validation; after the original group disappeared, PID/PGID reuse by unrelated work could therefore make a stale lease look permanently live. Reclamation now gives an explicit `pid_reused` identity mismatch precedence over numeric group liveness while preserving the legitimate descendant case. Separately, `ProcessSessionManager.start()` checked cancellation again after spawn/bind but previously threw without terminating the just-created child or returning its session handle. That post-spawn cancellation branch now removes the unreachable session and force-terminates its tree, leaving close handling to release the already-bound lease exactly once. Deterministic regressions cover both mechanisms.

The same PID-generation mechanism also applied to crash-only resource staging recovery. Internal exclusive-file staging names encode a publisher PID but not its start identity; recovery therefore treated any currently live reuse of that number as proof that an uncommitted `.tmp` still had a live owner and could block every subsequent lease/waiter scan indefinitely. The staging file's own write time is now used as an ownership lower bound with the existing process-instance inspector: a genuine publisher necessarily started before it wrote the staging file, while a process generation that started materially later is reclaimable. A regression backdates a staging artifact beneath the current numeric PID and proves it is reclaimed, while the existing live-publisher case remains protected.

Documentation was also rechecked against executable protocol behavior rather than copied audit prose. `TESTING.md` and `THREAT_MODEL.md` still described `subscriptions/listen` as a zero-capacity rejection even though the current controller and integration test deliberately accept a bounded valid filter, acknowledge an empty supported subset, emit correlated completion, and close without persistent subscription state. Those stale claims are corrected to match the actual finite stateless contract.

Relay control handling had one analogous generation-gating omission. `tool_call` and terminal-result acknowledgement already require the authenticated ready relay generation, while `cancel_call` was accepted before that gate and validated only as a string. Worker handover does not require this exception: after a reconnect probe succeeds, the Worker rebinds pending calls and sends `resume_calls`, queued revocations, then `ready_ack` synchronously on the same ordered WebSocket before later cancellation can target that rebound socket. `cancel_call` is therefore now accepted only in the ready generation and only for the canonical call-ID grammar; pre-ready or malformed frames are protocol violations and cannot mutate local call state.

The revocation-delivery path was separately challenged for a possible "mutation succeeded but local authority stayed live forever" failure. The Worker side does preserve the core durability invariant: a revocation-producing account/OAuth save validates and updates the durable revocation queue in the same storage transaction as the authority mutation, so invalid queue state aborts the mutation rather than committing without a cleanup record; transient post-commit queue-read or relay-send failure also leaves the record durable. A later independent local-runtime pass found the missing half of that proof. `ManagedJobManager.revokeAuthority()` caught per-job inspect/cancel failures, logged them, and returned a partial count, so the daemon could still send `authority_revoke_ack` and let the Worker delete the durable queue record even when a matching managed job had not been cancelled. That was a genuine fail-open acknowledgement path. Revocation application now attempts every job but throws if any matching/possibly matching durable state cannot be safely inspected or cancelled; the control handler withholds acknowledgement and interrupts the current relay generation, causing the still-persisted queue to replay promptly during the next verified reconnect. Partial successful cancellations are idempotent on replay. The follow-up also proved that this internal safety path must not reuse the public `cancel_job` policy gate: a durable job accepted while writes were enabled could otherwise become impossible to revoke after the daemon policy narrowed. Revocation now calls a module-internal cancellation state machine that keeps maintenance, ownership, transition-lock, and persistence checks but intentionally bypasses only public tool availability. Tests prove public cancellation is denied under the narrowed policy while authority revocation still cancels the retained job. Tests also hold a matching job transition lock to force the failure, prove no acknowledgement is emitted, prove the relay generation is interrupted, then release the conflict and prove the retained revocation cancels the job on retry. `MULTI_ACCOUNT.md` now documents this complete request-scoped, durable acknowledgement contract rather than the deleted MCP session model.

A later managed-job deletion review found that terminal evidence validation still did not by itself bind the final recursive directory removal to the object that had been inspected. Retention could prove one job generation complete and then remove the same pathname after another generation replaced it. Whole-directory retirement now revalidates the lossless observed filesystem generation, atomically renames that exact directory to an internal name carrying device/inode identity, revalidates the moved object, and only then recursively deletes it. The review then challenged the crash path rather than stopping at the normal path: a process can die after rename but before deletion, so retired generations are recognizable persistent state. A later maintenance pass may reclaim only a generation whose encoded identity still matches; wrong-type, mismatched, or unreadable retired state is surfaced through active-state inventory as `unreadable` and blocks uninstall. Full uninstall attempts valid retired cleanup only while already holding its state-maintenance and machine-service locks, then repeats the fail-closed inventory check. Deterministic tests cover same-name replacement, valid crash residue, corrupt encoded identity, wrong-type residue, state-inventory projection, and uninstall behavior. The new retirement module is also in the critical coverage contract and currently measures 100% function / 72.7% branch coverage against a 90/70 gate.

After a frozen 115/115 local gate passed, the review deliberately continued and found that the first retirement namespace draft (`job_retired_*`) still matched the public managed-job ID grammar (`job_` plus at least 24 canonical ID characters). That made one internal crash residue eligible for a second ordinary-job/list/lock scan; a valid retired generation could therefore be projected once as cleanup-pending and again as an unreadable job, while an abnormal entry could leak its encoded device/inode through generic job diagnostics. The existing tests used `.some(...)` and therefore missed the duplicate projection even though the full gate was green. The internal namespace is now `retired_job_*`, which is outside the public grammar. Active-state inventory reports only a privacy-bounded `state_kind=retired_managed_job` with cleanup-pending/unreadable status and no internal filename/job ID, uninstall emits only a coarse `retired-managed-job:<status>` blocker, and public job/lock scans cannot reinterpret the directory. Recognized retired entries still count against the hard 50-item retained-state capacity until safely reclaimed, so namespace separation cannot become a capacity bypass. Regressions now require exact retired projection cardinality, no public-job-ID overlap, no filesystem-identity text in the uninstall error, and capacity refusal with 49 staged jobs plus one abnormal retired record. That source edit invalidates the earlier 115/115 run as final evidence and requires another frozen full gate.

The same final verification exposed two stale `local-self` assertions rather than product failures. Forced verified daemon reclamation still set its structured `forced` result and emitted an actionable warning, but the fixture required the old internal phrase `forcing process` after routine PID-oriented logging had been removed; the assertion now binds the stable semantics (`ignored graceful termination` plus forcing) instead of restoring identifier-heavy wording. Likewise, full uninstall correctly refused an active or unreadable managed-job state, but its test still matched the older narrower phrase `managed jobs are active`; that check now requires the current fail-closed uninstall meaning and inspection guidance. Removing the obsolete `job approve` parser table also briefly caused unknown job actions to be intercepted by a generic positional-count error. Unknown job actions now bypass the known-action positional table and reach the single command handler, preserving the explicit `Unknown job action` result without restoring any approval execution path.

The first frozen full gate then failed at `full-access:test` after 105 earlier tasks had passed. The failure was not a sandbox or execution defect: the diagnostic's first direct-process probe inherited the ordinary two-second foreground resource-admission window, while the full gate's own concurrent work temporarily produced Yellow `cpu_pressure_window`; the probe therefore returned the expected retryable resource-admission error after about two seconds. This was the same test-versus-cooperative-load mismatch already corrected in `local-self-test` and `agent-context-test`, but only the full-access managed-job observation window had previously been lengthened. `runFullAccessTest` now gives its internal `LocalRuntime` a five-minute cooperative admission budget while leaving command timeouts, detached-job step timeouts, and ordinary production foreground admission unchanged. A release architecture contract requires that override, and the focused real-machine full-access test passes with it.

The review then followed process ownership rather than treating ChildProcess `error` as equivalent to process death. Three local execution surfaces had the same causal defect in different forms: one-shot execution released tracker/resource ownership directly from an `error` event; detached managed-job steps cleared `activeChild` and released their lease from that event; and fixed internal `runExecutable()` commands settled before `close`. All three now latch the asynchronous error and wait for close/exit settlement before releasing ownership. Process-session startup had a related post-spawn gap: bind failure or cancellation deleted the session/untracked/released the lease after only a best-effort kill request. Failed starts now retain an internal hidden session, tracker entry, and resource lease until real close, and return a non-retryable ambiguous-side-effect record stating whether termination was requested. A direct forced `kill_process` or authority-revocation SIGKILL/tree request that returns false no longer reports success or deletes the process-session handle. Deterministic fault injection proves error-before-close ownership, failed bind/cancel termination retention, exactly-once lease release after eventual close, and revocation retry. Runtime authority revocation also attempts pending calls, process sessions, and managed jobs independently before aggregating any failure, minimizing revoked-authority lifetime without allowing acknowledgement of a partial result.

A separate retry review found that idempotent `start_job` replay could inspect an old `queued` status and relaunch a dead runner before reconciling a terminal `result.json` that had already committed in the result-first/status-second settlement window. That could repeat main effects under the same idempotency key after a status-write failure. Replay now performs full reconciliation before any queued-runner launch decision, and its prior-status presence check uses the bounded no-follow state reader so only real `ENOENT` is absence. Fault injection writes a queued status plus durable terminal result and requires replay to converge to that result without executing the main marker again. The detached runner fatal handler was tightened at the same boundary: it may write a fatal terminal record only after confirming this process owns the runner claim and after re-reading a valid same-job active status; unreadable, terminal, or ownership-mismatched state is retained rather than overwritten and scrubbed.

The destructive-state pass found that whole-state uninstall still validated one state-root pathname generation and then recursively removed whatever generation occupied that name. The documented same-user race attacker could therefore replace the root between validation and deletion. `state-root-retirement.mjs` now binds deletion to the exact lossless inspected generation: rename to a reserved sibling retirement name, recheck the moved device/inode, rerun the complete marker/allowed-content/workspace safety contract, and only then recursively delete. A crash-left valid retired root is recognizable and reclaimable; malformed reserved names, wrong types, encoded-generation mismatch, unreadable state, or a replacement generation are retained and block destructive cleanup. State/profile/job inventory was tightened in parallel so only `ENOENT` means absent and symbolic-link profile/job roots cannot be traversed as destructive control evidence. The dedicated regression covers normal removal, crash recovery, rename-window replacement, verification-failure quarantine retention and later safe recovery, malformed/wrong-type/mismatched residue, and symbolic-link profile refusal. The new critical module measures 100% function / 81.1% branch coverage against a 90/70 gate, and the complete critical-module coverage gate passes.

The follow-up reserved-namespace review found two additional fail-open deletion/capacity cases. First, `retired_job_*` cleanup inventory recognized only well-formed names, so malformed reserved crash residue could be silently skipped; second, the public `job_...` namespace was counted and inventoried only when the directory entry was actually a directory. A wrong-type job file/symlink could therefore escape the 50-item capacity, disappear from destructive inventory, and later be removed with the whole state root. Both namespaces are now reserved by name first and validated by type/grammar second: malformed/wrong-type entries remain `integrity_error` blockers, public wrong-type job state counts toward capacity, owner/local diagnostics expose only coarse unreadable state, and non-owner listing remains isolated. Deterministic target admission also securely inspects an existing idempotent job pathname before pruning, preventing a dangling/invalid deterministic target from evicting terminal history before the request fails. The same principle was applied one directory higher: `profiles/` destructive inventory no longer filters unknown/wrong-type children out of evidence; every child must be a real canonical 24-hex profile directory or uninstall is blocked for inspection.

The generation-race review also revisited the beta.60-compatible resource transaction mutex. Ordinary managed-job and state-root retirement now deliberately never roll a failed quarantine back onto the public pathname, because a check-then-rename restore can overwrite a new same-user generation. The resource lock is a constrained rolling-compatibility exception: beta.60 still requires the canonical `transaction.lock` directory to remain the mutex. The highest-risk incomplete-owner case no longer uses quarantine at all; after the bounded grace it rechecks the exact empty directory generation and missing `owner.json`, then uses `rmdir`, so a late beta.60 owner publication makes the directory non-empty and atomically defeats reclamation without ever moving the live mutex. Token-bearing quarantine restore remains only for release/stale-owner compatibility, now rechecks for a replacement before rename, refuses to overwrite one, and has deterministic replacement-preservation coverage. The remaining final check-to-rename window is documented as a temporary rolling-boundary residual rather than generalized as a safe deletion primitive.

A later critical-coverage run exposed a separate interruption mechanism that ordinary managed-job runs had only hit intermittently. The detached runner correctly gave background resource admission up to thirty minutes, but every coordinator transaction nested inside that wait still inherited a fixed five-second lock deadline. A transient transaction owner lasting slightly more than five seconds therefore bypassed the outer liveness contract and surfaced as `cleanup_error_class=resource_error`; recovery then permanently committed `recovery_failed` before its finally step had even produced a result. The test now reproduces that failure deterministically by holding the private coordinator transaction lock for 5.5 seconds during dead-runner recovery. Coordinator acquisition uses the caller's remaining admission budget capped at 30 seconds, while post-spawn lease bind/release use a 30-second ownership-settlement budget; the ordinary production two-second foreground admission ceiling therefore remains unchanged. The same investigation found a second bootstrap defect: a recovery runner marked its runner claim terminal-authoritative before recovery-lock handoff completed, so handoff failure could scrub a still-retryable plan. Terminal authority now begins only after the token-bound recovery lock has been released by the handed-off runner; a wrong-type lock fixture proves bootstrap failure retains `interrupted` state and plan with no terminal result. The deterministic lock-contention recovery passes both normally and with `NODE_V8_COVERAGE` enabled.

The next frozen full gate reached 113/116 before `install:test` exposed another real interruption boundary: the private hardened-npm bootstrap made one 60-second registry request and failed permanently on `ETIMEDOUT`, even though the requested artifacts are immutable exact URLs whose bytes are independently pinned by SHA-512. The downloader now makes at most three attempts with referenced per-attempt timeout timers and bounded 750/1500 ms backoff, but only for explicit transient network codes, HTTP 429, and HTTP 5xx. Redirects, proxy-configuration errors, certificate/policy failures, byte-limit violations, and other non-transient failures are never retried. This both removes another awaited-work `unref()` lifetime dependency and gives install/package flows bounded resilience without weakening origin/integrity checks. Deterministic tests drive ETIMEDOUT then HTTP 503 then successful bytes, and separately require certificate rejection and HTTP 302 to stop after one request. After a fixture-only timing correction separated the 30-second setup-acquisition budget from the measured 5.5-second resource-lock hold, the same source generation subsequently passed fast 87/87 and full 116/116 in 406.9 seconds, including canonical coverage, real install, stdio, Worker integration, and OAuth browser. A later status-enum review then found that the production `full-access-test` duplicated an incomplete managed-job terminal list and could wait five minutes on an already-terminal `recovered`, `cancelled_before_start`, or `expired_before_start` job. Full-access polling now consumes the shared terminal classifier, and active lifecycle classification is likewise shared by retention, manager reconciliation, and detached-runner fatal settlement. Because that is a source edit after the 116/116 run, that run remains strong evidence but is no longer the final frozen-source acceptance run; the complete gate must be repeated once more.

The continued independent review then challenged the **meaning** of that 60-second retry rather than merely its retry count. On the current network path a 256 KiB range from the exact npm 12.0.1 registry artifact completed at roughly 20 KiB/s, which is healthy forward progress but implies well over sixty seconds for the full multi-megabyte tarball. A fixed per-attempt wall deadline therefore restarted the same immutable transfer from byte zero forever under a sufficiently slow but non-stalled connection. Hardened downloads now separate liveness from duration: a referenced 60-second idle timer is reset only by response/body progress, while an independent five-minute per-attempt ceiling still bounds a peer that dribbles forever. Exact HTTPS, proxy validation, redirect rejection, byte ceilings, fixed SHA-512 verification, and the three-attempt transient classification are unchanged. The real `install:test` subsequently crossed the former sixty-second failure point, completed hardened npm construction, package packing, isolated global installation, and zero-argument startup in about seven minutes, and exited zero. The critical coverage gate initially rejected the new modules at 68.4%/38.2% and 100%/77.8%; instead of lowering the thresholds, fault injection was expanded across exact-HTTPS, default proxy-agent, malformed proxy/NO_PROXY, setup/stream failure, declared/streaming oversize, retry exhaustion, progress/idle/absolute timeout, and successful slow-body paths. The final measured coverage is 94.7% function / 78.1% branch for the downloader and 100% / 92.3% for the timeout helper.

The same fail-closed status review found a destructive launchd ambiguity and a cross-platform provider-state projection gap. `launchdStatusSummary` previously collapsed every nonzero `launchctl print` into `loaded=false`, and provider stop accepted `active=false` even if the service remained registered. A provider/permission failure could therefore look like absence, while a failed `bootout` followed by an independently exited process could authorize later plist deletion. The local platform probe confirms exit 113 is launchctl's explicit missing-service result. Current launchd handling accepts only exit 0 as loaded evidence and 113 as unloaded absence; every other nonzero result is `status_unavailable`, failed-query stdout is ignored, and stop/removal succeeds only after observing the service both inactive and unloaded. The same review found that systemd `is-active` transition/unknown output and launchd query failure were still projected as boolean `active=false`, which let owned-service mutation and `service status` erase the difference between inactivity and unavailable evidence. Provider activity is now tri-state: recognized active/inactive/failed/confirmed-missing states are boolean, while transition, installed-but-unknown, and unrecognized query state are `null`; launchd/systemd direct start/restart refuse unavailable state, and `effective_active` remains `null` unless a workspace daemon is positively alive. Deterministic tests cover ambiguous pre-stop status, explicit absence, normal unload, still-loaded/inactive state, post-bootout status loss, systemd transition/unknown state, and CLI tri-state projection. These source edits likewise require a fresh final frozen-source gate.

One live-runtime observation during this review reinforces the source/deployment distinction. The deployed daemon currently reports exactly 50 retained managed jobs, all listed records in the observed window are terminal, and an attempted new detached coverage job returned a generic failure without creating a new job. That is consistent with the already-identified exact-50 admission defect in the older live beta.61 bytes; the current working tree contains the protected-capacity/terminal-eviction repair, but those source edits have not been activated and are not claimed as live behavior.

A further independent verification pass found a source-tooling liveness defect rather than a TypeScript defect. The fast gate first stopped on two ordinary lint errors in the Wrangler lifecycle regression; after those were corrected, `typecheck` repeatedly reached Wrangler's final `Types written` and rerun-reminder output but never advanced to `tsc`. The same behavior reproduced with direct CLI execution, stdin disconnected from the terminal, and both retained 4.115.0 and current 4.120.0 toolchains under Node 26.7.0, so the evidence does not isolate the behavior to the beta.61 Wrangler upgrade. The cross-platform Worker-type generator now removes its target before launch, has a hard pre-completion timeout, and recognizes successful generation only after Wrangler's final command-completion notice and a newly created declaration file. It then allows a bounded natural-exit grace and may send graceful termination to a completed-but-resident CLI; inability to terminate gracefully is still a verification failure. A synthetic lifecycle regression covers clean exit, post-completion residency, nonzero exit, and pre-completion stall, while the real full typecheck chain now completes normally. The exact upstream Node/Wrangler lifecycle cause remains unknown.

Final release-readiness review then reproduced the same provider-CLI lifecycle defect in the separate release-only `worker:dry-run` check. The real Wrangler 4.120.0 process printed its complete upload/binding summary and the terminal `--dry-run: exiting now.` notice, but remained resident for more than ninety seconds; this was OS process evidence, not the older live daemon's known process-session close-settlement defect. The raw package script therefore could never finish one of the documented pre-candidate release gates on this host. Worker type generation and deployment dry-run now use one `wrangler-command-lifecycle.mjs` state machine with command-specific completion markers, a hard pre-completion deadline, bounded natural-exit grace, SIGTERM cleanup, and a bounded SIGKILL failure path. Generated types additionally require the newly written declaration target before a completion hang can be classified as successful. Synthetic fixtures drive normal exit, completed-but-resident, nonzero failure, and pre-completion stall for both commands; the exact real dry-run that previously remained resident now exits zero after bounded cleanup, and no matching Wrangler dry-run process remains. This repair changes only source verification lifecycle; it does not authorize or perform a Worker deployment.

The subsequent frozen full gate exposed a deeper resource-mutex crash-recovery defect through the managed-job coverage fixture. The first failure arrived as a bare `MBM_RESOURCE_TRANSACTION_BUSY`; the fixture itself also had an unhandled-rejection escape hatch because it awaited only a separate readiness Promise while its hold-lock task could reject first. After that control flow was corrected and the setup failure was labeled, repeated full coverage proved the private coordinator really remained busy for the full 30-second acquisition budget. Privacy-bounded diagnostics showed no readable `owner.json`. The remaining state transition was then mechanically identifiable: `tryAcquireDirectoryLock()` creates the final `transaction.lock/` directory before `replaceFileAtomicallySync()` publishes `owner.json`; a process killed after the atomic writer creates `.owner.json.<pid>.<nonce>.tmp` but before its final rename leaves an ownerless yet non-empty directory. The beta.60 compatibility path deliberately reclaims ownerless directories only with empty-directory `rmdir`, so that crash residue could never be removed and permanently pinned the transaction mutex.

The recovery boundary now recognizes only that exact atomic-owner staging grammar after the existing incomplete-owner grace. It inspects the encoded publisher PID against the staging file's write-time generation bound, preserves a still-current publisher, requires a single-link regular file, rechecks the staging filesystem identity, and only then unlinks a dead publisher's uncommitted staging file. Unknown names, multiple entries, wrong types, and hard links remain integrity failures rather than generic cleanup candidates. The caller still performs canonical-directory `rmdir`, so a beta.60 publisher that legitimately completes `owner.json` in the final race window makes the directory non-empty and atomically defeats reclamation exactly as before; no quarantine/split-brain shortcut was introduced. Deterministic resource-admission tests cover dead staging recovery, live-publisher preservation, and unknown-content refusal. `resource-staging-recovery.mjs` retains 100% function coverage and 61.4% branch coverage against its 100/55 gate, and the complete critical coverage suite passes after the production repair. The managed-job fixture keeps the immediate rejection observer too, so future setup-lock failures surface through its own test boundary instead of terminating Node asynchronously.

The first owner-terminal activation of that candidate then exposed a separate end-to-end release blocker that unit tests had masked. The live daemon came up cleanly as `3.0.0-beta.61`: relay readiness was verified, process/drain/termination ownership was empty, the resource coordinator had no active leases or waiters, and the security-audit chain was healthy. The deployed OAuth canary completed authorization-code exchange, authenticated MCP, refresh rotation, and refreshed MCP against the activated Worker, but exited with `release OAuth canary temporary state cleanup was incomplete`. A read-only live inventory immediately afterwards found zero synthetic `release-canary-*` reviewer accounts and zero `Machine Bridge release OAuth canary` clients, proving the remote cleanup had in fact settled. Source comparison then found the exact contract mismatch: the Worker `/admin/clients` DELETE path deletes the client, codes, access tokens, and refresh tokens and returns JSON `200`, while local `AccountAdminClient.request()` previously accepted only `204` for every DELETE. The local fixture had reproduced that assumption by returning `204` for both account and client deletion, so it could not detect the cross-layer divergence. The local client now enforces endpoint-specific success statuses: OAuth-client deletion requires `200`, account deletion requires `204`, account creation requires `201`, and all other admin operations require `200`; reverse-status negative tests ensure the two DELETE contracts cannot collapse back together. Because this changes packaged bytes after activation, beta.61 is explicitly rejected from acceptance/publication. The repaired source is assigned to `3.0.0-beta.62` and requires a fresh frozen gate, exact candidate, owner activation, deployed OAuth canary, and observed live verification before acceptance.

The same pass found a client-visible error-semantic defect in the current MCP response proxy. If the internal Durable Object SSE reader failed, `pumpUpstream()` called the generic cancellation path first; that path closed the public stream, so the subsequent attempt to error the public controller was necessarily too late. The existing test explicitly tolerated either behavior and therefore allowed a transport failure to become normal EOF. A red regression made the next public read require the synthetic upstream error, reproduced the clean-EOF bug, and then drove the fix: upstream failure still aborts the internal reader and schedules the credential-free cancel control but does not close the public controller before `target.error()`. Ordinary upstream completion, public reader cancellation, request-signal cancellation, random internal stream capability replacement, and credential stripping remain covered separately.

Managed-job launch ownership was challenged separately from normal runner execution. `launchRunner()` necessarily spawns before the child pid is known, while the parent then publishes `runner.pid`. The old publication path could create a readable matching claim and still throw during the final owner-only permission operation; its caller would try a best-effort `SIGKILL`, classify the launch as failed, and scrub launch artifacts, but a failed kill could leave the child able to confirm the claim it had already received and execute. Claim publication is now explicitly two-phase: the parent first writes a token-bound `committed:false` claim, hardens it, and only then atomically replaces it with `committed:true`. The child accepts only the committed form and merely waits on a matching uncommitted claim until its startup deadline. The full managed-job/resources integration test passes with this contract, including a valid pid/token uncommitted fault fixture that must remain unchanged and non-executable.

The same ownership principle exposed a cross-platform authority-revocation gap. On Windows, `terminateProcessTree()` returns after `taskkill.exe` is spawned while its eventual exit and fallback `ChildProcess.kill()` are asynchronous. A synchronous `true` therefore meant only "request accepted", yet process-session authority revocation previously deleted its session immediately and let the relay acknowledge the durable Worker revocation. Revocation is now asynchronous: all execution categories are attempted, the session termination is requested without serially blocking managed-job cancellation, and the durable revocation is acknowledged only after every targeted process session reports actual `close`; an undelivered or un-settled termination retains the handle and returns retryable `unavailable`. Fault injection now covers both outright kill-request failure and the subtler case where the request reports success but the process never closes.

Runtime teardown was aligned with that same standard rather than leaving a second escape hatch. The shared process tracker now has a terminal drain mode: existing children receive forced termination, children tracked after draining begins are immediately terminated, and shutdown waits for the tracker to become empty. Process-session teardown uses `clearAndWait()` and the previous fire-and-forget `clear()` API has been removed. A failed drain/clear moves the lifecycle from `stopping` to a dedicated non-operational `stop_failed` state, which rejects restart but permits another cleanup attempt; it is never reported as `stopped`. Relay supersession/fatal callbacks, failed activation cleanup, stdio shutdown, and CLI signal handling all await the same full runtime teardown before lock release or process exit. The runtime-infrastructure and process-output continuation regressions pass with the close-settled contract, including late-child drain and delivered-but-never-settled termination cases.

A later retry-path review found that `stop_failed` was not fully retryable even though its lifecycle API said it was. `ProcessTracker.requestDrainTermination()` marks a child as already requested before sending the forced termination, and the first drain implementation retained that marker when its settlement deadline expired. A second `runtime.stop()` could therefore retain the same child but skip the only termination request that might let it finish. Failed drains now clear the request marker only for children still active at the end of that attempt, preserving within-attempt de-duplication while allowing the next cleanup attempt to reissue termination. The regression forces a first drain to time out, requires the second drain to send a second request, then proves it can settle without losing ownership.

The same review compared the architecture's documented `exit` fallback against actual adapters and found that only the managed-job runner was using `child-process-settlement.mjs`; one-shot execution and process sessions still released tracker/session/resource ownership only from `close`. That made the documentation broader than the implementation and left both surfaces vulnerable to an indefinitely missing libuv `close` after an already-observed process `exit`. `attachChildProcessSettlement()` now owns one shared adapter policy for all three surfaces: prefer real `close`, retain the observed exit state, and after the existing one-second grace destroy only residual stdio, detach the already-exited child, and settle exactly once. Deterministic one-shot and process-session fixtures emit `exit` but never `close` and require successful result/session settlement plus tracker/resource cleanup; the managed-job suite passes after its duplicate listener/stdio-fallback wiring was removed in favor of the same helper.

One narrower shutdown race remained after that first repair and was found by challenging the ordering rather than the individual termination primitives. `CallRegistry.cancelAll()` aborted each call and immediately removed it from the registry, while the actual `ToolExecutor` handler could still be unwinding toward its lifecycle `finally`. That left a window where `ProcessTracker.drain()` observed no children and returned, after which the still-running cancelled handler could register a new child; the tracker's persistent drain flag would request termination, but runtime teardown was no longer waiting for that late child's close. The call registry now has its own terminal drain controller. Once shutdown starts it rejects new `open()` calls, aborts existing calls without synthetically finishing them, and waits until the ordinary handler lifecycle calls `finish()` for every retained record. Timeout is monotonic and fails retryably while preserving the stuck call. Runtime ordering is now call drain -> process drain -> process-session drain, so no handler remains capable of spawning after the final process-settlement barrier begins. Architecture tests pin that ordering, and critical coverage now explicitly includes both call-drain and process-session termination helpers rather than allowing those extracted safety modules to fall outside threshold accounting.

The first frozen full gate also found a packaging-only hygiene defect at task 112/117: six newly added `src/local/*.mjs` files were present with owner-only `0600` modes, which the npm package manifest test rejects for ordinary source. Two were created during this review and four were already present among the pre-existing uncommitted modules. All six were normalized to `0644` without content changes or executable bits, and the package regression reports the expected modes across its 417-file packed manifest. This is deliberately recorded as a release-contract defect even though it has no runtime semantics; it would otherwise survive lint, typecheck, architecture, privacy, and coverage gates until packaging.

Logging/privacy was rechecked after those fixes rather than inferred from the earlier privacy gate. Worker observability and the throttled outer-edge logger both bound field counts/names, redact credential-shaped keys, and apply the shared portable value redactor to surviving strings; current call sites use coarse route/event/error classes rather than request values. The stateful rate-limiter's binding-failure fail-open behavior was also challenged but is not an accidental security bypass introduced by this pass: the beta.18 audit and regression suite already record it as an explicit availability trade-off. No new raw credential/token/path logging path was established by this review. Best-effort local cleanup diagnostics that intentionally cannot replace the primary failure now carry an explicit catch rationale rather than unexplained empty catches.

This record does not accept the current source or candidate. The live repaired candidate is strong operational evidence, but the independent review continues across architecture, module boundaries, state cardinality, error/log projection, tests, documentation, privacy, and release behavior. Any packaged edit after the successful live candidate invalidates that candidate for final acceptance and requires a new exact candidate plus owner activation and repeated deployed OAuth/MCP verification. Formal acceptance remains withheld until the final source is frozen, all gates pass, and the final deployed candidate demonstrates authorization-code exchange, persisted access/refresh credentials, authenticated MCP operation, refresh rotation, and the applicable continuity/soak evidence.

## 2026-08-10 beta.61 stateful-session removal and conservative disk cleanup follow-up (intermediate protocol state)

At this intermediate point in beta.61, the review made MCP `2026-07-28` the only executable protocol while deleting the **stateful** `2025-11-25` initialize/session adapter, signed MCP sessions, `Last-Event-ID` replay, persisted MCP delivery/call rows, prepare/subscribe descriptors, era-specific controllers, and their DPoP retry binding rather than hiding them behind aliases. The browser-visible CORS contract also stopped advertising `Mcp-Session-Id` and `Last-Event-ID`; current request ownership remained transient and request-scoped, while same-daemon reconnect could rebind an already-dispatched call beneath the still-owned request without creating client replay semantics. The later 2026-08-11 hosted-client review supersedes only the “sole executable wire date” part of this paragraph by adding bounded **stateless remote-HTTP initialization compatibility** for `2025-06-18` and `2025-11-25`. It does not restore any of the deleted session/replay/delivery state described here.

Cancellation evidence was tightened rather than inferred from the local emulator. Wrangler/workerd does not reliably convert a raw local TCP close into a Worker `Request.signal` abort callback, so the integration gate no longer treats that observation as proof. A deterministic response-proxy regression now drives both response-body `cancel()` and request-signal abort, requires a fresh random internal stream capability instead of trusting caller-supplied control headers, binds exactly one credential-free cancel control to the direct request, and verifies that Authorization/DPoP are never copied into the private cancellation path. Real deployed-edge stream-close cancellation remains a candidate-activation observation.

State migration remains deliberately narrower than runtime compatibility. A read-only registry check at this source cutoff found public npm channels `latest=2.0.0` and `beta=3.0.0-beta.38`; the owner machine separately has the explicitly recorded live beta.60 candidate. Source comparison proves that 2.0.0 browser pairing stores a single `{token, port}` shape, beta.38 stores schema-2 dual tokens without the current pairing-auth version, and 2.0.0 OAuth refresh state uses schema 2. Those one-way readers therefore remain necessary to avoid destructive re-pairing or reauthorization for real published users. The beta.60 resource-transaction lock bridge likewise remains only for the exact live handoff. Successful migration writes current state and does not make the producing runtime or protocol executable again.

Disk cleanup followed the same evidence rule. The host recovered from the earlier roughly 67 GiB free-space low to above 100 GiB without broad deletion. The repository's `.project-local`, `.wrangler`, and dependency tree were small or actively useful; the remaining roughly 2.37 GiB shared `AgentBuilds.noindex` Swift cache did not contain this repository's canonical project partition and was therefore left untouched. Under the system temporary root, only `mbm-candidate-runtime-*` and `mbm-candidate-outside-*` directories older than twelve hours were deleted after source inspection proved they are test-only `mkdtemp` fixtures, no candidate-runtime test process was active, and successful current tests already clean them in `finally`. An active coverage process was detected separately, so every `machine-bridge-coverage-*` directory was preserved. The reclaimed candidate-test residue was only tens of KiB; no larger unknown or cross-project cache was deleted merely to increase the cleanup number.

## 2026-08-09 beta.61 scheduler frontier and CognactApp follow-up
