A second resource-scheduler review compared the beta.60 design against current Bazel `ResourceManager`, Ninja jobserver integration, Buck2 daemon/isolation/local-resource behavior, Linux PSI/cgroup documentation, DRF/Tetris-style multi-resource fairness, production backfill scheduling, and the local CognactApp process runtime. The review deliberately did not replace the coordinator with one of those systems. Bazel and Ninja support the existing work-conserving/shared-token direction; Buck2 reinforces that project state/cache identity must be scoped to the real project rather than a common parent; DRF is a multi-tenant fair-share objective rather than a direct fit for prioritized one-shot local roots; and Linux PSI provides a better contention signal than inventing another load-average threshold.

The review found two concrete scheduling defects. First, a safe shell `cd` could still fall back to the daemon workspace when the inner command family was unknown, so sibling repositories under one parent could receive the same contention key. Literal, provable directory changes now refine the project scope even for a generic serialized root; an uncertain directory transition makes subsequent relative transitions uncertain until a new absolute/home path re-anchors the parser. Second, shell-heavy detection looked at arbitrary command text, allowing filenames containing `test`, `build`, or `release` to turn metadata reads into heavy work. Script-name classification is now isolated from shell tokenization and considers only the actually executed shell/Node/Python script operand or package-manager script name. This also closes the opposite blind spot found during the final resource review: direct orchestration roots such as `node scripts/coverage-check.mjs`, `python -u verify-*.py`, and `npm run check` no longer begin with a 0.5-CPU adaptive lease before spawning expensive descendants. Negative tests keep module/eval modes and harmless scripts with heavy-looking later arguments unpromoted.

Fit-aware queue scanning remains the default because it avoids head-of-line underutilization. Aging alone, however, could not prevent a large feasible waiter from being bypassed indefinitely by a stream of smaller arrivals. Beta.61 therefore adds a conservative protected-drain phase: a rank-zero waiter must reach its protection age, be blocked by an existing coordinator lease for project/CPU/I/O/memory reservation, and remain structurally feasible after those leases disappear. Only then does it stop fresh backfill; impossible requests still cannot drain the queue. Reservation blockers are classified before the instantaneous CPU window so the scheduler can distinguish coordinator-owned capacity from unrelated host pressure without changing the admission result.

Platform verification exposed a separate event-loop liveness defect. The default admission sleep used `setTimeout(...).unref()`: when a full-access path had no other live handles, Node exited with code 13 while the top-level `await coordinator.acquire(...)` was still pending. The same helper also accumulated one abort listener per retry because successful timer completion did not remove it. `resource-wait.mjs` now owns a referenced timer and symmetric listener cleanup. A fast regression performs a real bounded wait instead of injecting a fake sleep, and the previously failing full-access machine sandbox succeeds under the repaired implementation.

A subsequent canonical-coverage run exposed a different pressure interaction: `local-self-test` could hit the production 2-second foreground admission window while an unrelated cooperating workload held I/O/CPU reservations, causing `resource_admission` even though the self-test was healthy. The self-test now constructs its four runtimes with a five-minute internal resource-wait override, and it asserts that the same value reaches both `ProcessExecutionService` and `ProcessSessionManager`. This does not weaken or bypass machine-user coordination, and it does not alter the public foreground contract: ordinary runtime calls still default to two seconds, while the long test waits for capacity and starts its command timeout only after admission.

The review also found that the critical V8 coverage gate did not explicitly include the new resource scheduler. Functional tests were present in the fast plan, but `coverage-check.mjs` neither executed the direct resource fixtures nor failed if scheduler modules lost coverage. Beta.61 adds both resource fixtures to the canonical coverage run and module-specific function/block thresholds. Deterministic tests now cover Linux `/proc` reader absence, PSI parsing, request-contract failures, protected-queue diagnostics, referenced wait timeout, in-flight abort, and already-aborted signals. Cross-platform host orchestration uses a function-only threshold because one local platform cannot honestly claim branch coverage for all platform samplers. A later canonical coverage run exposed the same test-vs-production admission mismatch in `agent-context-test`: its registered-command runtime could fail after the production two-second window while an unrelated cooperating workload drove host pressure Red. Only that resource-executing test runtime now opts into a five-minute wait, and the release architecture contract requires the override so the coverage fixture continues to wait for shared capacity rather than bypassing coordination or becoming load-sensitive. Production foreground admission remains unchanged. Architecture release contracts also require the direct resource fixtures and core scheduler threshold keys.

A full workflow-bundle development gate then exposed a distinct nested-admission deadlock that focused tests had not exercised. The gate process held a bounded `script-heavy` lease while pytest, running inside that process tree, attempted to acquire another bounded lease before starting a heavy child. Naive summation made the parent reservation consume the capacity needed by its own nested command; under Yellow pressure the nested waiter could age until its five-minute admission budget expired. Beta.61 keeps every child lease complete and independently crash-recoverable, but computes live reservations from an ephemeral process-ancestry forest: a parent lease is an orchestration envelope and the effective vector is the component-wise maximum of that envelope and the sum of direct lease children. Pending nested requests are charged only for the delta they add. Same-key contention is ignored only along the requester's actual ancestor lease chain, so siblings still serialize. If ancestry sampling fails, accounting reverts to full summation. A follow-up performance review found that retrying waiters would otherwise execute a whole-process-table `ps` or PowerShell/CIM query roughly every half second, so parent snapshots now have a one-second completion-time cache, including failed/timeout samples; stale or unavailable ancestry can only make accounting more conservative. The previously failing full workflow gate subsequently crossed the old 52-53% failure point with no nested waiter and completed 682 tests successfully.

A later live-host diagnosis reproduced the user-visible responsiveness failure more directly. On an eight-core host, load1 reached about 109 while the coordinator reported only roughly two reserved CPU cores. The process table showed a `swift-test` root already reparented to launchd plus about seven concurrent `swift-frontend` descendants, along with a separate Python test process; after those roots ended, load fell to roughly 11 and the resource queue drained. Inspection of the persisted leases showed no lease for the surviving Swift process group. The stale-pruning logic was already process-group aware, but explicit `releaseLease()` deleted a bound lease unconditionally. Beta.61 now interprets caller release as relinquishing the handle, not proof that an isolated POSIX group is gone: a still-live group keeps its persisted reservation until ordinary pruning observes group death. Regression tests release a real detached group while it is running and require the lease to survive, then require pruning after termination.

The same diagnosis identified a separate control-plane stall mechanism. The live admission path synchronously executed `ps`, `memory_pressure`, `vm_stat`, one-second `iostat`, `pmset`, whole-table ancestry sampling, and child start-time lookup on the daemon's Node event loop. Under load, the mechanism intended to measure pressure could itself delay relay heartbeats, browser bridging, and MCP responses. A local A/B timer probe around the full Darwin sample measured about 1.15 seconds elapsed with approximately 1.1 seconds of maximum 10 ms timer lag on the old synchronous path; the asynchronous path took about the same total observation time but reduced maximum timer lag to about 3 ms. Host/process probes are now async on the live path, independent Darwin probes run concurrently, concurrent ancestry requests share one in-flight sample, and child start identity is collected before entering the coordinator lock. Successful I/O evidence is reused for five seconds while cheaper signals refresh, and all freshness timestamps are completion-time timestamps.

The live disk snapshot also fell from roughly 125 GiB free earlier in the review to about 67 GiB. The shared `~/Library/Caches/AgentBuilds.noindex` root measured about 21.6 GiB, including several multi-gigabyte Cargo partitions. The cache is therefore a material contributor but not the whole disk change. Automatic deletion is intentionally not added without a cross-language maintenance lock, active-lease exclusion, and crash-safe cleanup semantics. Instead beta.61 tightens admission non-destructively: free space below `max(80 GiB, 15%)` becomes Yellow, while the existing post-reservation hard floor stays `max(50 GiB, 10%)`; build partitions now derive from the same canonical anonymous project hash as resource identity so aliases cannot create duplicate caches.

Cross-language probing during that repair found a second protocol defect: the Node and Python contention-key formulas were textually identical but canonicalized project paths differently. Node used lexical `path.resolve`, while Python resolved existing filesystem aliases; on macOS this made `/var/...` and `/private/var/...` hash to different project mutexes. The shared v1 key now canonicalizes the existing filesystem path before hashing on both sides; Windows additionally normalizes separator form, case, and extended `\\?\\`/UNC prefixes. Fixed golden vectors bind the exact v1 hash material in both repositories, while real symlink-alias tests exercise filesystem canonicalization. The privacy-preserving lease `project_hash` now derives from the same canonical identity, so diagnostics correlate aliases consistently without making that hash admission authority. A real interoperability probe also wrote a live Node lease and required Python to reject the same canonical project/family as `project_resource_busy`. Persistent lease schema version 1 is unchanged.

Linux sampling now reads `MemAvailable/MemTotal` plus `/proc/pressure/{cpu,memory,io}` avg10 values. PSI is optional; missing files remain missing evidence. A 10% recent stall is used only to tighten to Yellow, while memory `full` avg10 at 60% is treated as Red, matching the order of magnitude used by systemd-oomd's memory-pressure action boundary rather than pretending that the coordinator enforces memory. macOS retains its memory-pressure/pageout/swapout/disk/thermal probes.

CognactApp supplied an important negative design result. Its `RealProcessRunner` owns `posix_spawn`, `wait4`, process-group quiescence, and exact `rusage`, so it can retain settlement-time wall/user/system CPU, peak RSS, I/O blocks, and descendant evidence before releasing a process lease. That is a credible substrate for later profile calibration. Machine Bridge's Node `ChildProcess` boundary has no equivalent per-child `rusage`; beta.61 therefore does not add a wall-time EWMA or polling-derived pseudo-learning model. A future adaptive-cost implementation should first introduce a small trusted spawn/wait supervisor that emits bounded settlement usage, then calibrate profiles with explicit caps/decay and regression evidence.

Beta.60 has no local acceptance record, but it was activated from a local candidate at `2026-08-08T23:23:27.866Z`; the live Worker/daemon therefore remains beta.60 while beta.61 source work proceeds. Its pending candidate directory was subsequently removed before the beta.61 version bump. Beta.61 must regenerate all release evidence and must not inherit acceptance from the activated-but-unaccepted beta.60 runtime.

## 2026-08-08 beta.60 cooperative resource-admission review

A multi-agent local execution review found that existing per-runtime tool-call and process-session limits could not coordinate independent Machine Bridge daemons, detached managed jobs, or workflow-bundle processes. Static low concurrency would protect the host but waste idle capacity, while load-average-only gating reacts after fan-out has already started. Beta.60 therefore introduces a per-user durable, work-conserving coordinator: multi-resource reservations are combined with current host pressure and a five-second startup reservation window; priority aging prevents permanent background starvation; fit-aware waiter selection avoids head-of-line blocking; and same-project build-cache families serialize without imposing machine-wide serial execution.

The review also found two transaction defects while integrating the coordinator. First, Node and Python process helpers could acquire a lease and then fail while preparing an external build root, leaving phantom capacity until stale-lease recovery. Both implementations now release the lease on every post-admission preparation failure and have deterministic fault-injection coverage. Second, the Node build-root helper could ignore an explicit effective `AGENT_BUILD_ROOT` supplied to a managed step because it consulted only daemon process environment. The process helper now propagates only that explicit control override into build-root selection rather than reusing the isolated child `XDG_CACHE_HOME`, which would fragment the intended shared cache.

The persistent protocol deliberately excludes argv, shell text, and raw project paths. Lease/waiter ownership is PID/start-time bound and process-group aware; malformed state fails closed. `diagnose_runtime` exposes only coarse pressure/reservation/queue summaries. Internal fixed diagnostics bypass heavy admission, and interactive calls use short retryable waits so resource pressure cannot consume every ordinary tool-call slot. This remains cooperative availability control, not a security sandbox or cgroup-style CPU/RSS quota; an uncooperative same-user process remains outside the guarantee.

Coverage fault injection then exposed a staging publication crash window. `createExclusiveFileSync()` can be killed after linking the final lease but before unlinking its internal staging alias; `replaceFileAtomicallySync()` can likewise be killed with an uncommitted single-link staging file beside an older final lease. The coordinator's strict directory scanner originally treated either as permanent corruption, so a later recovery runner could fail closed instead of cleaning up. Recovery now recognizes only the exact internal staging filename grammar, verifies target/staging inode and link-count relationships twice, and removes either a committed two-link alias or a dead-publisher single-link uncommitted staging generation. It still rejects mismatched identities, extra links, live uncommitted publishers, symlinks, directories, and arbitrary dotfiles. Python interoperability implements the same recovery states so either controller can recover a Node crash artifact.

The real-world workflow suite then exposed a scheduling-policy defect rather than a correctness failure: Python classified any gate-like shell script as `unbounded`, and admission translated `unbounded` to the entire current CPU budget. The startup-window check therefore required the host to be almost idle before such a script could begin, producing a background waiter even though no lease was active. The profile is now a bounded mixed process-tree reservation shared with the Node implementation. This preserves same-project contention protection without turning an unknown script body into a near-idle-host requirement.

## 2026-08-08 beta.59 live-candidate browser pairing correction

The owner activated the exact beta.58 candidate, but independent live verification deliberately withheld acceptance. Worker and launchd daemon identity/readiness were healthy at beta.58; browser verification was not. `machine-mcp browser status` reported the broker running with no extension connection, and source tracing showed a release-blocking split between the two explicit pairing entrypoints: MCP `pair_browser_extension` used the new ephemeral fragment launcher, while CLI `browser setup` still invoked `openExternal()` on the sanitized long-lived broker `/pair` URL. Because that HTTP page is intentionally token/grant-free, the packaged content script receives no bootstrap and returns before registering pairing material. The documented first-pair CLI flow therefore could not work. beta.58 remains activated only as the current operational baseline; it has no acceptance record and is superseded by beta.59 before any push/publication.

The correction removes grant construction from both callers and makes `browser-pairing-launch.mjs` the single composition boundary: broker port + current extension credential become a 30-second grant only inside the one-shot listener helper. CLI pairing reads only current schema-2/auth-v2 state through the existing bounded/no-follow/single-link parser, opens the helper's random 127.0.0.1 listener, and still exposes only the sanitized broker URL in terminal/JSON output. Failure to spawn the browser closes the launch immediately. A new fast-plan regression exercises the CLI command with real temporary state and a real ephemeral listener, while architecture source guards prevent a return to direct fixed-URL opening.

## 2026-08-08 beta.58 independent follow-up: ownership, runner launch, and browser bootstrap

### Additional beta.58 independent review findings

- **Pairing bootstrap redirect inheritance:** fragment secrecy alone was insufficient when the long-lived broker port itself might be occupied by another local process: an HTTP redirect can inherit the original fragment before the content script runs. Explicit pairing now binds a separate OS-random loopback listener first, opens only that already-owned listener, serves one token-free page, and closes after the first delivery or 30 seconds. The fragment carries the target broker port and grant; the extension strips it at `document_start` before page scripts and authenticates the real broker separately.
- **Authentication-registry churn:** both already-paired HMAC auth and first-pair bootstrap originally allocated bounded pending state after seeing only a syntactically valid challenge/grant id. A local process without the secret could therefore fill the registry as a denial of service. Both flows now require a secret-derived init HMAC before allocation. Normal auth is keyed by client challenge and repeated identical requests return the same server nonce/proof; pairing auth is keyed by grant id and is likewise idempotent for the same challenge.
- **Rollback-compatible browser state:** a provisional schema-3 design would have made beta.55 rollback reject browser state. The released design intentionally preserves `schemaVersion: 2`, adds `pairingAuthVersion: 2`, rotates the historically page-exposed extension key, retains the runtime key for migration identity, and requires boolean `migrationPending`. A beta.55-compatible parser fixture prevents future envelope drift.
- **Authorized admin-body amplification:** device-signed `/admin/*` requests previously used `request.clone().arrayBuffer()` and only then checked 64 KiB. Valid signed chunked bodies could therefore force unbounded materialization. The path now bounded-reads the original stream once, hashes exact bounded bytes, and rebuilds the request from that buffer.
- **Internal MCP response materialization:** stream-descriptor and terminal HTTP fallback parsing no longer uses bare `response.json()`; both reads are bounded at the consumer using the descriptor or resumable-message contract.
- **Managed-job normalization:** explicit invalid approval fields are rejected instead of coerced to defaults. Over-limit or malformed persisted resource registries fail closed rather than silently truncating or becoming empty.
- **Cloudflare Computer assessment:** `cloudflare/computer@8758b51c8891c211dddd1903d2ee2d12a75ac7ff` was reviewed as a potential remote execution layer. Its Durable Object VFS, real rows/statement benchmark, default-closed worker egress, and explicit host capabilities are useful patterns; the package remains preview-only and its container transport still has authentication/frame-limit/process-user open questions. It is therefore not added to the production dependency or owner-machine authority path. A future integration should be default-off, non-authoritative cloud scratch using a separate DO namespace and explicit rows budgets, with worker-shell/worker-JS preferred before container transport.

Beta.57 remained unaccepted and unactivated while the filesystem transaction review continued. Two additional ownership gaps were found before a candidate was frozen.

- **SSH staging cleanup was not fully descriptor-bound.** The beta.57 installer verified the source/target hard link after publication, but the source unlink and outer `finally` cleanup still addressed the random staging pathname. A replacement appearing after verification could therefore be deleted by cleanup, and a source-path replacement between link creation and post-link validation could leave the just-created target behind. Beta.58 opens and verifies the staging file before publication, keeps that descriptor alive across `link`, refreshes the descriptor identity after the legitimate link-count/ctime change, and accepts the target only when it matches that pinned object. If the source path changes while the target still matches the descriptor, the target is rolled back by its exact generation; source/final staging cleanup likewise deletes only the known object and preserves replacements. A deterministic fault-injection replaces the staging source after the hard link is created and requires the replacement to survive while the installed target is removed. The residual final compare-to-filesystem-syscall race remains the documented same-OS-user limitation of Node pathname APIs.
- **Provisional runner claims trusted PID without launch identity.** `publishProvisionalRunnerClaim()` accepted an existing `runner.pid` when its PID matched, even if that claim belonged to a different launch attempt. Beta.58 validates the 128-bit hex launch token before publication and requires both PID and launch token to match before treating an existing claim as idempotent. This closes PID reuse and same-parent multi-launch inheritance without weakening the later process-start-time confirmation.
- **Browser broker discovery and first pairing trusted too much of the loopback transport.** The old runtime/extension WebSocket subprotocols exposed long-lived shared tokens to whichever process owned the candidate port before that process proved broker identity. The first beta.58 repair replaced those bearers with role-separated HMAC server/client proofs, but the initial pairing grant still let the HTTP listener supply the token used to validate its own grant: a fresh extension could therefore be convinced by a malicious local listener that chose a self-consistent token/grant pair. The final beta.58 protocol separates the two problems. Already-paired clients send a random challenge only through an internal-header loopback endpoint, verify the broker's HMAC server proof under the owner-only persisted token, and only then open WebSocket with a one-time client proof; pending proofs expire against a monotonic five-second deadline and are consumed once. First pairing no longer receives a token from HTTP at all. `pair(open=true)` places a 30-second bootstrap only in the URL fragment, so it is absent from the HTTP request; the packaged content script runs at `document_start`, removes the fragment before page scripts, and retains the proof only in the extension isolated world. The extension sends the non-secret grant id, a random challenge, and an init HMAC under the fragment secret to `/pair-auth`; the broker verifies that init proof before allocating any pending exchange state, so fabricated grant IDs cannot exhaust the bounded registry. The real broker then derives the same temporary secret from its owner-only extension token, proves possession, consumes the matching client proof once, and only then returns the extension token. The public `/pair` document is always token-free, manual repair obtains bootstrap material from the isolated content script rather than page DOM, and the pairing envelope keeps schema version 2 for beta.55 rollback readability while `pairingAuthVersion: 2` marks the hardened protocol and rotates the historically exposed extension token. A persistent `migrationPending` marker prevents an old broker on the original port from causing adjacent-port split-brain. The fixed custom request header remains a non-secret browser-origin/challenge-churn guard, not local-process authentication. Same-OS-user malicious code remains outside the repository-only isolation guarantee.

The Cloudflare Computer decision is unchanged after rechecking the pinned current upstream head: it remains useful as a future default-off scratch runtime, but its preview status and unresolved container authentication/process-ownership contracts still exclude it from Machine Bridge's production authority core.

## 2026-08-08 beta.57 independent re-review and Cloudflare Computer assessment

The beta.56 candidate was deliberately treated as untrusted before owner activation. A new source-level review re-walked filesystem mutation/rollback order, state-lock authority, managed-job ownership, service-definition removal, Worker HTTP/OAuth parsing, logs/error projection, dead modules, documentation claims, and external cloud-runtime options. Beta.56 had no activation or acceptance record; its candidate manifest/tarball were removed before beta.57 packaged changes.

### New local findings

- **SSH validation could mutate an invalid existing path.** Existing private/public paths were chmod-normalized before the pair was cryptographically inspected. A mismatched or non-key public path at `0600` could therefore be widened to `0644` and only then rejected. Beta.57 validates the complete pair first. Permission normalization is subsequently bound to the inspected filesystem generation and performed on an opened descriptor, so replacement between validation and chmod is rejected without touching the replacement.
- **SSH compensation was pathname-based.** Partial public-key installation and later resource-state persistence failures could remove a private/public pathname even if another process had replaced that entry after generation. Generated-key inspection now retains non-enumerable internal generation identities. Rollback deletes only the same generated objects, attempts both files, preserves replacements, and retains primary-before-cleanup `AggregateError` causality. Same-directory installation also dropped an unreachable `EXDEV` copy fallback. A successful hard-link install verifies source and target are still the same inode/generation before staging cleanup; if source unlink fails, the target is rolled back by that installation identity instead of being left behind.
- **Exclusive hard-link publication had an internal contention false positive.** The fast parallel plan reproduced a security-audit record failure when one owner-state lock contender observed the successful publisher between `link(staging, final)` and `unlink(staging)`: the complete lock inode legitimately had two links for that tiny interval and the strict reader mistook it for persistent hard-link corruption. Secure-file now gives multiple-link rejection a stable code and a four-attempt/1 ms bounded retry helper. Only consumers of the exact exclusive-publication protocol use it; persistent external hard links still fail after the budget. The same audit found runner-claim reads were still using the generic bounded reader, so they now require no-follow path identity and single-link evidence too.
- **Managed-job runner corruption could become absence.** `runner.pid` ENOENT is still absence, but successfully read malformed JSON now fails closed. Pruning retains the directory and emits only a bounded error class; active-job inventory conservatively reports unreadable ownership as potentially live so uninstall cannot proceed from damaged runner evidence.
- **Service definitions were deleted by pathname after provider shutdown.** Launchd/systemd removal now snapshots the existing no-follow, single-link definition generation before stop/disable and removes only that same object afterward. A replacement or a definition that appears after an initially absent snapshot is retained and returns `definition_changed`.
- **Several workspace mutations read authority before waiting for the startup lock.** SSH resource registration, ordinary start, secret rotation, and persistent activation now use the early state only to address the same lock namespace, then reload the authoritative workspace state after the startup lock is held. A deterministic SSH regression simulates another writer committing a resource while the operation waits and requires the concurrent entry to survive. Daemon-only persisted network environment is also loaded only after this boundary, preventing a wait from pinning obsolete proxy/CA values into `process.env`.
- **The beta.53 state-location split was not real.** `state-locations.mjs` had zero importers. Its Windows root, environment handling, and workspace hashing had already diverged from the live `state.mjs` contract, while actual direct `state.mjs` fan-in is 22, not the previously reported 17. The module is removed and the earlier architecture/changelog claim is corrected. `package-identity.mjs` remains a real independent boundary.

The HTTP/OAuth pass found no new release blocker. Worker production code has no direct `request.json()`, `request.text()`, `formData()`, `arrayBuffer()`, or `blob()` body consumption; JSON/form and unauthorized-body handling use the shared bounded reader. Dynamic OAuth registration caps redirect count/length, canonicalizes only HTTPS or loopback HTTP redirects without credentials/fragments, and the authorization page reflects only whitelisted fields through HTML escaping under a restrictive form-action CSP. Error/log scans found no new public details carrying raw commands, stdout/stderr, credentials, account identity, or local paths.

### Cloudflare Computer

The review also examined `cloudflare/computer@8758b51c8891c211dddd1903d2ee2d12a75ac7ff` (552 tracked files in the pinned shallow checkout). The package describes itself as preview/alpha (`@cloudflare/computer` `0.1.0-alpha.1`) and explicitly warns that the API is unstable and not suitable for production. Its useful design patterns are real: DOFS benchmarks actual Durable Object SQL statements/rows read/rows written; path resolution uses cache plus recursive SQL/index tuning; Worker-shell starts with outbound networking disabled; Worker-JavaScript exposes only host-granted capabilities and applies source/input/result/output/concurrency budgets; runtime handles require explicit disposal rather than relying on remote-object GC.

Those mechanisms are worth borrowing, but the project is not an appropriate production authority dependency yet. The container design documentation still lists connection authentication, RPC frame/in-flight limits, and process-user ownership as unresolved boundaries. The current container callback implementation routes a token-like UUID through the proxy path/query but `CloudflareContainerBackend.handleFetch()` only verifies `/ws` plus WebSocket upgrade and does not itself authenticate that token, relying on Cloudflare container reachability. That is weaker and less explicit than Machine Bridge's root-certified daemon/session, challenge, OAuth, and relay identity boundaries. Its DO-backed VFS would also move ordinary workspace file I/O into the same Durable Object rows-read/write billing domain that beta.54 had to optimize, and upstream performance data shows metadata-heavy operations can be good while large sequential reads and full package installs remain materially slower than native filesystems.

**Decision:** do not add Cloudflare Computer to the beta.57 dependency graph or current owner-machine authority path. The pinned public API is a workspace/runtime/backend library and does not provide a production MCP peer surface, so a future integration would be a Machine Bridge-owned backend adapter rather than MCP-to-MCP chaining. It can be valuable as a separately named, default-off cloud scratch/execution backend, preferably Worker-JavaScript or Worker-shell first. It must use an independent Durable Object namespace and explicit row/read/write budgets, host-owned backend allowlists, no inherited local credentials/state, closed-by-default outbound capabilities, bounded results/calls, and explicit disposal. It must not silently mirror or replace the authoritative local workspace. The container backend remains out of scope until upstream authentication, frame/concurrency limits, and unprivileged-process contracts are explicit and testable.

## 2026-08-08 beta.56 exact-head CodeQL follow-up

The accepted beta.55 branch was not merged after provider checks. Exact-head CodeQL raised one high-severity `js/insecure-temporary-file` alert at the POSIX managed-job directory `openSync` call. The new directory resolver opens an already-existing directory with `O_RDONLY | O_NOFOLLOW | O_DIRECTORY`, pins the descriptor while canonical/path identity is checked, and never uses `O_CREAT`; therefore the runtime operation is not temporary-file creation. The alert was nevertheless actionable as a release gate because CodeQL treats a file open reached from an operating-system temporary-directory test path as an insecure temporary-file sink when no secure mode argument is specified.

Beta.56 keeps the descriptor-pinned algorithm and passes an explicit owner-only `0o700` mode argument. Without `O_CREAT`, Node does not create or chmod the directory from that argument; the security behavior remains the existing no-follow, directory-only, descriptor-held identity check. The explicit mode makes the private ownership contract machine-readable to the static-analysis model. A behavior test intercepts the real `openSync` call and requires the third argument to be `0o700`; the architecture contract also requires that literal beside `O_NOFOLLOW` and `O_DIRECTORY`. The beta.55 candidate and acceptance were removed before this packaged-source change entered a new beta.56 release cycle.

## 2026-08-08 beta.55 filesystem-generation ABA correction after beta.54 activation

The exact beta.54 candidate was owner-activated successfully and independently verified as the live Worker, verified login daemon, committed service owner, and accepted package identity. Cloudflare per-minute analytics then validated the Durable Objects repair under higher request load: the complete beta.50 comparison window from 05:20 through 05:32 UTC produced 24,801 rows read for 546 invocations (45.42 rows/request), while the first three fully ingested beta.54 minutes from 05:34 through 05:36 produced 3,321 rows for 280 invocations (11.86 rows/request). The reduction is therefore real operational evidence, not a low-traffic artifact.

Exact-head Ubuntu CI subsequently failed `secure-file:test` before the full suite could proceed. The failing fixture intentionally read a file snapshot, unlinked the source, recreated the pathname, and required `preserveFileSnapshotSync` to reject removal of the replacement. On Linux the allocator reused the just-freed inode, so the replacement had the same device/inode pair and the production identity predicate accepted it. Because `unlinkRegularFileIfIdentitySync`, shared owned-JSON removal, Worker-secret cleanup, lock reclamation, state recovery, audit identity, and SSH-key inspection all rely on the shared identity primitive, this is a real ABA class: an inode number is unique only while that inode is allocated, not a permanent file generation. Merely changing the test to force a different inode would conceal the defect.

Beta.55 keeps exact BigInt device/inode identity and adds filesystem change-time generation when available. Real Node 26 BigInt stats expose `ctimeNs`; that exact value is carried into the shared identity. If either side of a comparison contains generation evidence, the other side must contain the same generation. Legacy/synthetic stat doubles without a change-time remain supported only when neither side claims generation evidence. The existing BigInt descriptor/path observations therefore become generation-aware without routing identifiers through lossy Number conversion. The secure-file regression now rejects same-device/same-inode identities whose `ctimeNs` differs, which deterministically models the Ubuntu reuse case.

The stronger identity exposed one legitimate self-change in Worker-secret setup: the file identity had been captured before an explicit chmod and reused for cleanup after that chmod. Beta.55 retains the creation identity for failure cleanup, then refreshes it immediately after successful permission enforcement and before the deployment callback. A deterministic test supplies one generation before chmod and a second stable generation afterward; cleanup must accept only the refreshed generation. A later candidate rerun exposed the corresponding directory distinction: managed-job directory ctime legitimately changes while runner children create and remove files, so ctime cannot be its stable object generation. POSIX managed-job resolution now opens the directory with `O_DIRECTORY|O_NOFOLLOW`, holds that descriptor across canonical/path rechecks, and compares exact device/inode to the pinned descriptor. The open descriptor prevents the old inode from being recycled during the check while allowing child-entry ctime churn. Windows retains exact device/inode path rechecks because Node does not provide equivalent directory-descriptor semantics. File snapshots and destructive file cleanup remain generation-aware.

Because `filesystem-identity.mjs` and `worker-secret-file.mjs` are packaged production files, beta.54 acceptance is invalid for publication even though its DO quota behavior is proven live. The running beta.54 service remains useful operational evidence. Beta.55 must repeat frozen-source verification, exact candidate preparation, owner activation, independent live verification, acceptance, guarded push, and exact-head provider checks.

## 2026-08-08 beta.54 Durable Object quota incident and independent read-amplification review

The owner reported a Cloudflare Durable Objects Free-plan warning that the account had consumed 90% of its 5,000,000 daily `rows_read` allowance. This review did not assume the warning was historical or caused by unrelated workloads. After the 00:00 UTC reset, authenticated Cloudflare analytics for the current account showed 1,344,623 rows read and 13,918 rows written by approximately 03:31 UTC. A second read-only query at 04:05 UTC showed 1,417,559 rows read (28.35% of the 5,000,000 daily allowance) and 15,231 rows written, proving that the old live runtime continued accumulating the same cost while the repair was developed. The usage grouped to one active `BridgeRoom` Durable Object rather than multiple namespaces. During the same window the object handled roughly 7,515 successful hibernation events, 3,822 successful HTTP events, 3,407 `responseStreamDisconnected` HTTP events, and 354 alarms. At that average read rate the daily rows-read allowance would be exceeded again, while the write rate was already close enough to the free-tier write budget that a fix could not safely trade reads for a new globally rewritten index.

The live beta.50 `server_info` also proved that this was not an unused legacy compatibility path. The current host was actively completing legacy resumable durable calls and opening the internal hibernatable terminal subscribers. Source tracing then identified a systematic O(events x retained-streams) read pattern. `BridgeRoom.fetch()` and every hibernatable WebSocket message called `expireOverdueCalls()`, which unconditionally delegated to durable `expireDue()`. That operation enumerated every `mcp-stream:*` row. A normal legacy call separately enumerated the same rows for request-key deduplication, stream admission, call activation, alarm deadline discovery, and call-ID lookup. Beta.45 intentionally removed beta.44's globally rewritten `mcp-stream-index` to cure rows-written amplification, but making per-stream rows authoritative left too many unrelated event paths using full prefix enumeration. The current quota incident is the read-side consequence of that earlier write optimization.

Beta.54 keeps the per-stream authority and does not recreate a global index. Legacy stream admission and durable-call activation are instead one transaction over one bounded prefix-list snapshot: request identity, expiry pruning, capacity, call admission, and the initial active record all use that observation. Production no longer exposes a separate request-key full-scan helper. A lost `prepare` response remains safe because a retry runs the same transaction and returns the existing stream as `resume`; a different argument fingerprint returns `conflict` and never sends a second daemon call. Sessionless legacy requests intentionally retain independent generations. The hot production path also drops one stream mutation because the transient pending stream and active durable call are now published together.

New random streams and daemon call IDs share one random 43-character generation suffix while retaining distinct `stream_` and `call_` domains. The suffix is generated from the stream identity, not from the reusable JSON-RPC request ID, so acknowledging one request and intentionally reusing its request ID still creates an unrelated generation. Terminal call lookup derives the stream key and performs one point read. If that row exists but is already terminal, duplicate daemon results stop there. Only when the derived row is absent may a pre-beta.54 independent random call ID use one bounded migration prefix scan; those records age out under the existing pending/reconnect limits.

Deadline recovery is now alarm-led rather than event-scan-led. A future persisted runtime alarm is sufficient evidence that all already-existing durable deadlines are protected, so ordinary HTTP and WebSocket entry performs no durable prefix scan. New durable admission commits the active stream row and advances the persisted alarm to no later than the exact operation deadline inside the same Durable Object storage transaction. The event cannot therefore crash between durable ownership publication and alarm coverage; the later scheduler only coalesces daemon/transient deadlines and does not enumerate durable rows. Detach/rebind preserves or extends existing deadlines and still schedules after the state change; settlement/cancellation/expiry only remove deadlines. If alarm inspection fails, or if no alarm exists or the alarm is already due, ordinary entry performs the old bounded durable sweep as a conservative recovery path. Actual alarm delivery remains failure-propagating and enumerates durable records to expire due ownership and schedule the next deadline. This preserves cross-event deadline safety without making every high-frequency hibernation event pay O(retained streams).

The regression suite now measures reads as well as writes. With 24 retained background stream rows, a new combined legacy durable call must perform exactly one prefix list at admission. New call-ID recovery must use one point get and zero lists; terminal settlement with the already-known call view and a later duplicate terminal lookup must use zero lists. The combined production lifecycle uses three stream-row mutations through terminal acknowledgement. A synthetic pre-beta.54 call ID must show exactly one derived point miss plus one bounded migration list. Runtime-alarm tests prove that a future alarm suppresses durable deadline reads entirely and a missing alarm retains the recovery scan. Combined-admission tests separately require the active stream row and operation-deadline alarm coverage to be committed by the same transaction. Production `startEventDrivenStreamCall(createStream)` tests also prove first dispatch, identical-request resume without a second socket send, and changed-argument conflict.

Operational visibility now includes isolate-local aggregate stream-read counters (`stream_rows_read_estimate`, point gets, prefix lists, and list-returned rows) beside the existing mutation/alarm counters. These metrics never contain storage keys, stream/request IDs, OAuth/account identity, or response content. They are diagnostic estimates rather than Cloudflare billing authority; post-activation validation must compare them with Cloudflare account analytics. The accepted live runtime remains beta.50 until the owner activates a new candidate, so no reduction is claimed yet. Beta.53 never reached candidate preparation, owner activation, or acceptance and is superseded by this release-blocking beta.54 repair. Acceptance of beta.54 requires an exact candidate plus live post-activation evidence that the Cloudflare rows-read slope has materially fallen.

## 2026-08-08 beta.53 independent architecture, state-integrity, privacy, and lifecycle re-review

This review was performed independently after the beta.52 Pi/LoopX comparison and intentionally invalidated that unactivated candidate when new packaged fixes became necessary. The review re-walked architecture boundaries, dependency fan-in, persistent-state reads and deletion authorization, lock/recovery snapshots, service-provider removal, OAuth Durable Object state, browser/resource flows, public error projections, logging, subprocess settlement, test coverage, documentation claims, ignored local evidence, and dead/duplicate adapters. Beta.52 had no owner activation or acceptance record and its candidate files were deleted before beta.53 preparation.

### Findings and changes

- **Ownership evidence was not uniformly descriptor-coherent.** Process/startup locks, managed-job locks, and shared owned-JSON removal could combine a pre-open path stat with bytes from a later open. The secure-file boundary now exposes the exact BigInt descriptor stat used for identity; ownership snapshots derive content, device/inode, size, mtime, and link count from that one descriptor, reject hard links, and perform identity/single-link checks again at destructive removal. A real regression discovered during this hardening—ordinary `Stats.mtimeMs` fractional precision versus BigInt-stat integer milliseconds—was fixed rather than hidden by weakening CAS checks.
- **Corrupt-state backup had a read→rename race.** State JSON can no longer be classified as corrupt from one file and then path-renamed after replacement. `file-snapshot-preservation.mjs` creates a complete backup from the exact read bytes, removes the source only if its identity still matches, removes a misleading backup on mismatch, and preserves primary-before-cleanup error causality. Direct fault injection covers success, mismatch, source failure, cleanup-ENOENT, and AggregateError ordering.
- **Destructive control evidence had inconsistent file hardening.** Worker deletion inventory, state/recovery/root markers, internal managed-job control JSON, service ownership, and the autostart log-schema marker now use the same no-follow/lossless-identity/single-link rules appropriate to Machine Bridge-owned state. Optional project discovery and explicit user files remain deliberately less restrictive where omission or hard links cannot grant authority or justify deletion.
- **Remote/provider prose was influencing destructive continuation.** Worker deletion no longer treats `not found`-like Wrangler stderr as success. Systemd unit removal now uses a pure structured decision over definition presence, parsed active state, and disable exit status; nonzero disable cannot authorize removal of an installed definition, and a missing file does not imply an active in-memory unit is stopped. A dead generic service-result message adapter was removed.
- **Main OAuth state validation lagged the refresh-store boundary.** Schema-1 main OAuth state previously checked only that five top-level maps existed. A dedicated deep validator and shared persisted-ID contract now validate production-shaped records before authorization. Invalid-role quarantine remains separate from structural corruption. Targeted coverage is 100% functions with substantial branch coverage and the module is included in the critical coverage gate.
- **File validation and later use diverged in browser resources.** Resource permissions, size, canonical path, and injected bytes now come from one validated descriptor snapshot. CLI browser pairing likewise no longer maintains a second credential parser: only the pairing store parses/validates tokens and the CLI consumes a port-only projection.
- **Persistence-domain fan-in review removed duplicate package identity parsing.** `package-identity.mjs` became the real shared source for static package root/name/version. This beta.53 note originally also claimed that `state-locations.mjs` had become an active path/hash boundary and reduced `state.mjs` fan-in to 17. Beta.57 later proved that part incorrect: `state-locations.mjs` had zero importers and divergent semantics, so it was removed rather than counted as an architectural improvement.
- **Audit/log maps had prototype-key and determinism gaps.** Structured log maps use null-prototype records, and operation-risk target redaction now both retains prototype-shaped own keys and sorts object keys before hashing so semantically identical objects do not create cardinality from insertion order.
- **Error control flow contained one remaining message coupling.** `search_text` now classifies skippable files by stable `BridgeError.details.reason` rather than English error strings. Worker health also rejects unbounded or non-token version strings before they can enter status/doctor diagnostics.
- **Ignored review evidence had regrown unnecessarily.** Reconstructible external clones, 10k-file benchmark corpora, raw coverage scratch, and obsolete endpoint-bearing validation files were removed. The ignored tree fell from about 135 MiB to about 12 MiB before new final-verification scratch was generated; `.workers.dev` endpoint hits in retained ignored files fell to zero at that cleanup point.

### Explicit non-findings / decisions

- The review did **not** replace real process/service timeout tests with virtual clocks: process-tree identity and OS-provider behavior need real lifecycle coverage.
- It did **not** impose single-link policy on user-selected external plans/resources. Internal ownership state and user files have different trust semantics; over-hardening this boundary was detected during the review and corrected.
- It did **not** add a custom MCP JSON serializer. The measured extra mirror stringify cost was about 2 ms at 1 MiB and about 12 ms at 7 MiB, while the largest process results already use the dedicated `$mcpText` projection; the complexity was not justified.
- It did **not** widen near-full module line caps merely to silence architecture gates. A proposed snapshot helper temporarily pushed `exclusive-file.mjs` over its cap and was instead extracted to a cohesive 20-line domain module.
- Same-OS-user malicious replacement in the final instant between identity observation and a filesystem syscall remains an explicit residual risk; Node path APIs do not provide an atomic compare-and-unlink/rename primitive. The project now minimizes that window and fails closed on persistent ambiguity, but OS/container/VM isolation remains the boundary for a hostile peer with the same user identity.

### Verification intent

Beta.53 is not accepted by this document. The source must pass targeted regression tests, architecture/type/lint/documentation gates, the 79-task fast plan, critical coverage thresholds, the full 108-task plan, privacy/history and supply-chain checks, then `release:candidate`. The repository owner must activate that exact candidate in a real terminal before the coding agent may independently verify the live Worker/daemon and record acceptance.

## 2026-08-08 beta.52 Pi/LoopX comparative source review

Before owner activation of beta.51, the external comparison was repeated against exact upstream heads `earendil-works/pi@e47b8e37a6211ebd0b2942fa87059d64f81eec02` and `huangruiteng/loopx@29b086a1752a5329cb46de220225f2902353af3e`. Shallow local review copies were placed only under ignored `.project-local/` and their HEADs were compared with GitHub metadata. The review enumerated and line-scanned each complete text/source tree (Pi: 1,347 text files / about 314k lines; LoopX: 2,230 text files / about 807k lines), searched repository-wide concurrency/state/retry/cache/lease/output/privacy patterns, and then read the relevant implementations and tests in full. This is a source-level comparative audit, not a claim that every generated fixture or vendored/lockfile line received equal manual attention.

Pi's directly transferable mechanism was bounded concurrency around independent metadata work. Machine Bridge already had stronger write semantics than Pi's per-file mutation queue: multi-path canonical reservations, case-alias handling, flushed staging, expected-hash CAS, no-overwrite target publication, rollback, and lossless filesystem identity. It also already had analogues of Pi's stale-generation/single-flight patterns in relay generations, browser `startPromise`/generation ownership, and per-socket daemon cleanup. Pi's provider-level deferred tools are valuable inside model APIs that support `additional_tools`, but they cannot be reinterpreted as task-dependent MCP tool visibility without changing the protocol/client contract. Pi's abortable provider retries similarly do not replace Machine Bridge's existing live relay state machine; the remaining synchronous retry helper is release-only rather than an interactive hot path.

The comparison did expose two serial read fan-outs. `list_dir` could inspect up to 10,000 entries but awaited each path metadata read before starting the next. A 10,000-file benchmark measured a stable serial median near 159.7 ms versus 78.7 ms with a conservative batch width of 16; earlier cold/noisy runs showed about 2.6-2.9x improvement. `directory-metadata.mjs` now prefetches only one bounded batch with `Promise.allSettled`, consumes results in directory enumeration order, checks cancellation before scheduling and before each yield, and throws an error only when the caller actually consumes the corresponding entry. An error in a later prefetched entry therefore cannot escape after the caller has already truncated, preserving the observable serial boundary.

`search_text` had the larger read amplification: each of as many as 10,000 default / 100,000 maximum files performed secure open/fstat/read/close/UTF-8 work serially. A no-match 10,000-small-file benchmark measured about 649.4 ms serial versus 287.4 ms with ordered batch-16 reads; the initial prototype measured roughly 978.5 ms versus 287.6 ms. `workspace-search.mjs` separates scheduled files from externally visible visited files, settles a bounded batch concurrently, then consumes in original walk order. Old and new algorithms produced byte-identical results for a full no-match scan, a `max_matches=50` early stop, and a `max_files=37` stop. Tests require exact peak concurrency, ordering, limit accounting, cancellation, suppression of a later same-batch failure after an earlier match has already completed the request, and propagation of that same failure when it is actually consumed.

LoopX's strongest transferable idea was not its lease/CAS machinery—Machine Bridge's filesystem and release mutations already fail closed at stronger identity/commit boundaries—but its explicit distinction between hot read models and cold diagnostic facts. Its CLI output-budget tests also verify semantic keys and growth slopes, not only one snapshot length. Machine Bridge now applies that discipline to both compact `server_info` and the new `project_overview detail=summary`: absolute output ceilings, exact summary shape, and high-cardinality fixtures make summary size independent of exact tool membership/per-tool pending maps. A coverage expansion caught and corrected an initial projector bug where a non-record summary input could be mistaken for an empty record; the fixed projector returns full/non-record inputs unchanged and reaches 100% function / over 90% block coverage in focused measurement.

`project_overview` previously repeated exact tool membership across local tools, daemon tools, and remote authorization, retained account identity/routing fingerprints, and returned path/size for every displayed top-level entry. The summary form removes those cold fields while preserving policies and counts. Critically, remote summary is not sent below the authorization boundary: normal and legacy resumable Worker paths send an empty/default request to the daemon, apply the account-role/daemon-policy intersection, and only then project the compact result. Durable pending-call metadata stores only the validated `summary` selector in addition to the pre-existing account transform fields. Full remains the default and historical persisted records remain valid. On the owner workspace a local full projection measured about 7,484 bytes versus 2,732 bytes summary; applying the Worker owner decoration measured about 10,503 bytes full versus 3,537 bytes summary.

Several attractive external patterns were deliberately rejected. LoopX's changed-surface canary planner can return no profile for an unclassified path and would create a second dependency map beside Machine Bridge's already parallel 79-task fast gate, so it is not a release or development correctness boundary here. Pi's database writer leases are unnecessary for local locks that are never reclaimed merely because a live owner exceeded a TTL. LoopX local revision packets do not supersede no-follow descriptor reads, BigInt identity, CAS, atomic/no-overwrite publication, and rollback. Skill/context discovery was measured before considering more concurrency: warm calls were tens of milliseconds, while their large payloads are explicit cold-path instruction/skill/command content with existing omit/fingerprint controls. No second fan-out was added there.

Because the directory and search changes affect packaged runtime bytes, the prepared beta.51 tarball and manifest were deleted before any owner activation. Beta.51 has no activation or acceptance record. Beta.52 is the next eligible candidate.

## 2026-08-07 beta.51 exact-head provider correction

The first guarded push of the owner-activated and locally accepted beta.50 candidate passed package audit, dependency review, governance, workflow policy, macOS platform/install, Ubuntu full, and CodeQL Actions checks, but exact-head provider evidence exposed two independent blockers. JavaScript/TypeScript CodeQL reported `js/useless-assignment-to-local` for `ownsTemporary = false` immediately before the successful return in `replaceFileAtomicallySync()` and `staged = false` immediately before the successful return in `atomicWriteText()`. Both assignments are semantically dead: the surrounding function returns immediately and no `finally` observes either flag. Beta.51 removes only those assignments; failure-path ownership and staging cleanup state remain unchanged.

Windows `check:platform` independently reached `security-properties:test` and failed before the intended managed-job canonical-target mismatch assertion. The test injected `/tmp/jobs` as though it were already canonical and compared the second `lstatSync` target against that literal. `resolveManagedJobRootIfPresent()` correctly resolves the requested root first, so Windows produced a drive-qualified path and the fixture returned its synthetic outside identity too early. The same fixture also asserted POSIX output strings for the BigInt identity success cases. Beta.51 makes only the fixture portable by deriving the root/outside paths with `path.resolve()` and the expected job directory with `path.join()`. The production managed-job directory resolver and its lossless identity checks are unchanged.

Because the CodeQL repair changes packaged production source, beta.50 cannot be published despite successful owner activation and local acceptance. Its tracked acceptance record is removed in accordance with the release contract. The running beta.50 service remains valid operational evidence but not publication authorization. Beta.51 requires a new full local gate, exact candidate, owner activation, Machine Bridge live verification, acceptance, guarded push, and exact-head provider checks.

## 2026-08-07 beta.50 independent adversarial re-review

Beta.49 was not activated. A second independent pass treated the prepared beta.49 candidate as untrusted and reconstructed architecture, file-transaction semantics, stream admission, cleanup causality, filesystem identity, logging fields, ignored-local retention, tests, package boundaries, and release documentation from source. That review found a release-blocking patch-transaction defect: the beta.49 mutation coordinator used one case-insensitive resolved-path identity on macOS/Windows, but patch collision preflight case-folded only Windows and the patch transaction used overwrite-capable rename for staged targets. On a case-insensitive macOS volume, distinct patch strings such as `Foo` and `foo` can denote one directory entry; a single multi-file patch could therefore pass preflight and let a later staged target replace an earlier one. The beta.49 private candidate was deleted before any owner activation.

Beta.50 makes conflict identity a shared primitive and changes staged patch target publication to no-overwrite hard links. `EEXIST` after preflight becomes `conflict/target_appeared`; the transaction never treats a late alias as permission to overwrite. Transaction mechanics now live in `workspace-file-transaction.mjs`, separate from `WorkspaceFileService` authorization/path/content orchestration. Whole-file and patch staging failures aggregate the primary failure with cleanup failures without exposing private paths or content. Once a create-only target has actually committed, a later staging unlink failure is reported as a fixed warning rather than converting a completed side effect into a false failure. Patch rollback preserves the primary commit error followed by every rollback/artifact cleanup cause.

The same cleanup audit found an unused but unsafe branch in the shared synchronous exclusive-file helper. Its former `cleanupTargetOnFailure` option unlinked the target whenever the exclusive hard-link step failed before `linked=true`; on `EEXIST`, that target belongs to the pre-existing winner and must never be removed. No production caller used the option, but beta.50 removes the behavior and adds a regression that supplies the old flag and requires the existing target to survive. Pre-commit descriptor/staging cleanup failures now aggregate with the primary error. Post-commit staging cleanup remains a successful commit with fixed warning metadata and non-enumerable internal cleanup evidence. Worker-secret setup consumes that evidence immediately, performs one identity-checked second deletion attempt, and refuses to invoke Wrangler if a secret-bearing hidden staging file cannot be proven removed.

A portability sweep generalized the beta.47 Windows finding. `secure-file.mjs` previously converted `dev` and `ino` to Number and, if either value was not a safe integer, returned `true` from its identity comparator. That was an explicit fail-open path at a primitive used by Worker fingerprinting, owner state, service configuration, pairing, operation authorization, hardened toolchains, security audit, and other sensitive reads. Beta.50 introduces a shared lossless filesystem-identity boundary. Default descriptor/path identity observations use BigInt `fstat`/`lstat`; safe injected Numbers remain supported, while unsafe Numbers fail closed. Process/startup locks, managed-job locks/directories, exclusive owner-file removal, Worker-secret cleanup, audit caching, and SSH-key snapshots use the same exact device/inode representation. Fault injection uses adjacent inode values `2^53` and `2^53+1`, which collapse under IEEE-754 Number, and requires them to remain distinct.

The Worker review found over-wide coordination rather than a missing lock. Legacy `McpStreamChannel` serialized every stream subscription through one Promise and held that queue across the post-registration storage read, even though subscription capacity is per stream and the count-to-`acceptWebSocket` step contains no await. Beta.50 removes the global queue. A deterministic test blocks stream A's second storage read while stream B must finish subscription, preserving the required storage-recheck race defense without unrelated-stream head-of-line blocking.

Logging and local-retention privacy were reviewed independently of publication scanning. Unexpected Worker HTTP failures now log only a low-cardinality route class, not request path text. Routine Worker deployment logs no longer retain Worker name or endpoint; the explicit operator handoff remains unchanged. The ignored `.project-local` tree contained no detected AWS/GitHub/npm/JWT token shapes, but it had accumulated reconstructible toolchain experiments, old live-endpoint probes, and machine paths. Removing only artifacts with no tracked dependency reduced it from about 406 MiB to 12 MiB and eliminated remaining `workers.dev` endpoint matches. Ignored storage remains machine-private operational state, not an exemption from retention/privacy discipline.

A second fail-open search distinguished malformed metadata from storage failure. Startup/daemon process-lock reads, managed-job recovery/transition locks, owner-state locks, and `runner.pid` formerly had catch paths that could collapse a bounded-read or storage error into malformed/null/fallback owner state. In the destructive cases, sufficiently old oversized metadata could then become reclaimable even though Machine Bridge had never reliably read the owner. Beta.50 separates the phases: ENOENT is absence, a successful bounded read followed by malformed UTF-8/JSON is content corruption with the existing bounded policy, and size/permission/I/O/descriptor/identity failures propagate. Recovery/pruning already retain unreadable managed-job state, so removing the deep fallback makes the existing fail-closed outer behavior effective. Oversized lock/runner fixtures verify the existing files survive.

The same pattern existed in autostart log rotation. `readLogSchema()` previously converted every exception to an empty string; `trimAutostartLogs()` interpreted that as a schema mismatch and truncated both daemon logs before rewriting the marker. An unreadable or oversized schema marker could therefore erase the very evidence needed to diagnose the storage failure. Beta.50 treats only ENOENT as first-run absence. A readable old marker still performs the intentional schema reset; any bounded-read, path-safety, permission, or I/O error stops before opening/truncating the log streams. The regression uses an oversized marker and requires both daemon logs and the marker to remain unchanged.

The source import graph remained acyclic. Near-ceiling modules were reviewed individually rather than split for line-count aesthetics: state-machine-heavy OAuth, daemon-socket, and diagnostic modules remain intact where extraction would scatter invariants. Same-OS-user malicious filesystem replacement remains an explicit residual risk outside the Node process's sandbox guarantees; mutually untrusted users still require OS/container/VM isolation. Beta.50 requires a new exact candidate and owner activation after the final frozen-source gates; no beta.49 activation or acceptance evidence exists.

## 2026-08-07 beta.49 comparative runtime review

A read-only comparative review of two actively maintained agent/control-plane projects was used as an adversarial design check rather than as a source-code dependency. LoopX's strongest transferable idea was the separation of canonical decisions from compact hot-path projections and explicit write-intent/conflict semantics. Pi's strongest directly applicable implementation pattern was per-canonical-file mutation coordination, including the requirement that cancellation not release a queue while an underlying filesystem operation is still in flight. Machine Bridge already had stronger local commit primitives than either pattern required: SHA-256 preconditions, no-follow and hard-link defenses, flushed staging files, atomic link/rename, and rollback-capable multi-file patches. The review therefore did not replace those primitives or adopt another agent runtime.

The concrete beta.49 defect was narrower: `LocalRuntime` serialized every `write_file`, `edit_file`, and `apply_patch` behind one process-wide Promise even when the operations touched disjoint files. The new `file-mutation-coordinator.mjs` accepts already-resolved absolute paths, normalizes case on Windows/macOS for conservative alias safety, registers all unique paths of a transaction before awaiting any predecessor, then releases them only after the callback settles. `WorkspaceFileService` resolves a single write/edit target before reservation and resolves all patch sources/destinations before reserving the complete set; mutable file contents, target existence, hashes, and patch hunks are re-read/evaluated only inside the reservation. Same-file concurrent edits therefore observe fresh state, multi-path patches block overlapping single-file mutations, and unrelated files can proceed independently.

Direct tests cover same-path ordering, independent-path concurrency, overlapping multi-path reservations, duplicate-path deduplication, callback failure cleanup, and real concurrent edits through `LocalRuntime`. Architecture checks prevent the global runtime queue from returning and require registration-before-wait/settlement semantics; critical coverage gates the coordinator independently. The same comparative review found a separate context-amplification issue: authenticated `server_info` always returned its full authority/tool/OAuth/observability graph even when an agent only needed version/readiness/relay health. Beta.49 therefore adds an explicit `summary` projection while preserving `full` as the default. Summary is a post-decision projection, not a separate authorization computation; it intentionally omits raw account ID, OAuth metadata, exact effective/advertised tool arrays, per-tool Worker observability, and local security/trust detail. Tests require materially smaller local and remote fixtures and preserve the detail argument through both ordinary and legacy resumable MCP paths. The final projection review also moved daemon ready-socket status formatting out of `BridgeRoom` into a pure `daemon-status.ts` module; stale-socket reclamation remains in the Durable Object, while connected/disconnected status shape and readiness/liveness constants are tested independently. This reduces the Worker composition root from 843 to 820 lines and tightens its enforced ceiling to 830. A third interruption audit found that `ToolExecutor` checked cancellation again after the handler returned even though its inner observability/audit middleware had already committed a completed outcome. For a filesystem or process operation whose final await had crossed a non-cancellable commit boundary, that could expose `cancelled` to the caller after the side effect actually completed. Beta.49 removes only that retroactive veto: pre-handler cancellation and every manager/service cancellation checkpoint remain, remote cancelled owners still suppress terminal delivery, and the local handler result becomes the single settlement fact once it returns. A targeted regression cancels while a synthetic commit is in flight and requires successful completion, one completed metric, zero cancelled/failed metrics, and no registry leak. No credential, account, network, host-UI, release-activation, or authorization boundary changes. The LoopX lock comparison also exposed a local cleanup-causality gap: `withOwnerStateLock` previously threw a release/ownership error before rethrowing an already-captured callback failure, so a secondary cleanup fault could erase the primary state-operation cause. Beta.49 now aggregates primary and release errors in that order; a fault-injection regression corrupts the owned lock during a failing callback and requires both causes to survive.

The Pi supply-chain comparison confirmed two repository defaults worth evaluating separately. Machine Bridge already rejects non-exact root dependency ranges in its release architecture contract, so adding `save-exact=true` simply makes ordinary future npm save behavior agree with that invariant. Pi also uses `min-release-age=2`; Machine Bridge intentionally does not adopt that setting. npm 12 defines it as a tree-resolution filter that makes versions published inside the age window ineligible and documents that it may block `npm audit fix` from installing a newly published patched version. This project instead relies on explicit exact upgrades, committed lockfiles, hardened npm, zero-vulnerability audits, registry signatures/attestations, lifecycle-script allowlists, private toolchain verification, and isolated consumer-package installation. A blanket age filter would reduce emergency patch agility without adding equivalent evidence about a version that has aged past the threshold.

Beta.48 acceptance remains evidence only for the immutable beta.48 artifact. Beta.49 was later blocked before activation by the beta.50 independent review above and must not be activated or accepted.

## 2026-08-07 performance, stability, architecture, and privacy re-review

A separate review after the beta.47 Windows/process remediation reconstructed the slow-path and interruption mechanisms instead of treating individual timeout symptoms as transport failures. The original fast plan ran 79 gates serially and took 53.4 seconds in the review environment, leaving almost no margin below the remote 60-second foreground execution ceiling. The runner now preserves every gate while directly executing simple single-Node scripts, retaining npm for lifecycle/compound/non-Node scripts, and using bounded concurrency with explicit process-heavy serial barriers. The syntax gate was independently responsible for hundreds of process launches; it now parses the dynamically discovered JavaScript surface in one non-evaluating VM-module parser subprocess plus the existing shell-wrapper check. The final local fast-plan measurement after these changes was 17.8 seconds, about one third of the original wall time; elapsed time is diagnostic evidence rather than a release guarantee on other hardware. The release-level review also found wall-clock-only fault-injection delays: the direct managed-job suite spent 189.2 seconds waiting for a deliberately long descendant timeout even though the assertion concerns the timeout/termination state transition, not the absolute duration. The same real process-tree fixture now uses a 15-second deadline and completed locally in 23.9 seconds while retaining the ignore-SIGTERM descendant and terminal-state checks. The shell process-tree fixture remains real but uses a ten-second timeout. Coverage no longer launches `runtime-self-test.mjs` separately after `local-self-test.mjs`, because the latter already invokes `runtimeSelfTest`; this removes duplicate execution without removing a covered path.

The same review reduced request/context amplification. `project_overview` now runs independent inventory/Git-root probes concurrently and returns at most 40 top-level entries with total/truncation metadata. Server and bootstrap guidance no longer requires `resolve_task_capabilities` before straightforward direct file, Git, or shell work; the resolver remains the explicit refresh/routing path for project instructions, skills/commands, and application/browser tasks. Documentation now distinguishes modern static `server/discover` from the legacy initialization-time bootstrap path.

Relay architecture was re-reviewed rather than only retested. The connection lifecycle module had reached its 780-line responsibility ceiling; close/error classification, handshake/readiness validation, user-facing relay causes, and reconnect backoff now live in a dedicated boundary module while the original exports remain compatible. The new boundary is explicitly included in the critical-module coverage threshold rather than inheriting coverage only indirectly through the former file. Architecture, relay lifecycle, Markdown, lint, type checking, and the complete fast plan cover the extraction and the new verification runner. Package inspection also caught and corrected the newly created module's accidental `0600` source mode before candidate preparation; publishable source remains `0644`.

Privacy and supply-chain checks were repeated independently. Both production-only and complete `npm audit` runs reported zero vulnerabilities. `privacy:history` scanned the tracked/unignored surface plus 2,864 reachable history blobs and 128 commit messages without a finding. `npm pack --dry-run` ran the package prepack gates and showed no `.project-local` review artifacts, `.env` files, or private-key/container candidates in the publish list; source modules whose names describe secret handling are not secret artifacts. Host-rendered “tool called” UI decorations are outside the MCP server/logging boundary: Machine Bridge keeps routine per-tool logs debug-only and can reduce unnecessary calls, but only the host can change its own invocation indicators. Likewise, a host may cache an older input schema; the connected runtime's 60-second foreground ceiling remains authoritative and an already cached host schema cannot be invalidated server-side.

## 2026-08-07 version 3.0.0-beta.48 live candidate activation correction

The beta.47 exact candidate passed the local release gates and the repository owner then ran the required persistent activation command. Remote preparation advanced the same-name Worker to beta.47, but activation exited nonzero with `unauthorized`; the failure settlement reported that a compatible candidate service had been installed, started, and verified ready for automatic recovery. Independent post-failure inspection confirmed the live Worker health, launchd service owner, verified service daemon, and authenticated relay had all converged on beta.47.
